Propositional Dynamic Logic has Craig Interpolation: a tableau-based proof
Manfred Borzechowski, Malvin Gattinger ORCID:0000-0002-2498-5073, Helle Hvid Hansen ORCID:0000-0001-7061-1219, Revantha Ramanayake ORCID:0000-0002-7940-9065, Francisco Trucco Dalmas ORCID:0000-0003-2493-023X, Yde Venema

Abstract
. We show that Propositional Dynamic Logic (PDL) has the Craig Interpolation Property. This question has been open for many years. Three proof attempts were published, but later criticized in the literature or retracted. Our proof is based on the main ideas from Borzechowski (1988, master thesis). We define a cyclic tableau system for PDL with a loading mechanism to recognize successful repeats. For this system, we show soundness and completeness via a game. To show interpolation, we modify Maehara’s method to work for tableaux with repeats: we first define pre-interpolants at each node, and then use a quasi-tableau to define interpolants for clusters (strongly connected components). In different terms, our method solves the fixpoint equations that characterize the desired interpolants, and the method ensures that the solutions to these equations can be expressed within PDL. The proof is constructive and we show how to compute interpolants. We also make available a Haskell implementation of the proof system that provides interpolants. Lastly, we mention ongoing work to formally verify this proof in the interactive theorem prover Lean, and several questions for future work.

[b]
[c]

[c]

1.
Introduction

Propositional Dynamic Logic

Propositional Dynamic Logic (abbreviated: PDL) is the propositional version of a first-order modal logic that was designed by Pratt
[
42
]
as
a formalism to reason about the behaviour of programs.
Since its introduction by Fischer and Ladner
[
19
]
PDL has been studied as a logic in its own right, and it has been recognized as an interesting and important modal logic for quite some time, see for instance Harel, Kozen and Tiuryn
[
24
]
or Troquard and Balbiani
[
51
]
for surveys.

The basic idea underlying PDL is that its syntax features a
structured family of modalities
, each of which is indexed by some regular expression that represents a
program
— it is in fact customary to refer to these
expressions themselves as “programs”.
Then, where
\alpha
is such a program and
\varphi
is a formula, the intended meaning of the boxed formula
[\alpha]\varphi
is that “after every execution of the program
\alpha
, the formula
\varphi
holds”.

The collection of all programs is generated from a set
\mathcal{P}_{0}
of so-called
atomic programs
.
What defines PDL is that we close the set
\mathcal{P}_{0}
under three
program constructors
which correspond to the standard regular operations
of sequential composition, non-deterministic choice and unbounded iteration
(to be denoted by the symbols
;
,
\cup
and
∗
, respectively).
In
test-free
PDL these are the only program constructors, so that the
programs correspond to the regular expressions over the set
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
The language of
full
PDL (which we will usually refer to as “PDL” without qualification) also admits so-called
test programs
;
that is, for every formula
\tau
, we have an expression
{\tau?}
which amounts to the program “proceed if
\tau
is true, else fail”.

The standard semantics for PDL is given by (multi-)modal Kripke models, which feature an accessibility relation
R_{a}
for each atomic program
a
.
This interpretation is extended to arbitrary programs by a straightforward induction on the complexity of programs
by interpreting
;
,
\cup
and
∗
on relations as composition, union and reflexive, transitive closure, respectively.

The most important properties of PDL were established within a few years after its introduction.
The decidability of its satisfiability problem was already proved by Fischer and Ladner
[
19
, Theorem 3.1]
, who also established an exponential-time lower bound
[
19
, Theorem 4.4]
.
The
ExpTime
upper bound was established by Pratt
[
44
]
.
A natural axiomatisation was proposed by Segerberg
[
46
]
and proved
to be complete by Parikh
[
41
, Theorem 1]
and others — see
Pratt
[
43
]
for an overview of the early history of PDL.
In contrast to these results, the status of the metalogical property of

interpolation
has been much less clear.

Craig interpolation and Beth definability

A logic has the Craig Interpolation Property (CIP) if for any validity
\varphi\to\psi
there exists a formula
\theta
in the common vocabulary of
\varphi
and
\psi
such that
\varphi\to\theta
and
\theta\to\psi
are valid.
Any such formula
\theta
is then called an
interpolant
of
\varphi
and
\psi
.
The property was named after Craig who established it for first-order logic
[
14
, Lemma 1]
.
Informally we will often say that a logic “has interpolation” if it enjoys the Craig Interpolation Property.

Interpolation is considered to be an attractive property of a logic.
Applications in computer science are generally related to modularity: if one needs to reason about two different parts of a system (for instance, in the setting of knowledge representation or program specification), interpolation guarantees that this reasoning can be done in the common vocabulary
[
39
,
15
,
28
]
.

A related property is that of Beth Definability, which informally states that any notion that can be implicitly defined in the logic, in fact also has an explicit definition.
This property owes its name to Beth, who established it for classical first-order logic
[
5
, Theorem 4.2]
; it is studied in the area of description logics, where it is used to optimize reasoning
[
49
]
.

Craig Interpolation and Beth Definability have been studied in logic, see for instance the work of Hoogland and that of van Benthem
[
27
,
52
]
. It is well known that, in the presence of a deduction theorem, the Craig Interpolation Property implies the Beth Definability Property.
For an overview of interpolation and definability properties in the setting of modal logics we refer to the monograph by Gabbay and Maksimova
[
20
]
.

To prove that a given logic has interpolation, proof methods from various areas have been developed, including algebra, model theory, proof theory and automata theory.
A widely applied proof-theoretic method originates with Maehara
[
36
]
: here one obtains an interpolant for a derivable implication, by taking some derivation for the implication and defining interpolants for every node of the derivation tree by a leaf-to-root induction.

Interpolation is a rather
fragile
property.
For instance, it is not preserved under axiomatic extensions of a logic or inherited by its fragments.
To elaborate on the latter: if a logic has interpolation, this does not necessarily mean that the interpolant of two formulas that both belong to some distinguished fragment of the language also belongs to this fragment.
To see a concrete example of this, observe that although full first-order logic has the Craig interpolation property
[
14
, Lemma 1]
, the two-variable fragment
\mathrm{FO}^{2}
does not: there exist sentences
\varphi,\psi\in\mathrm{FO}^{2}
where
\varphi\to\psi
is a valid sentence for which no
\mathrm{FO}^{2}
-interpolant exists, so that any first-order interpolant must use at least three variables
[
13
, Corollary 4]
; for a more modern reference see for example the work of ten Cate and Comer
[
50
]
.

Returning to the question whether PDL has interpolation, we must observe that the literature on this matter is rather puzzling.
As far as we know, affirmative answers have been claimed on three occasions.
The first of these claims was made in 1981 by Leivant
[
35
, Theorem 5.3.2]
and refuted in 1999 by Kracht
[
32
]
, but for the wrong
reasons
[
21
]
.
There is, however, a different gap in Leivant’s proof for which no bridge has been found — see
Appendix C
.
The second claim was made seven years later by Borzechowski, one of this paper’s coauthors, in his Diplomarbeit
[
8
, Theorem 5]
(comparable to an MSc thesis).
Written in German and never properly published, this work received little attention; the general opinion, if any, seems to have been that this work could be dismissed since it was not “possible to verify the argument”
[
32
, p. 493]
.
Finally, the third claim, made by Kowalski
[
29
, Theorem 2]
in 2002, was refuted and officially withdrawn in 2004
[
30
]
.
In
Appendix C
we review this intriguing history in more detail.

Propositional Dynamic Logic as a fixpoint logic

One way to understand why the interpolation problem for PDL constitutes such a challenging problem is by taking the perspective of modal fixpoint logic.
Since
{}*
-free PDL is just a notational variation of basic multi-modal logic, many if not all of the challenges in the study of PDL involve the iteration operator
{}*
, and it is this operator that takes Propositional Dynamic Logic inside the field of modal fixpoint logics (and outside the area of compact logics such as first-order logic).
The key step to see this is to think of the formula
[\alpha^{*}]\varphi
as the greatest fixpoint of the formula
\varphi\land[\alpha]x
.
That is, writing
\equiv
for semantic equivalence, we think of
[\alpha^{*}]\varphi
as a solution to the “equation”
x\equiv\varphi\land[\alpha]x
, in the sense that

[\alpha^{*}]\varphi\equiv\varphi\land[\alpha][\alpha^{*}]\varphi,

and it is the
largest
such, in the sense that for any formula
\psi
we have the following:

\text{if }\psi\equiv\varphi\land[\alpha]\psi\text{ then }\psi\text{ implies }[\alpha^{*}]\varphi.

Based on this observation, one may define a truth-preserving embedding of PDL into the modal
\mu
-calculus (
\mu
ML).
This logic, which extends basic poly-modal logic with arbitrary least- and greatest fixpoint operators, is a formalism of considerable importance, both because of its applications in computer science and for its metalogical properties, see Bradfield and Stirling
[
9
]
for an overview.
In fact, PDL corresponds to a natural fragment of the modal
\mu
-calculus, see Carreiro and Venema
[
12
]
for the details.

Interpolation for fixpoint logics

Over the past three decades various results concerning interpolation properties
for modal fixpoint logics have been obtained, both positive and negative.
A quite striking result was obtained by D’Agostino and Hollenberg, who proved, using automata-theoretic tools, that the modal
\mu
-calculus enjoys
uniform
interpolation
[
17
, Corollary 3.8]
, a very strong version of interpolation where the interpolant only depends on one of the two formulas and the vocabulary of the other.
These authors also showed that any extension of PDL with the uniform interpolation property must have the same expressive power as the full
\mu
-calculus.
From this it is immediate that the uniform interpolation property fails for PDL.
Another negative result worth mentioning concerns a fragment ML
∗
of PDL which features only a minimal trace of the iteration operator.
ML
∗
is obtained by adding the master modality
[*]
to the language of basic poly-modal logic — this modality can be expressed in PDL as
[(a_{1}\cup\cdots\cup a_{n})^{*}]
, if we restrict the language to a finite number of atomic programs
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
.
It was proved that this fragment of PDL does not have Craig interpolation by Studer
[
47
, Theorem 4]
, using an argument going back to Maksimova
[
37
]
.

Moving back to the positive side, in recent years Maehara’s method has become applicable to fixpoint logics through the introduction of so-called
cyclic
proof systems
[
11
,
10
]
.
Cyclic proof and tableau systems have been introduced for various modal fixpoint logics; in particular, the focus games of Lange and Stirling can be seen as cyclic tableau systems, respectively for linear time logic and computation tree logic
[
33
]
, and for PDL
[
34
]
.
In a cyclic proof, open leaves are allowed, as long as from each of these there is a back edge to an identically labeled ancestor (which is called the leaf’s companion), and each path from a companion to a repeat leaf satisfies some global soundness (or “progress”) condition.

Afshari and Leigh established the Lyndon interpolation property (a slight strengthening of the CIP) for the modal
\mu
-calculus using a cyclic sequent calculus
[
2
, Theorem 2]
.
Similarly, Marti and Venema introduced a focus-style derivation system for the alternation-free
\mu
-calculus (a fragment of
\mu
ML extending PDL), and used this to give a Maehara-style interpolation proof for this
logic
[
38
, Theorem 7.2]
.
(Note that the property itself had been established earlier by D’Agostino
[
18
, Theorem 3]
, who based her proof on automata-theoretic work by Santocanale and Arnold
[
45
]
.)
The idea underlying these interpolation proofs is to think of a finite, cyclic derivation of an implication as a
system of equations
, where each node of the derivation tree induces an equation, the shape of which is determined by the rule applied at the node — in the style of Maehara’s method.
In order to find the interpolant for the implication at the root of the derivation tree, one needs to
solve these equations
, that is, find a certain set of formulas, indexed by the nodes of the derivation tree, that
satisfy these equations.

Interpolation for PDL

We may now give an explanation why establishing the Craig Interpolation Property is significantly harder for PDL than for either the full
\mu
-calculus or its alternation-free fragment.
Given the developments in cyclic proof theory, it is relatively easy to come up with a well-behaved cyclic proof system (or tableau calculus) for PDL, such that for any cyclic derivation of an implication
\varphi\to\psi
one may define a system of equations as sketched above.
The problem is that, while the systems of equations one obtains can easily be solved in the alternation-free
\mu
-calculus, it seems to be impossible to set up a proof system such that the system of equations admits solutions

inside PDL
.
It is precisely here that the insights from Borzechowski’s
Diplomarbeit
play an important role.

Contribution

Based on ideas from Borzechowski’s
Diplomarbeit

[
8
]
, we show that Propositional Dynamic Logic has the Craig Interpolation Property, and, as a corollary, the Beth Definability Property.

We first introduce a new, cyclic, tableau system for PDL, with finitary rules such that proofs are finite trees.
We use a certain
loading
mechanism, which allows one to place a focus on certain formulas in the tableau, in order to formulate an appropriate soundness condition for companion-repeat paths.
While the rules for the boolean connectives and for the atomic modal programs are standard, a novelty in our approach is the treatment of complex programs.
With every formula of the form
[\alpha]\psi
, where
\alpha
is non-atomic, we associate a finite collection
\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)
of special sets of formulas such that
[\alpha]\psi
is equivalent to the disjunction
\displaystyle{\bigvee}\big\{\bigwedge\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)\big\}
, and the
unfolding rule
then simply replaces
[\alpha]\psi
with the respective members of

\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)
.
A similar rule takes care of formulas of the form
\neg[\alpha]\psi
.

Turning to the interpolation proof, we follow Maehara’s proof-theoretical method for establishing interpolation, and develop a version of the tableau system that operates on
split sequents
, that is, pairs of finite sets of formulas.
Given the cyclic nature of the tableau system, closed tableaux have a natural notion of a cluster, that is, a maximal strongly connected component of the graph that is obtained by adding the back edges from repeats to their companion to the parent-child relation of the tableau.
The idea of the proof is to define interpolants, by a leaf-to-root induction on the tableau structure, for the roots of these clusters.
The key insight of Borzechowski’s approach is that, in order to define the interpolant for the root of a (non-trivial) cluster, we use an
auxiliary structure
rather than the cluster itself to set up an appropriate system of equations.
This shift ensures that solutions for these equations can be found inside the language of PDL.
We will provide more intuitions on our approach in
Subsection 7.2
.

Outline

This article is structured as follows. We introduce preliminaries in
Section 2
, including the standard syntax and semantics of PDL, and notation and conventions for tableau systems.
In
Section 3
, we define the local part of our tableau system, and in
Section 4
, a full tableau system for PDL.
We then show soundness of the system in
Section 5
, completeness in
Section 6
and finally interpolation in
Section 7
.
We conclude in
Section 11
and provide an overview of notation and symbols in
Appendix A
.
As we also discuss in
Section 11
, we are formalizing the proof given here in the interactive theorem prover Lean 4 in an ongoing project.
In the present document, labels such as “
truthLemma
✓” provide a link to the corresponding definition or statement in the formalization.
A check mark next to the label means that this lemma or theorem has been fully formalized and verified in Lean. We note that the Lean proof may sometimes proceed differently than the paper proofs, and we refer to the code documentation for details about these differences.

2.
Preliminaries

We will write
\mathbb{N}
for the set of natural numbers (including
0
).
For finite sets, instead of writing
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}
we may write
{\{x_{i}\}}_{i\leq k}
. For countably infinite sets, we sometimes use
\omega
in place of
k
.
To denote sequences (or lists) we will simply use juxtaposition; instead of
x_{1}\cdots x_{k}
we may sometimes write
{(x_{i})}_{i\leq k}
.
We will use the notation
\varepsilon
to represent the empty sequence.

Given a set
A
, we denote the identity relation on
A
by
\mathsf{Id}_{A}\mathrel{:=}\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}
.
Let
A,B,C
be sets. Let
R\subseteq A\times B
and
S\subseteq B\times C
be two binary relations.
The composition of
R
and
S
, denoted
R\mathop{;}S
, is defined as:

R\mathop{;}S\mathrel{:=}\{(a,c)\mid\exists b\in B\text{ such that }(a,b)\in R\text{ and }(b,c)\in S\}

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
R\subseteq A\times A
.
The
n
-fold composition of
R
, denoted
R^{n}
, is defined as
R^{0}\mathrel{:=}\mathsf{Id}_{A}
and
R^{n}\mathrel{:=}R^{n-1}\mathop{;}R
for
n\geq 1
.
The reflexive-transitive closure of
R
, denoted
R^{*}
, is defined as
R^{*}\mathrel{:=}\bigcup\{R^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Given a binary relation
R
over a set
A
, we define an
R
-path
of length
n
as a finite sequence of elements
x_{0}x_{1}\ldots x_{n}
such that
(x_{i},x_{i+1})\in R
for all
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\}
. Given
a,b\in A
we will say that the path is
from
a
to
b
whenever
x_{0}=a
and
x_{n}=b
.

2.1.
Syntax of PDL

Fix
\mathcal{F}_{0}
to be a countably infinite set of proposition letters and
\mathcal{P}_{0}
to be a countably infinite set of atomic programs.

{defi}

The set
\mathcal{F}
of PDL formulas and the set
\mathcal{P}
of PDL programs are defined by mutual recursion as follows:

\mathcal{F}\ni\varphi,\psi,\tau\,\,\Coloneqq\,\,\bot\mid p\mid\lnot\varphi\mid\varphi\land\psi\mid[\alpha]\varphi

\mathcal{P}\ni\alpha,\beta\,\,\Coloneqq\,\,a\mid\tau?\mid\alpha\cup\beta\mid\alpha;\beta\mid\alpha^{\ast}

where
p\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
When clear from the context, we will talk of formulas and programs rather than PDL formulas and PDL programs.
Other connectives
\lor
,
\to
and
\leftrightarrow
are defined as usual in terms of
\neg
and
\land
.

PDL is a logic of programs where formulas of the form
[\alpha]\varphi
are to be read as:
\varphi
is true after all possible executions of the program
\alpha
.
The program
\alpha
in this formula can either be an
atomic
program
a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
or a complex program.
Next, we describe all possible complex programs.
A program of the form
\tau?
, called a
test
, represents a program that only succeeds when
\tau
is true.
A program of the form
\alpha\cup\beta
, called a
non-deterministic choice
, represents a program that executes
\alpha
or
\beta
non-deterministically.
A program of the form
\alpha;\beta
, called a
sequential composition
, represents a program that sequentially executes
\alpha
followed by
\beta
.
A program of the form
\alpha^{*}
, called an
iteration
, represents a program that executes
\alpha
a non-deterministically determined finite number of times.

For tests
\tau?
, we could have used
\varphi
or
\psi
for the formula
\tau
, but we prefer to use the symbol
\tau
to stress that it is the formula of a test.

The logic
\mathbf{PDL}
is usually defined via the following Hilbert-style axiomatisation.
We identify the logic with the set of its validities and will provide semantics later in
Subsection 2.2
.

{defi}

The logic
\mathbf{PDL}
is the least set of formulas that contains all propositional tautologies,
all instances of the following axiom schemas,

\begin{array}[]{ll}(K)&[\alpha](\varphi\to\psi)\to([\alpha]\varphi\to[\alpha]\psi)\\
(\mathrm{Ax}(?))&[\tau?]\varphi\leftrightarrow\neg\tau\lor\varphi\\
(\mathrm{Ax}(\cup))&[\alpha\cup\beta]\varphi\leftrightarrow[\alpha]\varphi\land[\beta]\varphi\\
(\mathrm{Ax}(;))&[\alpha;\beta]\varphi\leftrightarrow[\alpha][\beta]\varphi\\
(\mathrm{Ax}(^{*}))&[\alpha^{*}]\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi\land[\alpha][\alpha^{*}]\varphi\\
\mathrm{(Ind)}&\varphi\land[\alpha^{*}](\varphi\to[\alpha]\varphi)\to[\alpha^{*}]\varphi\end{array}

and is closed under modus ponens and necessitation (that is,
\varphi\in\mathbf{PDL}\text{ implies }[\alpha]\varphi\in\mathbf{PDL}
).

Next, we give a series of auxiliary definitions that will be used throughout the document.

As usual, the
subformulas
of a formula
\varphi
(or a program
\alpha
) refer to the set of formulas occurring in
\varphi
(respectively in
\alpha
).
Similarly, the
subprograms
of a formula
\varphi
(or a program
\alpha
) refer to the set of programs occurring in
\varphi
(respectively in
\alpha
).

Due to the mutual recursion between formulas and programs in
Subsection 2.1
,
tests and programs can be nested inside test formulas.
For instance,
p?
is a subprogram of
[p?]q?
and
p
is a subformula of
[p?]q?
.
However, when doing an inductive proof based on
Subsection 2.1
, the inductive step often only considers the
shallow
program structure for which the base cases are atomic programs and shallow tests.
For a program
\alpha
, the shallow tests and shallow subprograms of
\alpha
are the tests and programs that occur in
\alpha
that are not nested inside other tests.
Formally, we define shallow tests as follows:

{defi}

[
testsOfProgram
]
We define the set
\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
of
shallow tests of
\alpha
as:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathit{Tests}(a)&\mathrel{:=}&\emptyset\\
\mathit{Tests}(\tau?)&\mathrel{:=}&\{\tau\}\\
\mathit{Tests}(\alpha\cup\beta)&\mathrel{:=}&\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\cup\mathit{Tests}(\beta)\\
\mathit{Tests}(\alpha;\beta)&\mathrel{:=}&\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\cup\mathit{Tests}(\beta)\\
\mathit{Tests}(\alpha^{\ast})&\mathrel{:=}&\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\end{array}

where
a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{F}
.

We analogously define shallow subprograms as follows:

{defi}

[
subprograms
]
We define the set
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
of shallow subprograms of
\alpha
as:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathit{Progs}(a)&\mathrel{:=}&\{a\}\\
\mathit{Progs}(\tau?)&\mathrel{:=}&\{\tau?\}\\
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha\cup\beta)&\mathrel{:=}&\{\alpha\cup\beta\}\cup\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)\cup\mathit{Progs}(\beta)\\
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha;\beta)&\mathrel{:=}&\{\alpha;\beta\}\cup\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)\cup\mathit{Progs}(\beta)\\
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha^{\ast})&\mathrel{:=}&\{\alpha^{\ast}\}\cup\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)\end{array}

where
a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{F}
.

{exa}

We illustrate how
\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
does not include the tests that appear inside tests,
and how
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
does not include programs that occur inside tests.
We have:

(1)

\mathit{Tests}([q?]p?;a)=\{[q?]p\}
. Note that
q\notin\mathit{Tests}([q?]p?;a)
.

(2)

\mathit{Progs}([q?]p?;a)=\{[q?]p?;a,[q?]p?,a\}
. Note that
q?\notin\mathit{Progs}([q?]p?;a)
.

Given a list of programs
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}
and a formula
\varphi
we write
\Box(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n},\varphi)
to mean the formula
[\alpha_{1}]\dots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi
.
Formally:

{defi}

[
Formula.boxes
]
Given a list
\vec{\alpha}
of programs and a formula
\varphi
, we define the
formula
\Box(\vec{\alpha},\varphi)
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Box(\varepsilon,\varphi)&\mathrel{:=}&\varphi\\
\Box(\alpha\vec{\beta},\varphi)&\mathrel{:=}&[\alpha]\Box(\vec{\beta},\varphi)\end{array}

We have the usual definition of uniform substitution of proposition letters for formulas.

{defi}

[
Substitution
]
A
substitution
is a function mapping proposition letters to formulas.
Any such map
\sigma\colon\mathcal{F}_{0}\to\mathcal{F}
can be extended to a function
\langle\sigma\rangle\colon\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{P}

mapping formulas to formulas, and programs to programs, as follows.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bot\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&\bot\\
p\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&\sigma(p)\\
(\lnot\varphi)\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&\lnot\varphi\langle\sigma\rangle\\
(\varphi\land\psi)\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&\varphi\langle\sigma\rangle\land\psi\langle\sigma\rangle\\
([\alpha]\varphi)\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&[\alpha\langle\sigma\rangle]\varphi\langle\sigma\rangle\end{array}\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}a\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&a\\
(\alpha;\beta)\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&\alpha\langle\sigma\rangle;\beta\langle\sigma\rangle\\
(\alpha\cup\beta)\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&\alpha\langle\sigma\rangle\cup\beta\langle\sigma\rangle\\
\alpha^{\ast}\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&{(\alpha\langle\sigma\rangle)}^{\ast}\\
(\tau?)\langle\sigma\rangle&\mathrel{:=}&(\tau\langle\sigma\rangle)?\end{array}

The substitution
p\mapsto\theta
denotes the function that maps
p
to
\theta
and every other proposition letter to itself.
In the case that
p
occurs in
\varphi
we generally write
\varphi(q)
to denote the formula
\varphi\langle p\mapsto q\rangle
.

Note that in
Subsection 2.1
we use postfix notation and write the substitution after the formula to which it is applied.
For example, let
\sigma
be a substitution such that we have
\sigma(p)=r
and
\sigma(r)=p
.
Then
([[r?]p?]r)\langle\sigma\rangle=[[p?]r?]p
.

{defi}

Given a formula
\varphi
, the
single negation
\sim\!\varphi
of
\varphi
is the formula

\sim\!\varphi\mathrel{:=}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\psi&\text{if }\varphi\text{ is of the form }\lnot\psi,\\
\lnot\varphi&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

A set
X
of formulas is
closed under single negations
if for all formulas
\varphi
:

\varphi\in X\text{ implies }\sim\!\varphi\in X.

The following definition of the Fischer-Ladner closure is adapted from
[
6
, Definition 4.79]
.
We will use it to ensure termination of our proof system, e.g. for
29
.

{defi}

[
FL
,
FLb
,
FLL
]
A set
X
of formulas is called
Fischer-Ladner closed
if it is closed under single negations and subformulas and we have that:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}[\alpha\cup\beta]\varphi\in X&\text{ implies }&[\alpha]\varphi\in X\text{ and }[\beta]\varphi\in X\\[3.00003pt]
[\alpha;\beta]\varphi\in X&\text{ implies }&[\alpha][\beta]\varphi\in X\\[3.00003pt]
[\alpha^{\ast}]\varphi\in X&\text{ implies }&[\alpha][\alpha^{\ast}]\varphi\in X\end{array}

For any set
X
of formulas, we define its
Fischer-Ladner closure
denoted
\mathit{FL}(X)
as the smallest set containing
X
that is Fischer-Ladner closed.

The Fischer-Ladner closure of a finite set is finite.
For proofs, see
[
19
, Theorem 3.2]
and
[
24
, Section 6.1]
,
and note that
FLL
in Lean returns a
List
that must be finite.

We end this subsection with the definition of basic formulas, a notion we will use later to restrict when specific tableau rules may be applied.

{defi}

[
Formula.basic
]
A set of formulas is
basic
if it only contains formulas of the
forms
\bot
,
\lnot\bot
,
p
,
\lnot p
,
[a]\varphi
and
\lnot[a]\varphi
where
p\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
.

2.2.
Semantics of PDL

PDL formulas of the form
[\alpha]\varphi
are to be read as stating that
all possible executions of the program
\alpha
from the current state end in a state where
\varphi
holds
.
Intuitively, these states represent states in some computing environment, the programs represent computations that transform one state into another, and atomic programs represent atomic computations like primitive instructions in a programming language.
It is important to mention that these programs can behave non-deterministically, and for this reason we talk of
all
possible executions when interpreting the meaning of
[\alpha]\varphi
.

More abstractly, we view these states as states in a Kripke model, where the edges between these states are labeled by atomic programs and where in each state a set of proposition letters holds.

{defi}

[
KripkeModel
]
A
Kripke model

\mathcal{M}=(W,\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\},V)
consists of a set
W
of states, an accessibility relation
R_{a}\subseteq W^{2}
for every atomic program
a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, and a valuation
V\colon\mathcal{F}_{0}\to\mathcal{P}(W)
that assigns to each proposition letter a subset of
W
.
A pointed model is a pair
(\mathcal{M},v)
where
\mathcal{M}
is a Kripke model and
v
is a state in
\mathcal{M}
.

Next, we give the usual Kripke semantics that matches the previously discussed intuitions about the meaning of PDL formulas and programs.

{defi}

[
evaluate
,
relate
]
Let
\mathcal{M}=(W,\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\},V)
be a Kripke model.
The
truth
of a formula
\varphi
in
\mathcal{M}
at a state
v
(denoted as
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\varphi
)
and the interpretation of complex programs are defined by a mutual induction as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{M},v\Vdash p&\text{ iff }&v\in V(p)\\
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\lnot\varphi&\text{ iff }&\mathcal{M},v\not\Vdash\varphi\\
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\varphi\land\psi&\text{ iff }&\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\psi\\
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash[\alpha]\varphi&\text{ iff }&\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\varphi\text{ for all $w$ with }(v,w)\in R_{\alpha}\\[10.00002pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lll}R_{\varphi?}&\mathrel{:=}&\{(v,v)\mid v\Vdash\varphi\}\hfill(\text{test})\\
R_{\alpha\cup\beta}&\mathrel{:=}&R_{\alpha}\cup R_{\beta}\hfill(\text{choice})\\
R_{\alpha;\beta}&\mathrel{:=}&R_{\alpha}\mathop{;}R_{\beta}=\{(w,v)\mid\exists u:(w,u)\in R_{\alpha}\land(u,v)\in R_{\beta}\}\;\;\hfill(\text{composition})\\
R_{\alpha^{\ast}}&\mathrel{:=}&R_{\alpha}^{\ast}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\omega}R_{\alpha}^{n}\hfill(\text{reflexive transitive closure})\end{array}

Note that
R_{a}
for atomic programs is given by
\mathcal{M}
.
For
(v,w)\in R_{\alpha}
we also write
v\xrightarrow{\alpha}w
.
Given a set
X
of formulas, we write
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash X
if
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\varphi
for all
\varphi\in X
.

We extend the interpretation of single programs to lists of programs.

{defi}

[
relateSeq
]
For any list of programs
\vec{\delta}
and for any model
\mathcal{M}
with set of states
W
, we define
R_{\vec{\delta}}
inductively as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}R_{\varepsilon}&\mathrel{:=}&\mathsf{Id}_{W}=\{(v,v)\mid v\in W\}\\
R_{\alpha\vec{\delta}}&\mathrel{:=}&R_{\alpha}\mathop{;}R_{\vec{\delta}}\end{array}

where
\alpha
is a program.

Notice that for any Kripke model
(W,\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\},V)
and for any list
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}
of programs it holds that
R_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}=R_{\alpha_{1};\dots;\alpha_{n}}
.

Our consequence relation is defined as follows.

{defi}

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be formulas and let
X
be a set of formulas.
We say that
\varphi
is a
(local) semantic consequence
of
X
(written
X\models\varphi
), if for all pointed models
(\mathcal{M},v)
, if
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash X
then
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\varphi
.
We say
\varphi
is
semantically valid
(written
\models\varphi
), if for all pointed models
(\mathcal{M},v)
, we have that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\varphi
.
Finally, we say that
\varphi
and
\psi
are
semantically equivalent
(written
\varphi\equiv\psi
), if for all pointed models
(\mathcal{M},v)
we have that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\varphi
if and only if
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\psi
.

The Hilbert style axiomatisation of
\mathbf{PDL}
from
Subsection 2.1
is known to be sound and complete with respect to the above Kripke semantics:
\varphi\in\mathbf{PDL}
iff
\models\varphi
, for all formulas
\varphi
.
For a proof, see
[
41
]
for test-free PDL, and e.g.
[
32
, Sec. 10.2]
for full PDL.

The next theorem, called the Local Deduction Theorem, is used to show that the Beth definability property follows from the interpolation property in the proof of
51
.

Theorem 1

(
deduction
✓)
.

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be formulas and let
X
be a set of formulas.
Then
X\cup\{\psi\}\models\varphi
if and only if
X\models\psi\to\varphi
.

Proof 2.1
.

This follows directly from
Subsection 2.2
and
Subsection 2.2
.

The next lemma states that given a substitution
\sigma
(see
Subsection 2.1
), a Kripke model
\mathcal{M}
, and a formula
\varphi
, the formula
\varphi\langle\sigma\rangle
is true in state
w
if and only if the formula
\varphi
is true in the same state
w
in a model equal to
\mathcal{M}
except that the valuation is updated with the substitution as given by
V^{\sigma}
below.

Lemma 2

(
substitutionLemma
✓)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(W,R,V)
be a Kripke model and
\sigma
a substitution.
For every state
w
in
\mathcal{M}
and every formula
\varphi
, we have

\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\varphi\langle\sigma\rangle\text{ iff }\mathcal{M}^{\sigma},w\Vdash\varphi,

where
\mathcal{M}^{\sigma}
is the model
(W,R,V^{\sigma})
with
V^{\sigma}

the valuation given by

V^{\sigma}(p)\mathrel{:=}\{v\in W\mid\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\sigma(p)\}.

Proof 2.2
.

We also claim that for any two states
v
and
w
in
\mathcal{M}
and for any program
\alpha
, we have

(v,w)\in R_{\alpha\langle\sigma\rangle}\text{ iff }(v,w)\in R^{\mathcal{M}^{\sigma}}_{\alpha}.

where
R^{\mathcal{M}^{\sigma}}_{\alpha}
is the accessibility relation for
\alpha
in the model
\mathcal{M}^{\sigma}
.
This claim and the lemma can then be shown by a parallel mutual induction on the formula structure of
\varphi
and the program structure of
\alpha
.

We finish this section with one more useful technical lemma about the semantics of the iteration operator.

Lemma 3

(
stepToStar
✓)
.

For all formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
, and all programs
\alpha
,
if
\varphi\models\psi\land[\alpha]\varphi
then
\varphi\models[\alpha^{\ast}]\psi
.

Proof 2.3
.

This follows directly from the semantics in
Subsection 2.2
.

2.3.
Tableau Calculi

Trees are the fundamental structures underlying tableaux. We fix notation and recall concepts.

A
rooted tree
is a triple
(V,\lessdot,r)
where
V
is a set of nodes,
\lessdot
is a binary relation on
V
and
r\in V
, called the
root
of the tree, is such that for every node
s\in V
there is a unique
\lessdot
-path from
r
to
s
.
If
s\lessdot t
, then
s
is called the
parent
of
t
, and
t
a
child
of
s
.
If a node has no children then it is called a
leaf
, otherwise it is called an
interior node
.
We denote the transitive closure of
\lessdot
by
<
, and the reflexive and transitive closure by
\leq
.
If
s<t
we say that
s
is an
ancestor
of
t
and that
t
is a
descendant
of
s
.

In this document, we visualize trees as growing downwards from their root, so e.g., an ancestor of a node
s
is depicted above
s
.

A
sequent
is a finite set of formulas.
We will use
\Gamma,\Sigma,\Delta
to denote sequents.
Instead of
\Gamma\cup\Delta
we will write
\Gamma,\Delta
, and instead of
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
we will write
\Gamma,\varphi
.
Also, instead of
\Gamma\setminus\{\varphi\}
we will write
\Gamma\setminus\varphi
.

A tableau calculus is a finite set of
schematic inference rules
.
Schematic inference rules specify how to transform a sequent into a list of new sequents based on the form of the formulas contained within the original sequent.

In
Figure 1
, we present some examples of inference rules for tableau calculi.
The sequent above the horizontal line in a rule is called the
parent
of the rule, while the sequents below the line that are separated by the symbol
\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt
are called
children
of the rule.
Sometimes, instead of separating the rule children with
\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt
, we might write a set below the horizontal line, as exemplified by Rule
(P)
.
In this case, each element in this set is a rule child.
A rule is said to be
branching
if it has more than one rule child.
For instance, the rule
(\lnot\land)
is branching whereas
(\lnot)
is not.

\Delta,\lnot\lnot\varphi

(\lnot)

\Delta,\varphi

\Delta,\lnot(\varphi\land\psi)

(\lnot\land)

\Delta,\lnot\varphi\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Delta,\lnot\psi

\Delta,[\alpha]\varphi

(P)

\big\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in P(\alpha,\varphi)\big\}

Figure 1.
Examples of tableau rules.

The formula that appears in the rule parent is called the
principal formula
of the rule.
For instance,
\lnot\lnot\varphi
is the principal formula of rule
(\lnot)
.
The sequent
\Delta
that appears in the rules of
Figure 1
is called the
context
of the rule.
More generally, the context is the sequent that appears on the rule parent next to the principal formula.

A
rule instance
is an instantiation of the parent and children of the rule with specific sequents that match the pattern of the rule.
We will say that a rule can be applied to a sequent whenever the sequent matches the parent of the rule.
For instance, consider the rule
(\lnot\land)
.
The sequent
[a]p,\lnot(p\land q)
matches the rule parent,
[a]p,\lnot p
the first rule child and
[a]p,\lnot q
the second rule child, and thus the three sequents together are a rule instance of
(\lnot\land)
.
For brevity, we will write the rule instances as
\Gamma_{0}~/~\Gamma_{1}|\dots|\Gamma_{n}
where
{\{\Gamma_{i}\}}_{i\leq n}
are sequents.
Here the symbol
~/~
is used to separate the sequent that instantiates the rule parent and the ones that instantiate the rule children whereas
|
is used to separate the rule children.
Using this notation we can say that
[a]p,\lnot(p\land q)~/~[a]p,\lnot p\mid[a]p,\lnot q
is a rule instance of
(\lnot\land)
.

We use the same terminology that we used for rules for rule instances.
For example, the principal formula of a rule instance is simply the formula that substitutes the principal formula in the rule.

A key convention we follow when interpreting inference rules is that:

In all rule instances the principal formula is not an element of the context.

For example,
\lnot\lnot p~/~\lnot\lnot p,p
is not a rule instance of
(\lnot)
whereas
\lnot\lnot p~/~p
is.

Rules might have side conditions under which they are instantiated.
For example, we could add a side condition to the rule
(\lnot)
stating that the context
\Delta
does not contain a negated formula.
Then, with this side condition, the sequent
\lnot q,\lnot\lnot p~/~\lnot q,p
is not a rule instance of
(\lnot)
.

Next, we will give a formal definition of a
tableau
over a set
\mathsf{L}
of inference rules.
Informally, tableaux are rooted trees in which the nodes are labeled with sequents, and their construction adheres to the rules in
\mathsf{L}
.

{defi}

Given a set
\mathsf{L}
of inference rules,
a
tableau
\mathcal{T}
for a sequent
\Gamma
is a tuple
(V,\lessdot,r,\Lambda,L)
where

(V,\lessdot,r)
is a rooted tree,

L
is a function that assigns to every interior node
t\in V
a rule
L(t)\in\mathsf{L}
,

\Lambda
is a function that assigns to every node
t\in V
a sequent
\Lambda(t)
such that

\Lambda(r)=\Gamma
, and
if
s\in V
is an interior node with children
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
then
\Lambda(s)~/~\Lambda(t_{1})|\dots|\Lambda(t_{n})
is a rule instance of the rule
L(s)
(respecting any side conditions).

The tableau in
Figure 2
is an example of how we represent tableaux.
The root is always the uppermost node in the drawing.
Note that we do not write names for the nodes and instead write the corresponding sequent
\Lambda(s)
associated with a node
s
.
Whenever
s\lessdot t
we draw an arrow from
s
to
t
.
The rule
L(s)
associated to an interior node
s
is written next to the arrows to the children of
s
.

\lnot\lnot p{,}\ \lnot(p\land q)
p{,}\ \lnot(p\land q)
(\lnot)

p,\lnot p
(\lnot\land)

p,\lnot q
(\lnot\land)

Figure 2.
An example of a tableau over the rules
(\lnot)
and
(\lnot\land)
.

{defi}

A tableau is
maximal
if no rules can be applied to any of the sequents of the leaves.

For example, the tableau in
Figure 2
is maximal, as neither
(\lnot)
nor
(\lnot\land)
can be applied to
p,\lnot p
nor to
p,\lnot q
.

Intuitively, a tableau represents an attempt to construct a Kripke model satisfying the sequent at the root.
The following two definitions make this precise and will be used later.

{defi}

A tableau rule is
locally sound
if for all its instances
\Delta~/~\Gamma_{1}|\dots|\Gamma_{n}
and for all pointed models
(\mathcal{M},v)
: if
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta
then
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Gamma_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
.
It is
locally invertible
if conversely, for all its instances
\Delta~/~\Gamma_{1}|\dots|\Gamma_{n}
and for all pointed models
(\mathcal{M},v)
: If
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Gamma_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
then
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta
.

As an example, both the rules
(\lnot)
and
(\lnot\land)
are locally sound and locally invertible.
We include “locally” here because both definitions above are about the local truth at a specific state in a Kripke model — not about satisfiability.

3.
Local Tableaux

We now start to define a tableau proof system.
We make a key distinction between local and modal reasoning.
Local means that we reason about the same state in a potential Kripke model.
For example, we can go from
[a\cup b^{\ast}]p
to
[a]p{,}\ p{,}\ [b][b^{\ast}]p
by local reasoning, but from
\lnot[a]p
to
\lnot p
we need a modal step.
Here in
Section 3
, we only consider local reasoning.

As we are defining a tableau system for PDL the reader may expect reduction rules corresponding to the PDL axioms in
Subsection 2.1
.
Each of these rules would be applicable to formulas of the form
[\alpha]\psi
where
\alpha
has the corresponding top-level constructor.
For example, a
(\ast)
rule corresponding to the axiom
(\mathrm{Ax}(^{*}))
would allow us to replace
\Gamma,[\alpha^{\ast}]\psi
with
\Gamma,\psi,[\alpha][\alpha^{\ast}]\psi
.
With such rules we obtain the (not necessarily maximal) tableau shown in
Figure 3
.

[(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}]\psi
[a\cup p?][b^{\ast}]\psi
(;)

[a][b^{\ast}]\psi{,}\ [p?][b^{\ast}]\psi
(\cup)

(?)
[a][b^{\ast}]\psi{,}\ \lnot p
[a][b^{\ast}]\psi{,}\ [b^{\ast}]\psi
[a][b^{\ast}]\psi{,}\ \psi{,}\ [b][b^{\ast}]\psi
(\ast)

Figure 3.
A tableau with reduction rules for each program constructor — not our system.

We do
not
define our system with a rule for each program constructor as in
Figure 3
.
Instead, we have only
one general rule
(\Box)
which is applicable to all formulas of the form
[\alpha]\psi
where
\alpha
is a non-atomic program.
The rule
(\Box)
is called
local
, since it only incorporates propositional reasoning, and no modal reasoning.
Applying the rule
(\Box)
to
[\alpha]\psi
can be understood as reducing
\alpha
maximally by applying
({;})
,
(\cup)
,
(?)
,
(\ast)
exhaustively, while treating
\psi
and all
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
as atomic.
To illustrate, instead of
Figure 3
, our system will generate the tableau in
Figure 4
.

[(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}]\psi
(
\Box
)
[a][b^{\ast}]\psi{,}\ \lnot p
[a][b^{\ast}]\psi{,}\ \psi{,}\ [b][b^{\ast}]\psi

Figure 4.
A tableau in our system consisting of a single application of the
(\Box)
rule.

A key challenge when designing any PDL proof system is that naive rewriting of formulas of the form
[\alpha^{\ast}]\psi
may lead to this formula being regenerated locally, i.e., the formula
[\alpha^{\ast}]\psi
may occur again without a modal step having been applied.
For example, if we apply the rule
(\ast)
(corresponding to axiom
(\mathrm{Ax}(^{*}))
from
Subsection 2.1
) twice to
[{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
then besides
p
and
[a][a^{*}][{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
we also obtain
[{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
again.
We show this in the top left of
Figure 5
and note that, unless we build other constraints into our system, such a repeat would lead to an infinite tableau.
To avoid such issues, our rule
(\Box)
will eliminate the repeated unfolded formula as part of its definition, resulting in the bottom left of
Figure 5
.

[(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
p{,}\ [a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(\ast)

p{,}\ [(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(\ast)

[(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(\Box)

\lnot[(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(\lnot\ast)
\lnot p
\lnot[a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(\lnot\ast)
\lnot[(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
\lnot[a][a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
\times
\lnot[(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
\lnot p
\lnot[a][a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p

Figure 5.
Two tableaux with reduction rules for
[{(a^{*})}^{*}]p
and
\lnot[{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
and below them their corresponding local tableaux in our system.

Similarly to the local rule
(\Box)
, we will define a local rule
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
to reduce formulas of the shape
\lnot[\alpha]\psi
which we call
diamond formulas
.
To avoid getting into similar infinite repeats when unfolding diamond formulas, the rule
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
will eliminate sequents that contain the formula we are unfolding.
In
Figure 5
on the right the
\times
indicates such a sequent.

The soundness of both these eliminations can informally be explained by the fact that formulas of the form
[\beta^{*}]\varphi
are greatest fixpoints, and are therefore satisfied when unfolded infinitely often, whereas formulas of the form
\lnot[\beta^{*}]\varphi
are least fixpoints, and are therefore not satisfied when unfolded infinitely often.

We prepare the rules for boxes in
Subsection 3.1
, and in
Subsection 3.2
and
Subsection 3.3
we prepare the rules for diamonds.
In
Subsection 3.4
we then add the usual rules for the propositional connectives
\lnot
and
\land
to define local tableaux.

3.1.
Boxes

In this section, our goal is to unfold programs in boxes.
We start by sketching our approach.
For a formula
[\alpha]\psi
, where
\alpha
is a complex program, we will define a finite set
\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)
of sequents such that the following holds (cf.
13
):

[\alpha]\psi\equiv\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)\Big\}

(1)

and every formula in any sequent in
\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)
is either equal to
\psi
, or of the form
\lnot\tau
where
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
, or of the form
[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\psi
where
a,\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n}\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
and
a
is atomic. This will be made precise in
7
.

As a corollary of
13
, the following rule will be locally invertible and locally sound:

\Delta,[\alpha]\psi

(\Box)

\big\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)\big\}

Each
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\Box}(\alpha,\psi)
will correspond to a subset
\ell\subseteq\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
, which we will refer to as a test profile. Tests define control flow in the execution of
\alpha
, and
\ell
represents a situation where the tests in
\ell
succeed and the tests not in
\ell
fail.
Given a test profile
\ell
, the corresponding
\Gamma
will contain the formulas of the form
[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\psi
,
where the program list
a\delta_{1}\ldots\delta_{n}\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
is a possible execution of
\alpha
in the situation represented by
\ell
.

We give some further intuitions for the definition of
\mathsf{unfold}_{\Box}(\alpha,\psi)
in terms of the reduction axioms from
Subsection 2.1
. The right-hand side of Equivalence (
1
) is
almost
the disjunctive normal form obtained by rewriting
[\alpha]\psi
exhaustively using the reduction axioms
(\mathrm{Ax}(?))
,
(\mathrm{Ax}(\cup))
,
(\mathrm{Ax}(;))
,
(\mathrm{Ax}(^{*}))
while ensuring that formulas of the form
[\beta^{*}]\varphi

are only rewritten once, and considering test formulas and
\psi
atomic, since these formulas will be reduced recursively by other local rule applications. Multiple disjuncts result from applying the axiom
(\mathrm{Ax}(?))
.
The “almost” refers to two things:
First, each disjunct in the resulting disjunctive normal form corresponds to one or more
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\Box}(\alpha,\psi)
.
1
1

1

This can be seen by considering
[p?;q?]r
for which we obtain a disjunctive normal form
\neg p\lor\neg q\lor\neg r
. The disjunct
\neg p
corresponds to both
\ell=\emptyset
and
\ell=\{q\}
.
Informally, this can be explained as “if the test
p?
fails then the outcome of
q?
does not matter”.

Secondly, as mentioned above, we must also take care to avoid infinite unfolding of formulas of the form
[\beta^{*}]\varphi
(which would cause non-termination). This is done by excluding from
\Gamma
all formula occurrences of the form
[\beta^{*}]\varphi
that were
already reduced. We will explain this mechanism below before
Subsection 3.1
.

First, we define the notion of a test profile
\ell
for
\alpha
and prove a number of useful results.

{defi}

[
TP
]
Let
\alpha
be a program.
A
test profile
for
\alpha
is a subset
\ell\subseteq\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
.
The set of all test profiles of
\alpha
is denoted as
\mathit{TP}(\alpha)
.
Given a profile
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)
, we define its
signature formula

\sigma^{\ell}

as

\sigma^{\ell}\mathrel{:=}\bigwedge\{\pm_{\ell}\tau\mid\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\},

where
\pm_{\ell}\tau
denotes the formula
\tau
if
\tau\in\ell
and
the formula
\lnot\tau
if
\tau\notin\ell
.

In the following two remarks, we gather some basic observations on test profiles and their signature formulas.

Remark 4
.

We will frequently use the following facts:

\text{For all }\alpha\in\mathcal{P}:\quad\top\equiv\bigvee\big\{\sigma^{\ell}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)\big\},

(2)

\text{For all }\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\text{ and all }\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha):\quad\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\text{ implies }\sigma^{\ell}\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}\equiv\bot.

(3)

Note that in Statement (
3
),
the right to left direction does not hold, because
\sigma^{\ell}

does not have to be consistent.
Consider for example
\alpha\mathrel{:=}(p\land q)?;(\lnot p)?;{(\lnot q)?}
and the test profile
\ell\mathrel{:=}\{p\land q\}
.
Then
\sigma^{\ell}=(p\land q)\land\lnot p\land\lnot q
, and therefore
\sigma^{\ell}\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\bot
,
but still
\ell=\ell
.

Moreover, we will use the fact that for all formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
, and all programs
\alpha
:

\varphi\models\psi\text{ \ iff \ }\text{ for all }\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha):\varphi\land\sigma^{\ell}\models\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}.

(4)

We leave the verification of Statements (
2
), (
3
), and (
4
) as an exercise in propositional logic to the reader.

Remark 5
.

Below we will need to relate the test profiles of a complex program to those of its subprograms.
Here the following facts will be useful.

Assume that
\beta\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
.
Every test profile
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)
induces a (possibly smaller) test profile
\ell|_{\beta}\mathrel{:=}\ell\cap\mathit{Tests}(\beta)\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)
, and every test profile in
\mathit{TP}(\beta)
is of this kind.
For the signature formulas, we find for
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)
that

\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\sigma^{\ell|_{\beta}}\land\bigwedge\{\pm_{\ell}\tau\mid\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\setminus\mathit{Tests}(\beta)\}.

From this it follows by propositional logic that:

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{For all $\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)$, all $\beta\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)$, and all formulas $\varphi,\psi$,}\\
\text{if $\varphi\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\beta}}\equiv\psi\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\beta}}$
then $\varphi\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}$.}\end{array}

(5)

Given a formula
[\alpha]\psi
and a test profile
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)
,
we will define a set
B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\psi}
of formulas that suffice to make
[\alpha]\psi
true
when the tests that are not in
\ell
fail and those in
\ell
succeed.
Every formula in
B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\psi}
will be either of the form
\neg\tau
where
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\setminus\ell
or
of the form
[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\psi
for a possibly empty list
a\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}
of subprograms of
\alpha
.
Formulas of the form
[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\psi
will be represented by the list
a\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}
, and we compute them recursively.
The set
P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
will contain all the program lists of possible executions of
\alpha
when precisely the tests in
\ell
succeed.
Moreover, we must avoid infinite unfolding of formulas of the form
[\beta^{*}]\varphi
.
This is taken care of in the definition of
P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
below in the clause for
P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}

where removing the empty list
\varepsilon
from
P^{\ell}{(\beta)}
ensures that
[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi
is only unfolded once.
Put differently, this clause excludes regenerated occurrences of
[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi
from
B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\psi}
.

We now define
P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
and we will provide examples to illustrate
Subsection 3.1
below.

{defi}

[
unfoldBox
]
Let
\alpha_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
be a program and let
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha_{0})
be a test profile.
We define for all
\alpha\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha_{0})
, the set
P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
of lists of programs,
by induction on the shallow program structure of
\alpha
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}P^{\ell}{(a)}&\mathrel{:=}&\{a\}\\
P^{\ell}{(\tau?)}&\mathrel{:=}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\emptyset&\text{ if }\tau\notin\ell\\
\{\varepsilon\}&\text{ if }\tau\in\ell\end{array}\right.\\
P^{\ell}{(\beta\cup\gamma)}&\mathrel{:=}&P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\cup P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}\\
P^{\ell}{(\beta;\gamma)}&\mathrel{:=}&\big\{\vec{\beta}\gamma\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\cup\big\{\vec{\gamma}\mid\vec{\gamma}\in P^{\ell}{(\gamma)},\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\big\}\\
P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\varepsilon\}\cup\big\{\vec{\beta}\beta^{\ast}\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\end{array}

Furthermore, we define the set
F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
of negations of “failed test formulas”

F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\mathrel{:=}\{\lnot\tau\mid\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\text{ and }\tau\notin\ell\}.

Finally, for a formula
\psi\in\mathcal{F}
,
we define

B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\psi}\mathrel{:=}F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\cup\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\},

and

\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)\mathrel{:=}\big\{B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\psi}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)\big\}.

{exa}

Consider the program
\alpha\mathrel{:=}(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}
.
Then
\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)=\{p\}
, and hence there are two test profiles for
\alpha
, namely
\ell_{0}\mathrel{:=}\emptyset
and
\ell_{1}\mathrel{:=}\{p\}
,
corresponding to “
{p?}
fails” and “
{p?}
succeeds”.
For the test profile
\ell_{0}=\emptyset
, we have
F^{\ell_{0}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}=\{\lnot p\}
and:

\begin{array}[]{rl}&P^{\ell_{0}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}\\
=&\big\{\vec{\alpha}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell_{0}}{(a\cup p?)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\cup\big\{\vec{\beta}\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell_{0}}{(b^{\ast})},\varepsilon\in P^{\ell_{0}}{(a\cup p?)}\big\}\\
=&\big\{\vec{\alpha}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\alpha}\in(P^{\ell_{0}}{(a)}\cup P^{\ell_{0}}{(p?)})\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\ \cup\ \big\{\vec{\beta}\mid\vec{\beta}\in(\ldots),\varepsilon\in P^{\ell_{0}}{(a)}\cup P^{\ell_{0}}{(p?)}\big\}\\
=&\big\{\vec{\alpha}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\alpha}\in(\{a\}\cup\emptyset)\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\ \cup\ \big\{\vec{\beta}\mid\vec{\beta}\in(\ldots),\varepsilon\in\{a\}\cup\emptyset\big\}\\
=&\big\{ab^{\ast}\big\}\ \cup\ \emptyset\\
=&\{ab^{\ast}\}.\end{array}

The set
P^{\ell_{0}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}=\{ab^{\ast}\}
can be seen as the possible executions of
(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}
when the test
{p?}
fails.

For the test profile
\ell_{1}=\{p\}
, we get
F^{\ell_{1}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}=\emptyset
and:

\begin{array}[]{rl}&P^{\ell_{1}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}\\
=&\big\{\vec{\alpha}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell_{1}}{(a\cup p?)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\cup\big\{\vec{\beta}\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell_{1}}{(b^{\ast})},\varepsilon\in P^{\ell_{1}}{(a\cup p?)}\big\}\\
=&\big\{\vec{\alpha}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\alpha}\in(P^{\ell_{1}}{(a)}\cup P^{\ell_{1}}{(p?)})\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\\
&\ \cup\ \big\{\vec{\beta}\mid\vec{\beta}\in(\{\varepsilon\}\cup\{\vec{\gamma}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\gamma}\in P^{\ell_{1}}{(b)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\}),\varepsilon\in P^{\ell_{1}}{(a)}\cup P^{\ell_{1}}{(p?)}\big\}\\
=&\big\{\vec{\alpha}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\alpha}\in(\{a\}\cup\{\varepsilon\})\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\\
&\ \cup\ \big\{\vec{\beta}\mid\vec{\beta}\in(\{\varepsilon\}\cup\{\vec{\gamma}b^{\ast}\mid\vec{\gamma}\in\{b\}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\}),\varepsilon\in\{a\}\cup\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\\
=&\big\{ab^{\ast}\big\}\ \cup\ \big\{\vec{\beta}\mid\vec{\beta}\in(\{\varepsilon\}\cup\{bb^{\ast}\})\big\}\\
=&\{ab^{\ast},\varepsilon,bb^{\ast}\}.\end{array}

The set
P^{\ell_{1}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}=\{ab^{\ast},\varepsilon,bb^{\ast}\}
can be seen as the possible executions of
(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}
when the test
{p?}
succeeds.

This can then be used to unfold a formula
[(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}]\psi
, for arbitrary
\psi
, as follows:

B^{\ell_{0}}_{(a\cup p?);b^{\ast},\psi}=F^{\ell_{0}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}\cup\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell_{0}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}\big\}=\{\lnot p,[a][b^{\ast}]\psi\},

B^{\ell_{1}}_{(a\cup p?);b^{\ast},\psi}=F^{\ell_{1}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}\cup\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell_{1}}{((a\cup p?);b^{\ast})}\big\}=\{\psi,[a][b^{\ast}]\psi,[b][b^{\ast}]\psi\}.

Now by
Subsection 3.1
we get:

\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}((a\cup p?);b^{\ast},\psi)=\{\{\lnot p,[a][b^{\ast}]\psi\},\{\psi,[a][b^{\ast}]\psi,[b][b^{\ast}]\psi\}\}.

This means that our
(\Box)
rule will split the formula
[(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}]\psi
into two branches (cf.
Figure 4
), one containing

\{\lnot p,[a][b^{\ast}]\psi\}

and another containing

\{\psi,[a][b^{\ast}]\psi,[b][b^{\ast}]\psi\}
.

Lastly, the forthcoming
13
applied to our example gives us

[(a\cup p?);b^{\ast}]\psi\ \equiv\ (\lnot p\land[a][b^{\ast}]\psi)\lor(\psi\land[a][b^{\ast}]\psi\land[b][b^{\ast}]\psi),

which can also be verified directly.

{exa}

Consider
\alpha\mathrel{:=}{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}
.
As
\alpha
does not contain tests there is only the empty test profile
\ell\mathrel{:=}\emptyset
for
\alpha
.
Now first, we have
F^{\emptyset}{(\alpha)}=\emptyset
.
Second, one easily computes

P^{\emptyset}{(a)}=\{a\}
,

P^{\emptyset}{(a^{\ast})}=\{\varepsilon\}\cup\{aa^{\ast}\}
and

P^{\emptyset}{({(a^{\ast})}^{\ast})}=\{\varepsilon\}\cup\{aa^{\ast}{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}\}
.
Together we thus have
\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}({(a^{\ast})}^{\ast},\psi)=\{\psi,[a][a^{\ast}][{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]\psi\}
.
Compare this to the left side of
Figure 5
.

In the following
6
, we gather some syntactic information about
P^{\ell}
and the
\mathsf{unfold}_{\Box}
operation.
Intuitively, we will use this to show that programs without
\ast
become simpler when unfolded, and that will be useful to show termination of our proof system.

Lemma 6

(
boxHelperTermination
✓)
.

Let
\alpha
be a program, let
\ell
be a test profile for
\alpha
, and let
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
.
Then we have:

(1)

If
\alpha=a
for some atomic
a
, then
\vec{\delta}=a
.

(2)

If
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
for some
\beta
, then either
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
or
\vec{\delta}
is of the form
\vec{\delta}=a\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n}\beta^{\ast}
, where
n\geq 0
,
a\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
is atomic and
\delta_{i}\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)\setminus\{\alpha\}
, for each
i
.

(3)

Otherwise, either
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
or
\vec{\delta}
is of the form
\vec{\delta}=a\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n}
, where
n\geq 0
,
a
is atomic and
\delta_{i}\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)\setminus\{\alpha\}
, for each
i
.

We note that case (c) applies if
\alpha
is a test, or a choice or composition of simpler programs.

Proof 3.1
.

Follows directly from
Subsection 3.1
of
P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
.
We omit the details.

From
6
we also get the following two results which we will use to prove termination of local tableaux.

Lemma 7

(
unfoldBoxContent
✓)
.

Let
\alpha
be a program and let
\psi
and
\varphi
be formulas such that
\varphi\in\bigcup\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)
.
Then we have
\varphi\in\mathit{FL}([\alpha]\psi)
.
Furthermore, we find that
\varphi=\psi
,
or
\varphi
is of the form
\varphi=\lnot\tau
with
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
,
or
\varphi
is of the form
\varphi=[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\psi

with
a,\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n}\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
and
n\geq 0
.

Proof 3.2
.

Take any
\varphi\in\bigcup\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\psi)
.
To show
\varphi\in\mathit{FL}([\alpha]\psi)
we distinguish two cases.
Either
\varphi
comes from the function
P^{\ell}
or from
F^{\ell}{(\cdot)}
in
Subsection 3.1
.
If it comes from
P^{\ell}
then it can be obtained using the three cases given in
Subsection 2.1
and closure under subformulas.
If it comes from
F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
, then it is a subformula of
[\alpha]\psi
.
In both cases it must be in
\mathit{FL}([\alpha]\psi)
.

For the remaining claim, distinguish the same cases.
If
\varphi
comes from
P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
, then we apply
6
and note that in each of the three cases (a) to (c) the claim follows.
If
\varphi
comes from
F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
, then by definition of
F
we must have
\varphi=\lnot\tau
for some
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
.

Lemma 8

(
PgoesDown
✓)
.

For a program
\alpha
, let
|\alpha|
denote its length (
lengthOfProgram
).
Let
\alpha
be a program,
let
\ell
be a test profile for
\alpha
,
let
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
,
and let
\gamma\in\vec{\delta}
.
If
\alpha=a
for some atomic program
a
, then
\gamma=a
.
If
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
for some program
\beta
, then
|\gamma|\leq|\alpha|
.
In all other cases we have
|\gamma|<|\alpha|
.

Proof 3.3
.

Take any
\alpha
and
\ell
,
let
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
,
and let
\gamma\in\vec{\delta}
.
Now distinguish the three cases.

•

If
\alpha=a
for some
a
, then by
6
(a) we have
\vec{\delta}=a
and thus must have
\gamma=a
.

•

If
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
for some
\beta
, then by
6
(b) we have
\gamma\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
and thus
|\gamma|\leq|\alpha|
.

•

In any other case by
6
(c) we have
\gamma\in\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)\setminus\{\ \alpha\}
and thus
|\gamma|<|\alpha|
.

The following technical lemma will be needed in the proof of
13
below.

Lemma 9

(
boxHelperTP
✓)
.

Let
\alpha
be a program, and let
\ell
be a test profile for
\alpha
.
For all formulas
\psi
, we have:

\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}.

Proof 3.4
.

By
Subsection 3.1
, we have

B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\psi}\mathrel{:=}F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\cup\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}
, so left-to-right is immediate.
For right-to-left, we also use that
\sigma^{\ell}\to F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
is valid.

A crucial part of how we defined
P^{\ell}
in
Subsection 3.1
is the use of the empty sequence
\varepsilon
while unfolding tests, sequential compositions and iteration programs.

Readers familiar with
\mu
-calculus may want to know that these conditions allow our approach to deal with unguardedness in PDL formulas and programs.
We discuss this in
\mu
-calculus terms in
10
and then return to PDL in
11
below.

Remark 10
.

Using notation from
\mu
-calculus we note that

[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\equiv\nu x.\psi\land[\beta]x

where
x
is a fresh proposition letter not occurring in either
\psi
or
\beta
.
From this it follows that

[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\equiv\nu x.\psi\land\varphi(x)

for any formula
\varphi
such that
[\beta]x\equiv\varphi(x)
.
Now suppose that
\varphi^{\prime}
is a formula we obtain from
\varphi
by replacing any number of
unguarded
occurrences of
x
with
\top
.
Then it follows from the general theory of fixpoint logics that

\nu x.\psi\land\varphi^{\prime}(x)\equiv\nu x.\psi\land\varphi(x).

In particular, if
\varphi(x)
is of the form
\varphi=(x\land\chi_{0})\lor\chi_{1}
(where
x
may or may not have occurrences in
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
), we find that

[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\equiv\nu x.\psi\land(\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1}).

From this it follows that, in order to show that
\rho\models[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
, for some formula
\rho
, it suffices to show that
\rho\models\psi
and that
\rho\models\ (\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
.
We spell this out in the lemma below, and we also supply a direct proof.

Lemma 11

(
guardToStar
✓)
.

Let
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
be formulas, let
\beta
be a program, and let
x
be a proposition letter that does not occur in
\beta
(but
x
may occur in
\chi_{0}
and in
\chi_{1}
) such that
[\beta]x\equiv(x\land\chi_{0})\lor\chi_{1}
.
Then, for all formulas
\rho
and
\psi
,
if
\rho\models(\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
and
\rho\models\psi

then
\rho\models[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
.

Proof 3.5
.

The key observation in this proof is the following:

if
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\rho
and
(w,v)\in R_{\beta}
then

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\rho
.

(6)

To see why this is the case, assume that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\rho
.
Then by assumption we have
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash(\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
,
and since obviously the formula
x\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle=\rho
holds at
w
, it follows that

\mathcal{M},w\Vdash((x\land\chi_{0})\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
.
Now by the two assumptions we have
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash([\beta]x)\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
, which just
means that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash[\beta]\rho
.
It is then immediate by
(w,v)\in R_{\beta}
that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\rho
.
This finishes the proof of Statement (
6
).

To see how the lemma follows from this, let
\mathcal{M},w
be a pointed model such
that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\rho
.
Take an arbitrary state
v
such that
(w,v)\in R_{\beta^{\ast}}
, then there is
a finite sequence of states that forms a path of
\beta
-steps from
w
to
v
.
Now by induction on the length of this path we can apply Statement (
6
) to each of the
R_{\beta}
steps.
We find that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\rho
, so that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\psi
by assumption.
Hence
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
.

Before the main lemma of this section, we prove a technical helper lemma that relates test profiles to the set
X
.

Lemma 12
.

Let
\gamma
be a program, and let
\psi
be a formula.
Then the following hold:

(a)

If
[\gamma]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
, then
[\gamma]\psi\equiv\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)\Big\}
;

(b)

If
[\gamma]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\models\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
, then
[\gamma]\psi\models\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)\Big\}
.

Note that part (b) is almost the same as part (a), only that
\equiv
is replaced by
\models
in two places.

Proof 3.6
.

First, we show part (a):

\displaystyle[\gamma]\psi

\displaystyle\equiv[\gamma]\psi\land\top

by propositional logic

\displaystyle\equiv[\gamma]\psi\land\bigvee\big\{\sigma^{\ell}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)\big\}

by Statement (
2
)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{[\gamma]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)\big\}

by propositional logic

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)\big\}

by assumption

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)\big\}

\displaystyle\text{(see below)}.

For the last step, the downward implication is trivial.
For the other direction, let
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
be such that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}
.
Let
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
be the test profile of
w
; that is,
\ell^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}\{\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\gamma)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\tau\}
.
Then by definition we have
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}
.

We claim that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\gamma,\psi}
.
By definition of
\ell^{\prime}
it is clear that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\gamma)}
, so it is left to show that

\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\textstyle{\bigwedge}\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\gamma)}\}
.
Note that from
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}
we may infer that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\textstyle{\bigwedge}\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}\}
, so that it suffices to show
P^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\gamma)}\subseteq P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
.
By
Subsection 3.1
it now suffices to show
\ell^{\prime}\subseteq\ell
.
To show this, take any
\tau\notin\ell
.
Note that by assumption we also have
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
,
hence
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\tau
and thus by definition of
\ell^{\prime}
also
\tau\notin\ell^{\prime}
, so we are done.

To show part (b), note that the same proof works if we replace the fourth
\equiv
with a
\models
.

Lemma 13

(
localBoxTruth
✓)
.

For every program
\gamma
and every formula
\psi
we have

[\gamma]\psi\equiv\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)\Big\}.

Proof 3.7
.

By
12
(a) it suffices to show that for all programs
\gamma
,
for all formulas
\psi
and all test profiles
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
we have

[\gamma]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}.

(7)

We proceed to show Claim (
7
) by induction on the shallow program structure of
\gamma
.

Base Case.

Case
\gamma=a
:

In this case, the only test profile is
\ell=\emptyset
, and for this
\ell
we find
that
B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}=\emptyset\cup\{\Box(a,\psi)\}
, so that

\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\equiv[a]\psi
as required.

Case
\gamma={\tau?}
:

Here we make a case distinction.
If
\tau\notin\ell
then
B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}=\{\lnot\tau\}
, so that

\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\lnot\tau\land\pm_{\ell}\tau\equiv\lnot\tau\equiv[\tau?]\psi\land\lnot\tau
.
On the other hand, if
\tau\in\ell
, then

B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}=\emptyset\cup\{\Box(\varepsilon,\psi)\}=\{\psi\}
,
so that

\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\psi\land\pm_{\ell}\tau\equiv\psi\land\tau\equiv[\tau?]\psi\land\tau
.

Induction step.

Here we make the usual case distinction.

Case
\gamma=\alpha\cup\beta
:

Let
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
. By the induction hypothesis and
9
, we have that

\begin{array}[]{llll}[\alpha]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\alpha}}&\equiv&\displaystyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell|_{\alpha}}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\alpha}},\text{ and}\\
{[\beta]}\psi\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\beta}}&\equiv&\displaystyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell|_{\beta}}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\beta}}.\end{array}

It then follows from Statement (
5
) that

\begin{array}[]{llll}[\alpha]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}&\equiv&\displaystyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell|_{\alpha}}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell},\text{ and}\\
{[\beta]}\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}&\equiv&\displaystyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell|_{\beta}}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}.\end{array}

Furthermore, since
P^{\ell|_{\alpha}}{(\alpha)}=P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
and
P^{\ell|_{\beta}}{(\beta)}=P^{\ell}{(\beta)}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{llll}[\alpha]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}&\equiv&\displaystyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell},\text{ and}\\
{[\beta]}\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}&\equiv&\displaystyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}.\end{array}

On the basis of this, we calculate as follows:

\displaystyle[\gamma]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle=[\alpha\cup\beta]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv([\alpha]\psi\land[\beta]\psi)\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv\big([\alpha]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\big)\land\big([\beta]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\Big(\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)\land\Big(\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\cup P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}\hskip 184.9429pt\text{by \autoref{l:boxHelperTP}.}

Case
\gamma=\alpha;\beta
:

Let
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
. By the induction hypothesis and
9
, we have that

\begin{array}[]{lll}[\alpha][\beta]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\alpha}}&\equiv&\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},[\beta]\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell|_{\alpha}}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\alpha}},\text{ and}\\
{[\beta]}\psi\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\beta}}&\equiv&\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell|_{\beta}}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell|_{\beta}}.\end{array}

Using again Statement (
5
) and
P^{\ell|_{\alpha}}{(\alpha)}=P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
and
P^{\ell|_{\beta}}{(\beta)}=P^{\ell}{(\beta)}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lll}[\alpha][\beta]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}&\equiv&\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},[\beta]\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell},\text{ and}\\
{[\beta]}\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}&\equiv&\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}.\end{array}

We now argue as follows:

\displaystyle[\gamma]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv[\alpha][\beta]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},[\beta]\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}

as argued above

\displaystyle\equiv\Big(\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},[\beta]\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\land\Big(\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\varepsilon,[\beta]\psi)\mid\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle\equiv\Big(\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta}\beta,\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\land\Big(\bigwedge\big\{[\beta]\psi\mid\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle\equiv\Big(\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta}\beta,\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\land\Big(\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)},\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

as argued above

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\eta},\psi)\mid\vec{\eta}\in P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell}

by definition of
P^{\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\gamma,\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}

by
9
.

Case
\gamma=\beta^{\ast}
:

By the induction hypothesis for
\beta
we have:

[\beta]\varphi\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\beta,\varphi}\land\sigma^{\ell},\text{for all $\varphi$ and all $\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)$},

(8)

and, as we saw before, by
12
we obtain the following equivalence, for all
\varphi
:

{[\beta]}\varphi\equiv\bigvee\big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\beta,\varphi}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)\big\}.

(9)

We now have to show that Claim (
7
) holds for
\gamma=\beta^{*}
.
Note that
\mathit{TP}(\beta^{\ast})=\mathit{TP}(\beta)
, so we fix any test profile
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)
and any formula
\psi
.
We claim that

[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\equiv\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}.

(10)

For the implication from left to right of Claim (
10
), by unfolding the definition of
B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}
we only need to show that

[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\models\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}\big\}
.
Take an arbitrary program list
\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}
.
If
\vec{\alpha}=\varepsilon
then
\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)=\psi
,
and so we have
[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\models\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)
.
Otherwise
\vec{\alpha}
by
6
(b) must be of the form
\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}
, for some
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
in
P^{\ell}{(\beta)}
.
In this case, we apply Statement (
8
) where we take
[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
as the formula
\varphi
, and obtain, for all
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)
:

\displaystyle[\beta][\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\equiv\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\beta,[\beta^{\ast}]\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\beta)}\land\textstyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\beta},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\models\textstyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\beta},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}.

Here the last step follows because
\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}
implies
\bigwedge F^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\beta)}
.

Now:

\displaystyle[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\models([\beta][\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\top)\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv([\beta][\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\bigvee_{\ell^{\prime}}\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}})\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\models\bigvee_{\ell^{\prime}}([\beta][\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}})\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\models\bigvee_{\ell^{\prime}}(\textstyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\beta},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}})\land\sigma^{\ell}

\displaystyle\models\bigvee_{\ell^{\prime}}\Big(\textstyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\beta},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\beta)}\big\}\land\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}\land\sigma^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle\models\textstyle{\bigwedge}\big\{\Box(\vec{\beta},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid\vec{\beta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\big\}

\displaystyle\models\Box(\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast},\psi)

where the second step uses Statement (
2
), the third step uses distributivity, and the penultimate step uses Statement (
3
).

This finishes the proof that
[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}\models\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}\big\}
, and hence the direction from left to right of Claim (
10
).

For the implication from right to left of Claim (
10
) more work is required.
Let
\rho
be the formula

\rho\mathrel{:=}\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}\mid\ell^{\prime}\in\mathit{TP}(\beta^{\ast})\Big\}.

Our key claim is that

\rho\models[\beta^{\ast}]\psi.

(11)

To see why this suffices to prove the right-to-left implication of Claim (
10
), observe that we have
\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}\models\rho
by definition of
\rho
.
But then by Claim (
11
) we obtain
\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}\models[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
