, and from this and Statement (
4
) we arrive at the required
\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}\land\sigma^{\ell}\models[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\land\sigma^{\ell}
.

Our aim then is to show
\rho\models[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
by applying
11
.
For this, let
x
be a fresh proposition letter that does not occur in
\beta
.
We now need to find suitable formulas
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
such that

[\beta]x\equiv(x\land\chi_{0})\lor\chi_{1}
.
From Equivalence (
9
) we have

\displaystyle{[\beta]}x

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}B^{\ell}_{\beta,x}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)\big\}.

For any
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)
we will write

\varphi_{\ell}\mathrel{:=}\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},x)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\big\},

so that by definition of
B^{\ell}_{\beta,x}
we find

\displaystyle{[\beta]}x

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land\varphi_{\ell}\mid\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)\big\}.

Now partition the set
\mathit{TP}(\beta)
into
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}T_{0}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)\mid\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\},\text{ and}\\
T_{1}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta)\mid\varepsilon\not\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\},\end{array}

so that we have
\mathit{TP}(\beta)=T_{0}\cup T_{1}
and
T_{0}\cap T_{1}=\emptyset
.

Clearly, this gives

{[\beta]}x\equiv\bigvee_{\ell\in T_{0}}({\textstyle{\bigwedge}}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land\varphi_{\ell})\lor\bigvee_{\ell\in T_{1}}(\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land\varphi_{\ell}).

Now abbreviate

\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell}\mathrel{:=}\bigwedge\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},x)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\},

then we have

\varphi_{\ell}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x\land\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell}&\text{if }\ell\in T_{0}\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

so that using distributivity we obtain

\displaystyle{[\beta]}x

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee_{\ell\in T_{0}}({\textstyle{\bigwedge}}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land x\land\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell})\lor\bigvee_{\ell\in T_{1}}(\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell})

\displaystyle\equiv\big(x\land\bigvee_{\ell\in T_{0}}({\textstyle{\bigwedge}}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell})\big)\lor\bigvee_{\ell\in T_{1}}(\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell}).

Hence, if we define, for
i\in\{0,1\}
:

\chi_{i}\mathrel{:=}\bigvee_{\ell\in T_{i}}(\textstyle{\bigwedge}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell}),

then we obtain

[\beta]x\equiv(x\land\chi_{0})\lor\chi_{1},

(12)

as required.

It is left to verify that with this definition of
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
, the other conditions for
11
are also satisfied.
For starters, it is easy to check that

\rho\models\psi.

(13)

To see this, recall that
\varepsilon
belongs to the set
P^{\ell^{\prime}}{(\beta^{*})}
, for every
\ell^{\prime}
.
It follows that the formula
\Box(\varepsilon,\psi)=\psi
belongs to
B^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}
, for every
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathit{TP}(\beta^{*})
, and from this Statement (
13
) is immediate by definition of
\rho
.

The key claim then is the following:

\rho\models(\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle.

(14)

To prove this, by the definition of
\rho
it suffices to show

\bigwedge B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}\models(\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle,

for every test profile
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta^{\ast})
.
Fix such an
\ell
, then we find

\displaystyle{\textstyle{\bigwedge}}B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\psi}

\displaystyle={\textstyle{\bigwedge}}F^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}\land{\textstyle{\bigwedge}}\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}\big\}

\displaystyle\models{\textstyle{\bigwedge}}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land{\textstyle{\bigwedge}}\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha}\beta^{\ast},\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ by def.~of $F$ and $P^{\ell}$}

\displaystyle={\textstyle{\bigwedge}}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land{\textstyle{\bigwedge}}\big\{\Box(\vec{\alpha},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid\vec{\alpha}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\big\}

\displaystyle={\textstyle{\bigwedge}}F^{\ell}{(\beta)}\land(\varphi^{\prime}_{\ell}\langle x\mapsto[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\rangle)

\displaystyle\models(\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\rangle

\displaystyle\models(\chi_{0}\lor\chi_{1})\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle

Notice that the last step follows from the left-to-right direction of Claim (
10
) (shown before) and
12
(b), which together imply
[\beta^{*}]\psi\models\rho
.

With Statements (
12
), and (
13
), and Claim (
14
) it follows immediately from
11
that
\rho\models[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
, as required.

With
13
we reached the main goal of this section: local soundness and local invertibility of the
(\Box)
rule which we will define in the next section.
We now provide one more lemma about boxes that will be needed in the proof of the Truth Lemma (
38
).
Informally,
14
says the following: no matter how a Kripke model executes a program, the definitions of
F
and
P
will provide a list of failed tests and a list of programs that match the program execution in the model.

Lemma 14

(
existsBoxFP
✓)
.

Let
\gamma
be a program, let
\mathcal{M}=(W,R,V)
be a Kripke model, and let
v
and
w
be states in
\mathcal{M}
such that
(v,w)\in R_{\gamma}
.
Furthermore, let
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\gamma)
be such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
.
Then we find
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
for some
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
.

Proof 3.8
.

We prove the lemma by induction on the shallow program structure of
\gamma
.

Base case.

Case
\gamma=a
:

Suppose
(v,w)\in R_{a}
.
Note that
P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}=\{a\}
for any
\ell
.
Hence we can choose
\vec{\delta}\mathrel{:=}a
to get
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
and are done.

Case
\gamma={\tau?}
:

Here
(v,w)\in R_{\gamma}
implies that
v=w
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\tau
.
Now assume that
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\tau?)
is such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\tau?)}
.
It cannot be the case that
\tau\notin\ell
since then
F^{\ell}{(\tau?)}=\{\neg\tau\}
would imply
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\lnot\tau
.
This means that
\tau\in\ell
and we are fine since now
\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\tau?)}
and
(v,w)\in R_{\varepsilon}
.

Induction step.

Case
\gamma=\alpha\cup\beta
:

If
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
then since
F^{\ell}{(\gamma)}=F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\cup F^{\ell}{(\beta)}
, obviously
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\beta)}
.
Furthermore,
(v,w)\in R_{\gamma}
implies that
(v,w)\in R_{\alpha}
or
(v,w)\in R_{\beta}
; without loss of generality, we may assume the first.
Then by the induction hypothesis on
\alpha
we may infer that
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
for some
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
.
Clearly, then by the definition of
P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
this
\vec{\delta}
also belongs
to
P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
.

Case
\gamma=\alpha;\beta
:

Let
u
be such that
(v,u)\in R_{\alpha}
and
(u,w)\in R_{\beta}
.
By definition of
F^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
we have
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
, so that by the induction hypothesis on
\alpha
we find

(v,u)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
for some
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
.
We make a further case distinction.

If
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
then we have
v=u
and so
(v,w)\in R_{\beta}
.
Furthermore, by definition of
F^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
we have
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\beta)}
.
Hence, by induction hypothesis on
\beta
we find
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\eta}}
,
for some
\vec{\eta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}
.
It follows that
\vec{\eta}\in P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
, and so we are done.

If
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
, then we have
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}\mathop{;}R_{\beta}=R_{\vec{\delta}\beta}
, and this suffices since

\vec{\delta}\beta\in P^{\ell}{(\gamma)}
.

Case
\gamma=\beta^{\ast}
:

Since
(v,w)\in R_{\beta^{\ast}}
we can make the following case
distinction.

If
v=w
then we have
(v,w)\in R_{\varepsilon}
and we are done since

\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}
.

If
v\neq w
then there is some
u\neq v
such that
(v,u)\in R_{\beta}
and

(u,w)\in R_{\beta^{\ast}}
.
Now let
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta^{\ast})
be such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}
.
Since
F^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}=F^{\ell}{(\beta)}
this implies, by the induction hypothesis on
\beta
, that
(v,u)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
, for some

\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}
.
Note that
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
since
v\neq u
.
It then follows that
\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}
and so
we are done since
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}\mathop{;}R_{\beta^{\ast}}=R_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}
.

3.2.
Diamonds

Analogously to the previous section about boxes, in this section our goal is to locally unfold programs in “diamonds”.
That is, given a formula
\lnot[\alpha]\psi
, we will define a finite set
\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
of sequents such that

\neg[\alpha]\psi\equiv\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)\Big\},

and for every formula
\varphi
in
\bigcup\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
, we have either
\varphi\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
or
\varphi
is of the form
\lnot[a][\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]\psi
, where the atomic program
a
and all
\alpha_{i}
are in
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
.

As a corollary, the following rule will be locally invertible and locally sound:

\Delta,\lnot[\alpha]\varphi

(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})

\big\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)\big\}

We again give some intuitions with respect to the well-known reduction axioms from
Subsection 2.1
.
Each
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
corresponds to a disjunct in the disjunctive normal form obtained by rewriting

\neg[\alpha]\psi
exhaustively using the reduction axioms
(\mathrm{Ax}(?))
,
(\mathrm{Ax}(\cup))
,
(\mathrm{Ax}(;))
,
(\mathrm{Ax}(^{*}))
while ensuring
that formulas of the form
[\beta^{*}]\varphi
are only rewritten once and treating test formulas and
\psi
as atoms.
To prevent infinite local unfolding, disjuncts containing a formula of the form
\neg[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi
that has been reduced before will not be represented by any
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
.
Inspecting the axioms, we see that multiple disjuncts in the normal form arise from applications of
(\mathrm{Ax}(\cup))
and
(\mathrm{Ax}(^{*}))
,
and that every
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
will consist of exactly one formula of the form

\lnot[a][\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]\psi
(for a possibly empty list
a\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}
) together with the test formulas (zero or more) that must be true for
a\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}
to be a successful execution of
\alpha
.
We can therefore represent such a sequent as a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})

where
X\subseteq\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
and
\vec{\delta}
is a list of programs from
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
.
We compute the set
D_{\alpha}
of these pairs recursively using the function
D
defined below.
Note that the clause for
D_{\alpha;\beta}
ensures that
\beta
is reduced iff
\alpha
does not entail executing an atomic program.
Note also that in the clause for
D_{\alpha^{\ast}}
, the requirement
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
corresponds to the exclusion
of sequents that contain a regenerated formula of the form
\neg[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi
.

{defi}

[
Dset
]
For all programs
\alpha
, we recursively define
D_{\alpha}
as a set of pairs
(X,\vec{\delta})

where
X\subseteq\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
and
\vec{\delta}
is a list of programs from
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}D_{a}&\mathrel{:=}&\big\{(\emptyset,a)\big\}\\
D_{\tau?}&\mathrel{:=}&\big\{(\{\tau\},\varepsilon)\big\}\\
D_{\alpha\cup\beta}&\mathrel{:=}&D_{\alpha}\cup D_{\beta}\\
D_{\alpha;\beta}&\mathrel{:=}&\big\{(X,\vec{\delta}\beta)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}\\
&&\cup\big\{(X\cup Y,\vec{\delta})\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{\alpha},(Y,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}\big\}\\
D_{\alpha^{\ast}}&\mathrel{:=}&\big\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\big\}\cup\big\{(X,\vec{\delta}\alpha^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}\end{array}

{defi}

[
unfoldDiamond
]
For all programs
\alpha
and all formulas
\psi
, we define:

\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)\mathrel{:=}\{X\cup\{\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\}\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}\}

We illustrate the definition of
D
and
\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}
with a few examples.

{exa}

To illustrate the exclusion of sequents to prevent infinite unfolding via the requirement
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
,
consider the program
{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}
.
Note that we have
D_{a^{\ast}}=\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon),(\emptyset,aa^{*})\}
and therefore we get

\begin{array}[]{rcl}D_{{(a^{*})}^{*}}&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{a^{*}},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon),(\emptyset,aa^{*})\},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in\{(\emptyset,aa^{*})\}\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon),(\emptyset,aa^{*}{(a^{*})}^{*})\}\end{array}

and hence for all
\psi
,
\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}({(a^{\ast})}^{\ast},\psi)=\{\{\neg\psi\},\{\neg[a][a^{\ast}][{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]\psi\}\}
, cf.
Figure 5
.

{exa}

To illustrate how the clause for
D_{\alpha;\beta}
reduces the program
\beta
if and only if
\alpha
does not entail the execution of an atomic program, consider the program
(p?\cup a);b^{\ast}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}D_{(p?\cup a);b^{\ast}}&=&\{(X,\vec{\delta}b^{*})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{p?\cup a},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}\\
&&\cup\;\;\{(X\cup Y,\vec{\delta})\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{p?\cup a},(Y,\vec{\delta})\in D_{b^{*}}\}\\
&=&\{(X,\vec{\delta}b^{*})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in\{(\{p\},\varepsilon),(\emptyset,a)\},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}\\
&&\cup\;\;\{(X\cup Y,\vec{\delta})\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in\{(\{p\},\varepsilon),(\emptyset,a)\},(Y,\vec{\delta})\in\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon),(\emptyset,bb^{*})\}\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,ab^{*})\}\cup\{(\{p\},\varepsilon),(\{p\},bb^{*})\}\end{array}

hence for all
\psi
,
\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}((p?\cup a);b^{\ast},\psi)=\{\{\neg[a][b^{*}]\psi\},\{p,\neg\psi\},\{p,\neg[b][b^{*}]\psi\}\}
.

{exa}

We illustrate with the program
{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast}
how nesting of
{?}
and
∗
leads to exclusion of a sequent via the requirement
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}D_{{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast}}&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{(p?\cup a)},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{p?}\cup D_{a},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in\{(\{p\},\varepsilon),(\emptyset,a)\},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})=(\emptyset,a)\}\\
&=&\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon),(\emptyset,a{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast})\}\end{array}

17
below, applied to
\neg[{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast}]\psi
, will then tell us that for all
\psi
:

\lnot[{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast}]\psi\ \equiv\ \lnot\psi\lor\lnot[a][{(p?\cup a)}^{\ast}]\psi.

We also note the following fact about
Subsection 3.2
that will later be useful to show the termination of our system and interpolation.

Fact 15
.

For all
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
,
if
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
, then the first element of
\vec{\delta}
is an atomic program.

The following lemma will also be used to show termination of our system.

Lemma 16

(
unfoldDiamond_in_FL
✓)
.

Let
\alpha
be a program and let
\psi
and
\varphi
be formulas such that
\varphi\in\bigcup\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
.
Then we have
\varphi\in\mathit{FL}(\lnot[\alpha]\psi)
(as in
Subsection 2.1
).

Proof 3.9
.

By inspection of
Subsection 3.2
and
Subsection 3.2
.

Later we will use
Subsection 3.2
only for non-atomic programs to ensure termination of our tableau system, but the following
17
also holds for atomic programs.

Lemma 17

(
localDiamondTruth
✓)
.

For all programs
\alpha
and all formulas
\psi
, we have:

\neg[\alpha]\psi\equiv\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}\Big\}.

We will prove
17
by induction on the shallow program structure of
\alpha
.
In the inductive case where
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
we shall need the following lemma.

Lemma 18

(
guardToStarDiamond
✓)
.

Suppose we have two formulas
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
, a program
\beta
and a proposition letter
x
that does not occur in
\beta
(but may occur in
\chi_{0}
and in
\chi_{1}
), such that
\lnot[\beta]\lnot x\equiv(x\land\chi_{0})\lor\chi_{1}
.
Then for any two formulas
\rho
and
\psi
such that
\chi_{1}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle\models\rho
and
\lnot\psi\models\rho
we have
\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\models\rho
.

Proof 3.10
.

Assume that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
, then we need to show that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\rho
.
Note that since
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
there is a
\beta
-path from
w
to some state where
\psi
does not hold, and hence, by assumption,
\rho
does hold.
Therefore, it suffices to prove the following claim.

\text{For all states $u,v$ in $\mathcal{M}$, if }\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\rho\text{ and }(u,v)\in R_{\beta}\text{ then }\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\rho.

(15)

To prove this, assume
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\rho
and
(u,v)\in R_{\beta}
.
It follows that
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\neg[\beta]\neg\rho
, and so by the assumption on the
\chi
-formulas we find that
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash(\rho\land\chi_{0}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle)\lor\chi_{1}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
.

This means that we either have
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\rho\land\chi_{0}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
, in which case we find
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\rho
immediately, or
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\chi_{1}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle
, in which case we find
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\rho
by the assumption that
\chi_{1}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle\models\rho
.
This shows that in both cases we have
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\rho
, as required.

We will also need the following fact, which can easily be verified given
Subsection 3.2
of
D_{\alpha}
above.

Fact 19

(
keepFreshDset
✓)
.

For any proposition letter
x
and program
\alpha
, if
x
does not occur in
\alpha
then
x
also does not occur in
D_{\alpha}
in the sense that for all
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
, for all
\varphi\in X
and all
\beta\in\vec{\delta}
,
x
occurs in neither
\varphi
nor
\beta
.

Proof 3.11

(Proof of
17
)
.

By induction on the shallow program structure of
\alpha
.

Base Case.

Case
\alpha=a
:

In this case we find
\lnot[a]\psi\equiv\top\land\lnot[a]\psi\equiv\bigwedge\emptyset\land\lnot\Box(a,\psi)
, as required.

Case
\alpha={\tau?}
:

Here we obtain
\lnot[\tau?]\psi\equiv\tau\land\lnot\psi\equiv\tau\land\neg\Box(\varepsilon,\psi)
.

Induction hypothesis:
Suppose that for two programs
\beta
and
\gamma
we have, for all formulas
\psi
:

\displaystyle\neg[\beta]\psi\equiv\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}\Big\},

(IH
\beta
)

\displaystyle\neg[\gamma]\psi\equiv\bigvee\Big\{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\gamma}\Big\}.

(IH
\gamma
)

Induction step.

Case
\alpha=\beta\cup\gamma
:

First we use that
D_{\beta\cup\gamma}=D_{\beta}\cup D_{\gamma}
, then we can rewrite the disjuncts using the IH for
\beta
and
\gamma
, so we are done.

Case
\alpha=\beta;\gamma
:

This case follows from the following calculation:

\displaystyle\lnot[\beta;\gamma]\psi

\displaystyle\equiv\lnot[\beta][\gamma]\psi

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},[\gamma]\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}\big\}

by (IH
\beta
)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},[\gamma]\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\lor\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\varepsilon,[\gamma]\psi)\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta}\big\}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta}\gamma,\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\lor\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot[\gamma]\psi\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta}\big\}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta}\gamma,\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\lor\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land{\textstyle{\bigwedge}Y}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\lambda},\psi)\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta},(Y,\vec{\lambda})\in D_{\gamma}\big\}

by (IH
\gamma
)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}Z}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\eta},\psi)\mid(Z,\vec{\eta})\in D_{\alpha}\big\}.

Case
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
:

Define

\rho\mathrel{:=}\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta^{*}}\big\}.

Then our goal will be to show that
\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\equiv\rho
.

We first show that
\rho\models\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
as we will use it for the other direction.
For this it suffices to show that each of the disjuncts of
\rho
implies
\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
, so take an arbitrary pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
.
In case
(X,\vec{\delta})=(\emptyset,\varepsilon)
we find

\bigwedge X\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)\equiv\top\land\neg\psi
, and this formula obviously implies
\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi
.
If
(X,\vec{\delta})\neq(\emptyset,\varepsilon)
, then
\vec{\delta}
by definition of
D_{\beta^{\ast}}
is of the form
\vec{\gamma}\beta^{\ast}
with
\vec{\gamma}\neq\varepsilon
and
(X,\vec{\gamma})\in D_{\beta}
.
In this case we find

\displaystyle{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\psi)

\displaystyle={\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\gamma},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)

\displaystyle\models\neg[\beta][\beta^{\ast}]\psi

by (IH
\beta
)

\displaystyle\models\neg[\beta^{\ast}]\psi.

In order to prove that
\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi\models\rho
we will apply
18
.
For this, let
x
be a fresh proposition letter not occurring in
\beta
and therefore, by
19
, also not occurring in
D_{\beta}
.
Define

\begin{array}[]{lll}\varphi_{0}&\mathrel{:=}&\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta}\big\},\text{ and}\\
\varphi_{1}&\mathrel{:=}&\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\neg x)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}.\end{array}

It now suffices to show that these formulas meet the conditions of
18
.

First, note that
\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}
are based on disjoint subsets of
D_{\beta}
for the empty and non-empty program sequences, respectively.
This is similar to
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
in the proof of
13
.
It follows from (IH
\beta
) and distributivity that
\lnot[\beta]\lnot x\equiv(x\land\varphi_{0})\lor\varphi_{1}
.
Second, we have

\lnot\psi=\lnot\Box(\varepsilon,\psi)\equiv\bigwedge\emptyset\land\lnot\Box(\varepsilon,\psi)\models\rho

because
(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
.
It thus remains to show the last condition of
18
, namely that
\varphi_{1}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle\models\rho
; this we prove as follows, where the first equality holds because
x
does not occur in
D_{\beta}
.

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\langle x\mapsto\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\neg\rho)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}

\displaystyle\models\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\neg\neg[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}

by
\rho\models\lnot[\beta^{\ast}]\psi

\displaystyle\models\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},[\beta^{\ast}]\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}

\displaystyle=\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast},\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\big\}

\displaystyle\models\bigvee\big\{{\textstyle{\bigwedge}X}\land\lnot\Box(\vec{\eta},\psi)\mid(X,\vec{\eta})\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}\big\}

by def. of
D_{\beta^{\ast}}

\displaystyle=\rho.

This finishes the main result of this section.
Only later (for
36
) will we need the following lemma.
As it is a simple result about diamonds and our
D
function, we include it here.
Note that we use
R_{\vec{\delta}}
from
Subsection 2.2
.
Informally,
20
is the diamond analogue of
14
for boxes.
It says that no matter how a Kripke model executes a program, some element of
D_{\gamma}
will match the program execution in the model.

Lemma 20

(
existsDiamondDset
✓)
.

Let
\gamma
be a program, let
\mathcal{M}=(W,R,V)
be a Kripke model,
and let
v
and
w
be states in
\mathcal{M}
such that
(v,w)\in R_{\gamma}
.
Then there is a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\gamma}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash X

and
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
.

Proof 3.12
.

By a straightforward induction on the shallow program structure of
\gamma
.
We cover only two cases of the induction step and refer to the Lean proof for full details.

Case
\gamma=\alpha;\beta
:

It follows from
(v,w)\in R_{\gamma}
that there is some
u
such that

(v,u)\in R_{\alpha}
and
(u,w)\in R_{\beta}
.
By the induction hypothesis on
\alpha
we find a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}

such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash X
and
(v,u)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
.

Now distinguish cases.
If
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
then we find

(X,\vec{\delta}\beta)\in D_{\gamma}
and
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}\beta}

as required.
On the other hand, if
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
we have
v=u
;
furthermore,
by the induction hypothesis it follows from
(u,w)\in R_{\beta}
that there
is a pair
(Y,\vec{\eta})\in D_{\beta}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash Y
and

(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\eta}}
.
Combining all of this we find
(X\cup Y,\vec{\eta})\in D_{\gamma}
,

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash X\cup Y
and
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\eta}}
, as required.

Case
\gamma=\beta^{\ast}
:

It follows from
(v,w)\in R_{\beta^{\ast}}
that there are two possibilities.
If
v=w
then we can take the pair
(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
:
It follows that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge\emptyset
and

(v,w)\in R_{\varepsilon}
.

If, on the other hand,
v\neq w
then there is some
v_{1}\neq v
such that

(v,v_{1})\in R_{\beta}
and
(v_{1},w)\in R_{\beta^{\ast}}
.
By the induction hypothesis there is some pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}

such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash X
and
(v,v_{1})\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
.
Note that we must have
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
since
v\neq v_{1}
.
This implies that the pair
(X,\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast})
belongs to

D_{\beta^{\ast}}
.
Furthermore, we already saw that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash X
and we have

(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}\mathop{;}R_{\beta^{\ast}}=R_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}
.
In other words, we are done.

3.3.
Loaded Diamonds

The unfolding of boxes and diamonds that we defined in the previous two subsections is sufficient to deal with local repeats.
However, repeats can also be generated by non-local, that is, modal reasoning.
To recognize these situations in our tableau system, we will
load
diamond formulas before applying the modal rule.

{defi}

[
NegLoadFormula
]
The set
\underline{\mathcal{P}}
of
loaded programs
and the set
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
of
loaded formulas
are given by:

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{P}}\ni\beta

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\underline{\alpha},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}\ni\xi

\displaystyle\Coloneqq[\underline{\alpha}]\varphi\mid[\underline{\alpha}]\xi

where
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
.

A sequent is
loaded
if it contains a loaded formula, otherwise it is
free
.
A node in a tableau is loaded (free) if it is labeled with a loaded (free) sequent.

Given any (possibly) loaded formula
\xi
, we let
\xi^{-}
denote its
unloaded version
obtained by replacing each
\underline{\alpha}
with
\alpha
, without changing anything else and leaving the structure of the formula as it is.
Given a set
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, we will let
\Gamma^{-}
denote the set of formulas we obtain by unloading every formula in
\Gamma
, that is,
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{:=}\{\xi^{-}\mid\xi\in\Gamma\}
.

For example,
[\underline{a}][\underline{b\cup c^{\ast}}]p
is a loaded formula and its unloaded version is
[a][b\cup c^{\ast}]p
.
All loaded formulas are of the form

[\underline{\alpha_{1}}]\dots[\underline{\alpha_{n+1}}]\varphi
where

\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
,
\underline{\alpha_{i}}\in\underline{\mathcal{P}}
for
i\leq n+1
,
for some
n\geq 0
.
For simplicity we sometimes also write
\underline{[\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi

to mean a formula
[\underline{\alpha_{1}}][\underline{\alpha_{2}}]\cdots[\underline{\alpha_{n}}]\varphi
.

The loading mechanism is purely syntactic.
That is, there is no semantic difference between
\alpha
and
\underline{\alpha}
, as the following definition and fact make explicit.

{defi}

We extend
Subsection 2.2
from
\mathcal{F}
to
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
and from
\mathcal{P}
to
\underline{\mathcal{P}}
by adding two cases.
First, let
v\Vdash[\underline{\alpha}]\varphi
iff
v\Vdash[\alpha]\varphi
.
Second, define
R_{\underline{\alpha}}\mathrel{:=}R_{\alpha}
.

Fact 21
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a Kripke model and
w
be a state of
\mathcal{M}
.
We then have
w\Vdash\underline{[\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi
if and only if
w\Vdash[\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi
.

{defi}

[
loadMulti
]
Given a list
\vec{\alpha}
of programs and a formula
\varphi
,
we define the loaded formula
\underline{\Box}(\vec{\alpha},\varphi)
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\underline{\Box}(\varepsilon,\varphi)&\mathrel{:=}&\varphi\\
\underline{\Box}(\alpha\vec{\beta},\varphi)&\mathrel{:=}&[\underline{\alpha}]\underline{\Box}(\vec{\beta},\varphi)\end{array}

For example, if
\vec{\alpha}
is the list [
a
,
b^{\ast}
] then
\underline{\Box}(\vec{\alpha},[c]p)=[\underline{a}][\underline{b^{\ast}}][c]p
.

{defi}

[
unfoldDiamondLoaded
]
For all programs
\alpha
and all possibly loaded formulas
\xi
, we define:

\underline{\mathsf{unfold}}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\xi)\mathrel{:=}\{X\cup\{\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta},\xi)\}\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}\}

The following lemma is analogous to
16
, and we will use it to prove termination of our system.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\alpha
be a program and let
\psi
and
\varphi
be formulas such that
\varphi\in\bigcup\underline{\mathsf{unfold}}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
.
Then we have
\varphi^{-}\in\mathit{FL}(\lnot[\alpha]\psi)
(as in
Subsection 2.1
).

Proof 3.13
.

By inspection of
Subsection 3.3
and
Subsection 3.2
.

3.4.
Local Rules

Recall from
Subsection 2.1
that a set of formulas is called
basic
if it only contains formulas of the forms
\bot
,
\lnot\bot
,
p
,
\lnot p
,
[a]\varphi
and
\lnot[a]\varphi
.

{defi}

[
LocalRule
]
A
local PDL-tableau
for
\Gamma
is a tableau over the following
rules, called the local rules:

\Delta,\lnot\lnot\varphi

(\lnot)

\Delta,\varphi

\Delta,\varphi\land\psi

(
\land
)

\Delta,\varphi,\psi

\Delta,\lnot(\varphi\land\psi)

(\lnot\land)

\Delta,\lnot\varphi\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Delta,\lnot\psi

\Delta,[\alpha]\varphi

(\Box)

\alpha
non-atomic

\big\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)\big\}

\Delta,\lnot[\alpha]\varphi

(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})

\alpha
non-atomic

\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)\}

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi

(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})

\alpha
non-atomic

\big\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{unfold}}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\xi)\big\}

\Delta,\lnot[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi

(L+)

\Delta
free and basic and
n\geq 0
maximal

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi

(L-)

\Delta
basic

\Delta,\lnot[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi

where
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{F}
are formulas,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathcal{P}
are programs,
a
is an atomic program,
\xi\in\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e.,
\xi
is a possibly loaded formula, and
\Delta
is a possibly empty set of possibly loaded formulas.

Furthermore, if
t
is a child of a node
s
and
L(s)=(L+)
then
L(t)\neq(L-)
.
That is, we are not allowed to apply
(L-)
immediately after
(L+)
.

Notice that the rules involving
\mathsf{unfold}
are only applicable when the program
\alpha
is non-atomic.
Furthermore, notice that the
(L+)
rule loads
all
boxes at the front of the principal formula.
For example, given
\lnot[a][b]p
we will obtain
\lnot\underline{[a][b]}p
and not
\lnot\underline{[a]}[b]p
.
That is, we always load maximally.

We will use the following fact implicitly throughout the rest of the article.

Fact 23

(
basic_iff_noLocalRuleApp
✓)
.

A set
\Gamma
of formulas is basic if and only if no local rules other than
(L+)
and
(L-)
can be applied to
\Gamma
.

Lemma 24

(
localRuleTruth
✓)
.

All local rules are locally sound and locally invertible.
That is, for any local rule with parent
\Delta
and children
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}
,
and any pointed model
(\mathcal{M},w)
, we have

\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Delta
if and only if there is an
i
with
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Gamma_{i}
.

Proof 3.14
.

For the propositional rules
(\lnot)
,
(\land)
and
(\lnot\land)
this is easy to check.
The rules
(L+)
and
(L-)
do not affect the semantic meaning of the sequent, so for them the claim holds as well.
There remain the three modal rules.
For the
(\Box)
and the
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
rules, use, respectively,
13
and
17
.
For the
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
rule, we also use
17
via
21
.

Next, we introduce a measure on formulas and sequents that we use to show that all local PDL-tableaux are finite and that we will also use in the soundness proof.

{defi}

[
lmOfFormula
]
Let
m\colon\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}\to\mathbb{N}
be the following measure:

\displaystyle m(\bot)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}0

\displaystyle m(\lnot\bot)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}0

\displaystyle m(p)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}0

\displaystyle m(\lnot p)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}0

\displaystyle m(\lnot\lnot\varphi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}1+m(\varphi)

\displaystyle m(\varphi\land\psi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}1+m(\varphi)+m(\psi)

\displaystyle m(\lnot(\varphi\land\psi))

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}1+\max(m(\lnot\varphi),m(\lnot\psi))

\displaystyle m([a]\varphi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}0

\displaystyle m(\lnot[a]\varphi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}0

\displaystyle m([\alpha]\varphi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}1+m(\varphi)+\sum_{\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)}m(\lnot\tau)

\displaystyle m(\lnot[\alpha]\varphi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}1+m(\neg\varphi)+\sum_{\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)}m(\tau)

\displaystyle m(\lnot\underline{[\alpha_{1}]\dots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}m(\lnot[\alpha_{1}]\dots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi)

where
\varphi
and
\psi
are PDL formulas,
p
is a proposition letter,
a
is an atomic program,
\alpha
is a non-atomic program, and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
are arbitrary programs.
Recall that sequents are finite sets.
We thus extend the domain of
m
to sequents as follows:

m(\Gamma)\mathrel{:=}\sum_{\psi\in\Gamma}m(\psi).

We stress that
m(\cdot)
from
Subsection 3.4
is
not
meant to be a generally useful measure of formula complexity, as for example we have
m(\lnot\lnot p)=1
but
m([a]\lnot\lnot p)=0
.
Intuitively,
m(\varphi)
counts how many steps we can make by applying local rules to
\varphi
.
No local rule can be applied to
[a]\lnot\lnot p
, and end nodes of local PDL-tableaux will have measure zero.

Lemma 25

(
measureProp
✓)
.

The following properties hold:

(1)

m(\lnot\lnot\varphi)>m(\varphi)
,

(2)

m(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2})>m(\varphi_{1})+m(\varphi_{2})
,

(3)

m(\lnot(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}))>m(\lnot\varphi_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
,

(4)

if
\alpha
is not atomic,
then
m([\alpha]\varphi)>m(\Gamma)
for all
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)
,

(5)

if
\alpha
is not atomic,
then
m(\lnot[\alpha]\varphi)>m(\Gamma)
for all
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)
.

Proof 3.15
.

The properties (a), (b), (c) are immediate by the definition of
m
.

We now show (d).
Let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)
.
By
Subsection 3.1
of
\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)
, it follows that

\Gamma=F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\cup\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\}
,
for some
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)
.
By
6
, for all
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
, either
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
, in which case
m(\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi))=m(\varphi)
, or the first element in
\vec{\delta}
is an atomic program, in which case
m(\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi))=0
.
Hence
m(\big\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\big\})\leq m(\varphi)
.
Furthermore, note that
F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\subseteq\{\neg\tau\mid\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)\}
.
Consequently,

m(\Gamma)\ \leq\ m(\varphi)+\sum_{\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)}m(\lnot\tau)\ <\ 1+m(\varphi)+\sum_{\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)}m(\lnot\tau)\ =\ m([\alpha]\varphi),

which shows (d). Note that the last equality above holds because
\alpha
is not atomic.

For (e), let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)
.
Then, by the definition of
\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}
, there exists
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
\Gamma=X\cup\{\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\}
.
Note that
X\subseteq\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
by the definition of
D_{\alpha}
.
If
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
then
m(\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi))=m(\neg\varphi)
.
Otherwise, by
15
, the leading program (i.e., the first element of
\vec{\delta}
in
\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)
) is atomic, and hence
m(\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi))=0\leq m(\neg\varphi)
.
In both cases it follows that:

m(\Gamma)\ =\ m(\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi))+m(X)\ \leq\ m(\neg\varphi)+m(X)\ <\ 1+m(\neg\varphi)+\hskip-8.0pt\sum_{\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)}\hskip-10.0ptm(\tau)\hskip 3.0pt\ =\ m(\lnot[\alpha]\varphi).

This shows (e). Note again that the last equality holds because
\alpha
is not atomic.

Lemma 26

(
LocalRuleDecreases
✓)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(V,\lessdot,r,\Lambda,L)
be a tableau over the local rules which has no applications of either
(L+)
or
(L-)
.
If
s,t\in V
are such that
s\lessdot t
, then
m(\Lambda(s))>m(\Lambda(t))
.

Proof 3.16
.

The key idea behind the proof is that for each rule,
25
provides exactly what is required to show that
m(\Lambda(s))>m(\Lambda(t))
.

We only consider the case
L(s)=(\square)
; the other cases are analogous.
For this case, we have that
\Lambda(s)=\Delta\cup\{[\alpha]\varphi\}
where
[\alpha]\varphi\not\in\Delta
.
We also have that

\Lambda(t)=\Delta\cup\Gamma
where
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)
.
From this and
25
(d) it follows that:

m(\Lambda(t))\ \leq\ m(\Delta)+m(\Gamma)\ <\ m(\Delta)+m([\alpha]\varphi)\ =\ m(\Lambda(s))

Lemma 27
.

All local PDL-tableaux are finite.

Proof 3.17
.

Recall that sequents are finite sets.
Towards a contradiction, assume there exists an infinite local PDL-tableau for some sequent.
From
7
,
16
,
22
and inspection of the rules it follows that all branching is finite, that is, for every node
s
in a local PDL-tableau, there are finitely many children of
s
.
As all branching is finite, by Kőnig’s Lemma there exists an infinite branch
{(s_{i})}_{i<\omega}
.

Assume for contradiction that
(L+)
is applied at some node
s_{i}
.
This means that the sequent
\Lambda(s_{i+1})
is basic and loaded, but then by
23
and the fact that
(L-)
may not be applied directly after
(L+)
, no local rule would be applicable at node
s_{i+1}
.
Similarly, if the rule
(L-)
were applied at some node
s_{i}
, then the sequent
\Lambda(s_{i+1})
is basic and free, and so the only applicable local rule at node
s_{i+1}
would be
(L+)
, but we just saw that this is impossible.

It follows that
L(s_{i})\neq(L+),(L-)
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, by
26
, we obtain an infinite descending chain
m(\Lambda(s_{0}))>m(\Lambda(s_{1}))>\dots
, which provides the desired contradiction.

4.
PDL-Tableaux

We obtain the system of PDL-tableaux by adding a modal rule to the rules of local tableaux, and adding some loop checking conditions that will ensure that all closed tableaux are finite.

4.1.
Modal Rules and PDL-Tableaux

Intuitively, the application of a modal rule represents a move from one state to another state in a Kripke model.
A modal rule application is necessary to satisfy atomic diamond formulas, i.e., formulas of the form
\neg[a]\psi
, since such a formula requires the existence of an
a
-successor where
\neg\psi
is true.
Due to the semantics of box formulas, for any formula of the form
[a]\varphi
in the context of
\neg[a]\psi
, the formula
\varphi
must also be satisfied at this
a
-successor.
These formulas
\varphi
are obtained via the notion of projection.

{defi}

[
projection
]
For a sequent
\Gamma
and an atomic program
a
, we define the
projection
of
\Gamma
under
a
as
\Gamma_{a}\mathrel{:=}\{\varphi\mid[a]\varphi\in\Gamma\}
.

For example, if
\Gamma\mathrel{:=}\{[a]p,[b]q,[a^{*}]r,\lnot[b]r,r\}
then
\Gamma_{a}=\{p\}
and
\Gamma_{b}=\{q\}
.
In particular, note that
r\not\in\Gamma_{b}
because the projection does not consider any negated formulas.

Since loading as defined in
Subsection 3.3
can extend beyond the leading atomic diamond, the modal rule takes the following form.

{defi}

The
modal rule
is:

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a]}\xi

(M)

\Delta
basic

\Delta_{a},\lnot\xi

where
a
is an atomic program,
\Delta_{a}
is from
Subsection 4.1
and
\xi\in\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e.,
\xi
is a possibly loaded formula.

Note that the modal rule can only be applied to formulas starting with a loaded
atomic
diamond and that the context of the rule must be basic.
This amounts to requiring that first all local rules are exhausted before applying a modal rule, which ensures that unsatisfiability due to propositional constraints is not missed. Without this requirement, the system would not be sound.

{defi}

[
LoadedPathRepeat
]
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a tableau over the local rules (
Subsection 3.4
) and the modal rule (
Subsection 4.1
).
A
repeat
is a node
t
such that
\Lambda(t)=\Lambda(s)
for some ancestor
s
of
t
; in this case, the nearest such ancestor to
t
is called its
companion
and we denote it by
c(t)
.
A
loaded-path repeat
is a repeat
t
such that the path from
c(t)
to
t
(including end points) consists only of loaded nodes.
A
free repeat
is a repeat
t
where
t
is a free node, i.e.,
\Lambda(t)
is not loaded.

Note that local tableaux cannot contain repeats. This follows from observing that:
(i) the rules
(\Box)
,
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
, and
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
(by design) eliminate repeated formulas (see the discussion at the start of
Section 3
), (ii) the rules for the propositional connectives always remove the principal formula,
and (iii) we do not allow applying
(L-)
immediately after
(L+)
.

Fact 28
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a tableau over the local rules and the modal rule.
Let
t
be a loaded-path repeat in
\mathcal{T}
.
Then on the path from
c(t)
to
t
the modal rule
(M)
is applied at least once.

In our system, we enforce that free repeats and loaded-path repeats are leaves.

{defi}

[
Tableau
]
The
PDL-rules
are the local rules from
Subsection 3.4
together with the modal rule from
Subsection 4.1
.
A
PDL-tableau
is a tableau over the PDL-rules which adheres to the following two stopping conditions:

(T1)

every loaded-path repeat is a leaf;

(T2)

every free repeat is a leaf.

We note that our system would still be sound without any special condition about free repeats, but to ensure termination of proof search (cf.
29
), we also require free repeats to be leaves per Condition (T2) from
Subsection 4.1
.

One other type of repeat may be encountered: a node
t
such that
\Lambda(t)
is loaded and the path from
c(t)
to
t
goes through an unloaded sequent.
There is no stopping condition for such repeats so the construction of the PDL-tableau proceeds in such cases.

{defi}

[
Sequent.closed
]
A sequent
\Gamma
is
closed
if we have
\bot\in\Gamma
, or
\{\varphi,\lnot\varphi\}\subseteq\Gamma
for some
\varphi
.
A sequent that is not closed is called
open
.
A node
t
in a tableau is
closed
if
\Lambda(t)
is closed, and
t
is
open
if
\Lambda(t)
is open.

{defi}

[
inconsistent
,
consistent
]
A PDL-tableau is
closed
if each of its leaves is either closed or a loaded-path repeat.
A PDL-tableau is
open
if it is not closed.
A sequent
\Gamma
is
inconsistent
if there is a closed PDL-tableau for
\Gamma
.
Otherwise
\Gamma
is said to be
consistent
.

Now that we have defined open and closed tableaux, we can explain the role of the loading mechanism in our system.
A loaded sequent
\Delta=\neg\underline{[\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi,\Gamma
can be seen as a promise to show that the loaded formula
\neg[\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi
, in the context
\Gamma
, is not satisfiable.
That is, we want to show that there is no execution of the loaded program sequence
\alpha\mathrel{:=}\alpha_{1}\mathop{;}\cdots\mathop{;}\alpha_{n}

that leads to a state that satisfies
\varphi
.
This promise can be fulfilled by showing the existence of a (sub)tableau for
\Delta
in which all leaves are closed leaves or loaded-path repeats,
but it will remain unfulfilled if we can construct a (sub)tableau for
\Delta
which has a free repeat.
In particular, a loaded-path repeat represents a looping execution of (part of)
\alpha
.
In order to fulfil the promise, it is necessary that we exit the loop, and that all exits lead to a closed leaf or another loaded-path repeat.
Taking the perspective that the aim is to refute the satisfiability of
\Delta
by constructing a closed tableau, using terminology from the literature, we also think of loaded-path repeats as
successful repeats
and free repeats as
unsuccessful repeats
.
We provide two examples to illustrate the two kinds of repeats.

{exa}

To illustrate why we treat free repeats as open leaves,
consider the PDL formula
[a^{\ast}]\lnot[a]p\to p
which is
not
valid, i.e., its negation is satisfiable.
We show an open tableau for
\{[a^{\ast}]\lnot[a]p{,}\ \lnot p\}
in the left side of
Figure 6
.
The free repeat is visualized by the dashed arrow labeled with
\infty
.

[a^{\ast}]\lnot[a]p{,}\ \lnot p
\lnot[a]p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]\lnot[a]p{,}\ \lnot p
(\Box)

\lnot\underline{[a]}p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]\lnot[a]p{,}\ \lnot p
(L+)

[a^{\ast}]\lnot[a]p{,}\ \lnot p
(M)

\infty
w
a

Figure 6.
An open tableau ending in a free repeat and a model satisfying its root.

In the right side of
Figure 6
, we show a Kripke model that satisfies the root sequent of the tableau and thus falsifies the formula
[a^{\ast}]\lnot[a]p\to p
.
This model can be obtained from the open tableau by making one state
w
where
\lnot p
holds, and adding one
a
-edge from that state to itself — we do this ad hoc here, a systematic approach will be given in the completeness proof below in
Section 6
.
We stress that the companion and the repeat are satisfied by the same state.
Moreover, on the way from the companion to the repeat in the tableau we have executed
all
loaded programs (in this case: just one).
This means the self-loop suffices to satisfy the whole sequence, and no other state is needed.

{exa}

We now show an example of a tableau that has a loaded-path repeat as well as an open leaf.
Consider
[a][a^{\ast}]p\to[a][a^{\ast}]q
which is
not
a validity.
We show an open tableau for its negation
\{[a][a^{\ast}]p,\lnot[a][a^{\ast}]q\}
in
Figure 7
.
Note that this tableau is only open because the left branch ends in an open node (whereas a tableau for
\{[a][a^{\ast}]p,\lnot[a][a^{\ast}]p\}
, which is not satisfiable, could have the same shape but would be closed).
The loaded-path repeat is visualized by the dashed arrow labeled with

\heartsuit

.

[a][a^{\ast}]p{,}\ \lnot[a][a^{\ast}]q
[a][a^{\ast}]p{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}q
(L+)

[a^{\ast}]p{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}q
(M)

p,[a][a^{\ast}]p{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}q
(\Box)

p,[a][a^{\ast}]p{,}\ \lnot q
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})

p,[a][a^{\ast}]p{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}q
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})

[a^{\ast}]p{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}q
(M)

\heartsuit

w
v:p
a

a

Figure 7.
An open tableau with a loaded-path repeat and a model satisfying its root.

Next to the tableau in
Figure 7
, we again show a model that satisfies its root.
It consists of two states,
w
which satisfies the sequents of the first two nodes in the tableau and

v
which satisfies the sequents of all other nodes.
In the figure, we write
v:p
to indicate that
v
satisfies
p
.
Also here the companion and the repeat are satisfied by the same state.
Note that the two applications of the
(M)
rule correspond to the two edges in the model.
A crucial difference to
Subsection 4.1
is that here we need to make not only
p
true but also
q
false.
This information is coming from the left branch, not from the loaded path.

In spite of the presence of a loaded-path repeat in the tableau in
Figure 7
, we can still construct a model, since it is possible to exit the looping execution represented by the loaded-path repeat and reach an open leaf, which witnesses satisfiability.

Of course these two examples are just that, examples.
But the difference between free repeats and loaded-path repeats will be crucial for
the soundness proof, where in
36
we deal with loaded-path repeats,
and for the completeness proof, where we use free repeats to build a model.

The first main result on PDL-tableaux that we prove concerns their finiteness.

Lemma 29
.

Every PDL-tableau is finite.

Proof 4.1
.

Suppose towards a contradiction that there is an infinite PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
with root sequent
\Gamma
.
Since all PDL-rules have a finite number of children, the underlying rooted tree is finitely branching, hence by Kőnig’s Lemma it must have an infinite branch.
For each node
s
on the branch,
\Lambda(s)
is a sequent.
Every element of
\Lambda(s)
is either a formula from the Fischer-Ladner closure of
\Gamma
or a loaded version of such a formula.
Since the Fischer-Ladner closure is finite,
it follows that the number of distinct labels that can be encountered is finite.
Since any repeat of a free sequent would yield a leaf in the PDL-tableau (
Subsection 4.1
), the infinite branch cannot repeat any free sequents.
This means that from some node onwards, every descendant on the branch is loaded, and hence there must be a loaded-path repeat.
The latter is a leaf (
Subsection 4.1
) so the branch is finite, reaching a contradiction.

The following theorem states the soundness and completeness of our tableau system.
Soundness is the statement that satisfiability implies consistency (or, equivalently, that no sequent with a closed tableau is satisfiable); completeness is the converse statement that for every unsatisfiable sequent we may find a closed tableau.

Theorem 30

(
consIffSat
✓)
.

Let
\Phi\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be a sequent.
Then
\Phi
is consistent if and only if it is satisfiable.

Proof 4.2
.

The two directions of the statement will be established in the next two sections: soundness (the direction from right to left) as
34
and completeness (the direction from left to right) as
37
.

4.2.
Properties of PDL-Tableaux

We finish this section by making some useful observations concerning the shape of PDL-tableaux.

{defi}

Let
s_{1}\lessdot\ldots\lessdot s_{n}
be a path of a PDL-tableau.
If all
s_{i}
are loaded, then we say that the path is
loaded
, otherwise it is
free
.

We note that the modal rule
(M)
is the only additional rule that can be used in PDL-tableaux but not in local tableaux.
Therefore each PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
can be partitioned into maximal local sub-tableaux that are glued together by applications of the modal rule:
to each leaf of a local sub-tableau that is not already a leaf of
\mathcal{T}
the rule
(M)
is applied to obtain the root of another local sub-tableau.
We illustrate this with a simple example.

{exa}

We prove the validity

([a;b](p\land q)\land[c]\bot)\to([a;b]q\land[c]r)

in our system by constructing a closed tableau for the set

\{[a;b](p\land q)\land[c]\bot,\lnot([a;b]q\land[c]r)\}

in
Figure 8
.
It consists of four local tableaux, connected by three applications of the modal rule, marked with dotted lines.

[a;b](p\land q)\land[c]\bot,\lnot([a;b]q\land[c]r)

[a;b](p\land q),[c]\bot,\lnot([a;b]q\land[c]r)

(\land)
[a][b](p\land q),[c]\bot,\lnot([a;b]q\land[c]r)

(\Box)
(\lnot\land)
[a][b](p\land q),[c]\bot,\lnot[a;b]q

[a][b](p\land q),[c]\bot,\lnot[a][b]q

(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
[a][b](p\land q),[c]\bot,\lnot\underline{[a][b]}q

(L+)
[b](p\land q),\lnot\underline{[b]}q

(M)
p\land q,\lnot q

(M)
p,q,\lnot q

(\land)
\times

[a][b](p\land q),[c]\bot,\lnot[c]r

[a][b](p\land q),\underline{[c]}\bot,\lnot[c]r

(L+)
\bot,\lnot r

(M)
\times

Figure 8.
A PDL-tableau consisting of four local tableaux.

Recall that we write
s\lessdot t
when
s
is a parent of
t
.
We now define another relation
\lhd
, which extends
\lessdot
by adding

back edges
, that is, connections
from loaded-path repeats to their companions
.
Alternatively, we could say that we now view our tableaux as
cyclic
proofs
[
16
,
10
]
.

{defi}

[
companion
]
Given a PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
, we let

\heartsuit

be the
companion relation
such
that

s\hskip 1.99997pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\heartsuit$}\hskip 1.00006ptt\ \ \text{if}\ \ \text{$s$ is a leaf that is a loaded-path repeat with the companion $t$},

and we define the
edge relation

\lhd
by putting:

s\lhd t\ \ \text{if}\ \ s\lessdot t\text{ or }s\hskip 1.99997pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\heartsuit$}\hskip 1.00006ptt,

that is,
\lhd=\lessdot\cup\hskip 1.99997pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\heartsuit$}\hskip 1.00006pt
.
A sequence
t_{1}\lhd\ldots\lhd t_{n}
is called a
\lhd
-path
;
it is a
proper path
if
n>1
.
Finally, we let
\lhd^{+}
and
\lhd^{*}
denote, respectively, the transitive
and the reflexive transitive closure of
\lhd
.

Clearly we have
s\lhd^{*}t
iff there is a path from
s
to
t
, and
accordingly we think of
\lhd^{*}
as a reachability relation in the underlying
graph of
\mathcal{T}
(that is, the version where the edge relation
\lhd

includes back edges).
On the basis of this we define two further relations.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a PDL-tableau.
We define the relations
\equiv_{c}
and
<_{c}
by putting, for any pair of nodes
s,t\in\mathcal{T}
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}s\equiv_{c}t&\text{if}&s\lhd^{*}t\text{ and }t\lhd^{*}s\\
s<_{c}t&\text{if}&s\lhd^{*}t\text{ but not }t\lhd^{*}s.\end{array}

Informally, we have
s\equiv_{c}t
if there are
\lhd
-paths from
s
to
t

and from
t
to
s
, and we have
s<_{c}t
if there is an

\lhd
-path from
s
to
t
, but not vice versa.
The following lemma states some immediate observations.

Lemma 31

(
eProp
✓)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a closed PDL-tableau.
Then the following hold:

(a)

the relation
\equiv_{c}
is an equivalence relation, and its equivalence classes are node sets of maximal strongly connected components of
\lhd
;

(b)

the relation
<_{c}
is a strict partial order which is both well-founded and conversely well-founded.

Proof 4.3
.

For part (a), note that by
Subsection 4.2
the relation
\equiv_{c}
is transitive, reflexive and symmetric.
Moreover, for all nodes
s,t
, by the definition of
\equiv_{c}
, it is immediate that
s\equiv_{c}t
iff
s
and
t
belong to the same strongly connected component for
\lhd
.
Hence the node set of every maximal strongly connected component is an equivalence class. Conversely, every equivalence class is the node set of a maximal strongly connected component due to equivalence classes being disjoint.

For part (b), first note that by
Subsection 4.2
the relation
<_{c}
is transitive and asymmetric.
Using the transitivity of
\lhd^{*}
we can also show that
<_{c}
is irreflexive.
Second, by
29
there are only finitely many nodes in
\mathcal{T}
.
Hence
<_{c}
is a relation over a finite set and to show that it is (conversely) well-founded it suffices to show that its transitive closure is irreflexive.
But because
<_{c}
is transitive, its transitive closure is
<_{c}
itself, and we already noted that
<_{c}
is irreflexive, so we are done.

Based on
31
(b), we may use the converse of
<_{c}

as a basis for well-founded induction.
That is, in order to prove that every node of
\mathcal{T}
satisfies some property
P
,
it suffices to show that
P
holds of any node
t
,
given that
P
already holds of all
s
such that
t<_{c}s
.

Based on
31
(a) we introduce some further terminology.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a closed PDL-tableau.
The equivalence classes of
\equiv_{c}
are called
clusters
.
Non-singleton clusters will be called
proper
.

Remark 32
.

To explain the terminology ‘proper’ we first note that in every non-singleton
cluster any pair of nodes is connected by a proper
\lhd
-path.
However, this is not the case for singleton clusters.
The key observation here is that in any tableau
\mathcal{T}
, the relation
\lhd

is irreflexive — we leave it for the reader to verify this.
As a consequence, we can only have
s\lhd^{+}s
if there is a

different
node
t
such that
s\lhd^{+}t
and
t\lhd^{+}s
.
This means that a singleton
\{s\}
can only be a cluster of
\equiv_{c}
if
there is no proper path from
s
to itself.
In summary, a cluster
C
has more than one element iff any pair of nodes
in
C
is connected by a proper
\lhd
-path.

{exa}

We prove the validity
[a^{\ast}]q\to[a][{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]q
in our system, by
constructing a closed tableau for the set

\{[a^{\ast}]q,\ \lnot[a][{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]q\}
, cf.
Figure 9
.
The solid arrows here show the
\lessdot
relation.

[a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot[a][{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]q
q{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot[a][{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]q
(\Box)

q{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a][{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]}q
(L+)

[a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot\underline{[{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]}q
(M)

q{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot\underline{[{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]}q
(\Box)

(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
q{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot q
q{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a][{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]}q

\heartsuit

\lhd^{+}

Figure 9.
A closed tableau with a highlighted cluster.

This tableau has two leaves.
The left leaf labeled with
\{q{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot q\}
is closed and free. Recall that all free leaves of a tableau must be closed for the tableau to be closed.
The right leaf labeled with
\{q{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}]q{,}\ \lnot\underline{[a][{(a\cup p?)}^{\ast}]}q\}
is not closed, but it is a loaded-path repeat,
since all nodes on the
\lessdot
-path from the companion to the loaded-path repeat are loaded.
The right dashed arrow shows the

\heartsuit

relation from the loaded-path repeat to its companion, a node with the same label.
The other dashed arrow is an example of the
\lhd^{+}
relation (
Subsection 4.2
) given by first making a

\heartsuit

-step and then a
\lessdot
-step.

The dashed zone with yellow background indicates the
proper cluster
to which the loaded-path repeat and companion belong.
The other nodes of the tableau each form a
singleton cluster
.
We stress that clusters are
not
the same as local tableaux (as highlighted in
Figure 8
).

Lastly, if we call the left leaf
s
then for all nodes
t
in the dashed cluster we have
t<_{c}s
.
On the other hand, if
u
is the root of the tableau then for all nodes
t
in the dashed cluster, we have
u<_{c}t
.

In
33
below we gather some observations on the relations that we just introduced.
In particular, these will be needed in the soundness proof we give in the next section.

Lemma 33

(
ePropB
✓)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a PDL-tableau.
For all
s,u,t\in\mathcal{T}
we have:

(1)

if
s\lessdot t
then
s<_{c}t
or
t\equiv_{c}s
;

(2)

if
s\hskip 1.99997pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\heartsuit$}\hskip 1.00006ptt
then
t\equiv_{c}s
;

(3)

if
s
is free and
s<t
, then
s<_{c}t
;

(4)

if
t
is free and
s<t
, then
s<_{c}t
;

(5)

if
s
is loaded,
t
is free and
s\lessdot t
, then
s<_{c}t
;

(6)

if
s<_{c}u
and
u<_{c}t
then
s<_{c}t
;

(7)

if
t\lhd^{+}s
and
t\not\equiv_{c}s
then
t<_{c}s
;

(8)

if
s<_{c}t
then
s\not\equiv_{c}t
;

(9)

if
s<_{c}u
and
s\equiv_{c}t
then
t<_{c}u
.

Proof 4.4
.

We only show (d) since the proof of (c) is similar, and all other items are straightforward from the definitions.

First we show the following Claim.

Claim.

If
u
is free, then for all loaded-path repeats
l
such that
u<l
, we have that
u<c(l)
.

Proof of the Claim:

First note that since
u<l
and
u
is free, we have either
c(l)<u
or
u<c(l)
.
Assume that
c(l)<u
. Then
c(l)<u<l
, which means that the unique path from
c(l)
to
l
contains a free node, which contradicts that
l
is a loaded-path repeat.
This shows the claim.

Assume that
s<t
. This implies that
s\lhd^{*}t
.
We must therefore show that it is not the case that
t\lhd^{*}s
.
Assume towards a contradiction that there exists a
\lhd
-path of the form

t=t_{0}\lhd\ldots\lhd t_{m}=s
.
Fix such a
\lhd
-path with a minimal number
n
of

\heartsuit

-steps.
If
n=0
then
t<s
, which contradicts
s<t
.
If
n\geq 1
, then there is a smallest
j
such that
t_{j}\hskip 1.99997pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\heartsuit$}\hskip 1.00006ptt_{j+1}
, i.e.,
t_{j}
is the first loaded-path repeat in the path and
t_{j+1}=c(t_{j})
.
By the Claim we get
t<t_{j+1}
, hence

t=t_{0}<t_{j+1}\lhd\ldots\lhd t_{m}=s

which means that there is a
\lhd
-path from
t
to
s
with
n-1

\heartsuit

-steps.
This contradicts
n
being minimal.

5.
Soundness

In this section, we will prove the soundness of the PDL-tableau proof system, stating that every satisfiable sequent is consistent.
In fact, contrapositively we will prove the following statement.

Theorem 34

(
soundness
✓)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a closed PDL-tableau.
Then for any node
t
in
\mathcal{T}
, the sequent
\Lambda(t)
is unsatisfiable.

We will prove
34
by induction on the well-founded strict partial order

<_{c}
from
Subsection 4.2
above.

The final preparations for the soundness proof are the following two technical lemmas.

Lemma 35
.

Assume that
t
is a node in some closed PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
and that
v
is a state of a Kripke model
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Lambda(t)
.
Suppose
t
is loaded.

Then there is a
\lhd
-path
\pi
of satisfiable nodes from
t
to a node
s
such that the loaded formula is principal at
s
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Lambda(r)
, for any node
r
on
\pi
.

Proof 5.1
.

First, note that every loaded node has children with respect to
\lhd
, obtained by going to the companion or by applying a rule either to the loaded formula or other formulas.

If the loaded formula is not the principal formula at
t
, then the rule applied at
t
must be local and leave the loaded formula untouched.
Moreover, by
24
the label set of at least one of the children of the node is also true in
v
.
Now consider any
\lhd
-path starting from
t
in which no rule is ever applied to the loaded formula, and the state
v
satisfies the label set of every node on the path.
This means that only local rules may be applied on this path.
By
26
the measure of the sequents along this path strictly decreases at each
\lessdot
-step.
But after each

\heartsuit

-step in the path there must be a
\lessdot
-step, so the path must be finite.
Furthermore, every label set on such a path contains the original loaded formula.

Hence if we consider a
maximal
path of this kind, the only rule that is applicable at its final node must have the loaded formula as its principal formula.
Thus any such maximal path meets the conditions of the lemma.

Lemma 36

(
loadedDiamondPaths
✓)
.

Assume that
t
is a node in some closed PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
and that
v
is a state of a Kripke model
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Lambda(t)
.
Furthermore, let
\xi
be a (possibly loaded) formula such that
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi\in\Lambda(t)
, and suppose that
w
in
\mathcal{M}
is such that
v\xrightarrow{\alpha}w
and
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\xi
.

Then there is a
\lhd
-path
\pi
of satisfiable nodes from
t
to a node
s
such that either
s\not\equiv_{c}t
or every node on
\pi
belongs to the same cluster as
t
, and
s
satisfies all these three conditions:

(1)

\lnot\xi\in\Lambda(s)
;

(2)

\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Lambda(s)
;

(3)

\Lambda(s)\setminus\{\neg\xi\}
is free.

Note that “
\Lambda(s)\setminus\{\neg\xi\}
is free” just means that there can be no loaded formula in
\Lambda(s)
other than
\neg\xi
(which may itself be loaded or not).

Proof 5.2
.

We will prove the Lemma by induction on the shallow program structure of
\alpha
.
Before distinguishing the different cases of
\alpha
, we note that by
35
we may without loss of generality assume that either the modal rule or the loaded diamond rule is applied to
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi
at node
t
.

To see this, assume that
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi
is not principal at
t
.
Then by
35
there is a
\lhd
-path
\rho
of satisfiable nodes from
t
to a node
t^{\prime}
such that the loaded formula is principal at
t^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Lambda(r)
, for any node
r
on
\rho
.
If some node
r
on this path falls out of the cluster of
t
, we may put
s\mathrel{:=}r
and we are done.
Hence, we assume otherwise, i.e., we have
t\equiv_{c}t^{\prime}
.
Now if the rule applied at
t^{\prime}
is the unloading rule
(L-)
, then we are done as well.
For, in this case
t^{\prime}
has a unique descendant, say,
s
, and this descendant is free.
By
33
(e) this implies
t^{\prime}<_{c}s
, and so by
33
(f) we find
t<_{c}s
which means in particular that
s\not\equiv_{c}t
.
Then this
s
meets the requirements of the lemma.
Hence we may assume that at
t^{\prime}
some rule other than
(L-)
is applied to the loaded formula
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi
.
Concretely, this rule must either be
(M)
(if
\alpha
is atomic) or
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
(if it is not).

We now turn to the inductive proof of the Lemma.

Base case:

In the base case of the proof we consider the following options.
In both cases we assume that
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi
is principal at
t
.

Case
\alpha=a
:

In this case the rule applied at
t
is the modal rule
(M)
; let
s
be the unique child of
t
, then
s
is labeled with the sequent

{\big(\Lambda(t)\big)}_{a},\neg\xi
.
Furthermore, we have
v\xrightarrow{a}w
.
Since
\Lambda(t)
holds at
v
, it is then obvious that
s

satisfies the required properties (a), (b), and (c).

Case
\alpha={\tau?}
:

In this case the rule applied at
t
is
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
.
Moreover, we have
w=v
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\tau,\neg\xi
, so we may take the
child
s
of
t
that is labeled with the sequent
\Lambda(t),\tau,\neg\xi
.

Induction step:

In the induction step of the proof we prove the statement of the lemma for an arbitrary complex program
\alpha
.
Given that
\alpha
is not atomic, the rule applied at
t
cannot be
(M)
.
Moreover, as argued above, without loss of generality we may assume that the rule applied
at
t
is
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
, with
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi
as the
principal formula.

As our induction hypothesis, we may suppose that the lemma holds for all programs
simpler than
\alpha
, but in fact, it will be convenient to use the following
extended version of this statement that applies to
lists
of such programs.

Claim (Extended Induction Hypothesis)
:
Let
\vec{\delta}
be a sequence of programs that are all simpler than
\alpha
.
Let
\psi
be some (possibly loaded) formula, and assume that
r
in
\mathcal{T}
and

v
in
\mathcal{M}
are such that

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Lambda(r)
,

\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta},\psi)\in\Lambda(r)
,
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
and

\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\neg\psi
.

Then there is a
\lhd
-path
\pi
of satisfiable nodes from
r
to a node
s
such that either
s\not\equiv_{c}r
or every node on
\pi
belongs to the same cluster as
r
, and
s
satisfies the following three conditions:
(a)
\lnot\psi\in\Lambda(s)
;
(b)
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Lambda(s)
;
(c)
\Lambda(s)\setminus\{\neg\psi\}
is free.

One may
prove
this Claim by induction on the length of the sequence
\vec{\delta}
, applying the original induction hypothesis for each of the programs simpler than
\alpha
.
We return to the proof of the induction step of the main proof.
Here we make a case distinction as to the nature of
\alpha
.

Case
\alpha=\beta\cup\gamma
or
\alpha=\beta;\gamma
:

By
20
there is a pair
(F,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such
that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash F
and
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
.
Since the rule applied at
t
is
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
,
t
has a child
t^{\prime}
such
that
F\cup\{\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta},\xi)\}\subseteq\Lambda(t^{\prime})
,
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Lambda(t^{\prime})
.

Since all programs in
\vec{\delta}
are proper subprograms of
\alpha
,
by the Extended Induction Hypothesis we obtain a
\lhd
-path
\rho
in

\mathcal{T}
from
t^{\prime}
to some node
s
such that
(a)
\lnot\xi\in\Lambda(s)
;
(b)
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Lambda(s)
; and
(c)
\Lambda(s)\setminus\{\neg\xi\}
is free.
From this and the fact that
t\lhd t^{\prime}
the statement of the Lemma follows.

Case
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
:

Here we would like to use the same argument as in the previous cases, and it is
true that there is a pair
(F,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash F
and
(v,w)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
.
The problem, however, is that here we may encounter
\beta^{*}
itself as the
final program on the list
\vec{\delta}
, so that the Extended Induction Hypothesis
may not apply directly.

We therefore reason as follows.
Since
(v,w)\in R_{\beta^{*}}
, there is a path

v=w_{0}\xrightarrow{\beta}w_{1}\xrightarrow{\beta}\ldots\xrightarrow{\beta}w_{n}=w.

Here we may take
n
to be minimal, which means that all
w_{i}
are distinct.

We now first show the following claim.

Claim
:
For all
k
such that
0\leq k\leq n
there is a satisfiable
\lhd
-path
from
t
to a node
s_{k}
such that either
s_{k}\not\equiv_{c}t
or we have
(a
k
)
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi\in\Lambda(s_{k})
,
(b
k
)
\mathcal{M},w_{k}\Vdash\Lambda(s_{k})
,
(c
k
)
\Lambda(s_{k})\setminus\{\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi\}
is free,
and (d
k
) the formula
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi
is principal at
s_{k}
.

We prove the claim by an inner induction on
k
.
For the base case
k=0
we take
s_{0}
to be any node as given by
35
.
Then clearly
s_{0}
either satisfies
s_{0}\equiv_{c}t
or else it has the properties (a
0
) to (d
0
).

For the induction step to
k+1
, we assume as our inner inductive hypothesis
that the Claim holds for
k
.
If
s_{k}\not\equiv_{c}t
then let
s_{k+1}\mathrel{:=}s_{k}
and check that we are done.
Otherwise, there is a satisfiable
\lhd
-path
\pi^{k}
from
t
to
s_{k}

satisfying the properties (a
k
) to (d
k
).

Furthermore, by
20
there is a pair
(F,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}
such that
\mathcal{M},w_{k}\Vdash F
and
(w_{k},w_{k+1})\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
.
Note that since
w_{k}\neq w_{k+1}
it cannot be the case that
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
; from this it follows that
(F,\vec{\delta}\beta^{*})\in D_{\beta^{*}}
.
Then, since
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi
is principal at
s_{k}
by (d
k
),

s_{k}
must have a successor
s^{\prime}_{k}
labeled

\Lambda(s^{\prime}_{k})=\big(\Lambda(s_{k})\setminus\{\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi\}\big)\cup F\cup\{\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast},\xi)\}.

Now observe that, since all programs in
\vec{\delta}
are subprograms of
\beta

and hence proper subprograms of
\beta^{*}
, we may apply the outer inductive
hypothesis, or rather its strengthened version, to the node
s^{\prime}_{k}
, the list

\vec{\delta}
, and the formula
\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast},\xi)=\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta},\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi)
.
This gives a satisfiable path
\rho
from
s^{\prime}_{k}
to some node
t_{k+1}
such that either
t_{k+1}\equiv_{c}s_{k}
, or we have
(a)
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi\in\Lambda(t_{k+1})
,
(b)
\mathcal{M},w_{k+1}\Vdash\Lambda(t_{k+1})
, and
(c)
\Lambda(t_{k+1})\setminus\{\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi\}
is free.
Finally, if
t_{k+1}\equiv_{c}s_{k}
we are done; otherwise, let
s_{k+1}
be a node as given by
35
,
in particular, the formula
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi
is principal at

s_{k+1}
, while in addition
s_{k+1}
satisfies the properties
(a
k+1
) to (d
k+1
).

It is then straightforward to verify that the concatenation of the path

\pi^{k}
(from
t
to
s_{k}
), the one-edge path
s_{k}\lhd s^{\prime}_{k}
,
the path
\rho
(from
s^{\prime}_{k}
to
t_{k+1}
) and the path from

t_{k+1}
to
s_{k+1}
satisfies the required conditions.

This finishes the proof of the Claim.

Finally, returning to the main proof of the lemma, in order to obtain the
required node
s
in the inductive case where
\alpha=\beta^{*}
, we consider
the node
s_{n}
as given by the Claim instantiated with
k\mathrel{:=}n
.
Since by condition (d
n
) the formula
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi
is
principal at
s_{n}
and the pair
(\emptyset,\varepsilon)
belongs to

D_{\alpha}
, the node
s_{n}
has a
\lhd
-successor
s
such that

\Lambda(s)=\big(\Lambda(s_{n})\setminus\{\lnot\underline{[\beta^{\ast}]}\xi\}\big)\cup\{\lnot\xi\}.

It is then straightforward to verify that this node
s
has the required
properties.

We are now ready to prove the soundness of our system.

Proof 5.3

(Proof of
34
)
.

We prove the theorem by induction on the relation
<_{c}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a closed PDL-tableau, take an arbitrary node
t
in
\mathcal{T}
and
assume inductively that all nodes
s
with
t<_{c}s
are unsatisfiable.
It suffices to show that
t
is unsatisfiable.
We make a case distinction as to the nature of
t
.

First, assume that
t
is free.
If
t
is a leaf, then since
\mathcal{T}
is assumed to be closed,
t
must be closed
and hence,
\Lambda(t)
is unsatisfiable.
If
t
is not a leaf, then since it is free, by
33
(c) each of
its children is
<_{c}
-smaller than
t
.
It thus follows by the induction hypothesis that no child of
t
carries a
satisfiable sequent.
But since the rule applied at node
t
is sound (as all rules are), this means
that
\Lambda(t)
cannot be satisfiable either.

The interesting case is where
t
is loaded; say, we have

\lnot\underline{[\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]}\xi\in\Lambda(t)
,
for some (unloaded) PDL formula
\xi
.

Let
C
be the cluster to which
t
belongs.
We claim that any
\lhd
-path of satisfiable nodes starting at
t
must
remain in
C
.
This easily follows from
33
(g): if we leave the cluster we arrive at a node which is unsatisfiable by the induction hypothesis.

Now assume for contradiction that
\Lambda(t)
is satisfiable.
Satisfiability of
\lnot[\alpha_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{n}]\xi

and successive applications of
36

(together with the observation that paths of satisfiable nodes must stay in
C
) imply
that there is a
\lhd
-path of satisfiable nodes leading from
t
to a
node
s
such that
\lnot\xi\in\Lambda(s)
and
\Lambda(s)\setminus\{\neg\xi\}

is free.
Since
\xi
is not loaded this means that
s
itself is a free node.
Since
s
is free, it cannot belong to the same cluster as
t
.
Since there exists a
\lhd
-path from
t
to
s
and
t\not\equiv_{c}s
, we get that
t<_{c}s
.
Thus we have arrived at the desired contradiction:
We had that
\Lambda(s)
was satisfiable, but by the induction hypothesis,
\Lambda(s)
is unsatisfiable.
We thus conclude that
\Lambda(t)
is unsatisfiable.

6.
Completeness

The aim of this section is to prove the completeness of the PDL-tableau system, stated as follows.

Theorem 37

(
completeness
✓)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a sequent.
If
\Gamma
is consistent, then it is satisfiable.

In
Subsection 6.1
we will first define
model graphs
, a class of models based on sets of formulas and similar to canonical models used in many other completeness proofs
[
6
, Section 4.2]
.
Then in
Subsection 6.2
we describe how proof search in our tableau system can be seen as a two-player game, and in
Subsection 6.3
we show how to convert a winning strategy for one of the players to a model graph.
Combining all of this we then prove
37
in
Subsection 6.4
.

6.1.
Saturated sets and model graphs

As mentioned, the states of model graphs are given by certain sets of formulas, which we will call
saturated
.

{defi}

[
saturated
]
A set of formulas
X
is
saturated
if it fulfils the following conditions for all formulas
\varphi
,
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
and all programs
\alpha
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lnot\lnot\varphi\in X&\text{ implies }&\varphi\in X\\[3.00003pt]
\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}\in X&\text{ implies }&\varphi_{1}\in X\text{ and }\varphi_{2}\in X\\[3.00003pt]
\lnot(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2})\in X&\text{ implies }&\lnot\varphi_{1}\in X\text{ or }\lnot\varphi_{2}\in X\\[3.00003pt]
[\alpha]\varphi\in X&\text{ implies }&\Delta\subseteq X,\text{ for some }\Delta\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\Box}(\alpha,\varphi)\\[3.00003pt]
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in X&\text{ implies }&\Delta\subseteq X,\text{ for some }\Delta\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)\\[3.00003pt]
\end{array}

A saturated set
X
is
locally consistent
if
\bot\notin X
and
p\in X\text{ implies }\lnot p\notin X
, for all proposition letters
p
.

As in the setting of completeness proofs that involve the canonical model, the key statement on model graphs is a
truth lemma
stating that formulas that belong to a saturated set are also satisfied by that set, taken as a state.
In the definition of model graphs and in the proof of this truth lemma we use a variation
Q_{\alpha}
of the accessibility relation
R_{\alpha}
— the main difference being in the case of tests, where we use, in anticipation of the truth lemma, the membership relation rather than the truth relation.

{defi}

[
Q
]
Let
W
be some collection of saturated sets, and let, for every atomic program
a
,
R_{a}
be a binary relation on
W
.
Then by program induction we define a relation
Q_{\alpha}
on
W
for every
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}Q_{a}&\mathrel{:=}&R_{a}\\[3.00003pt]
Q_{\tau?}&\mathrel{:=}&\{(X,X)\mid\tau\in X\}\\[3.00003pt]
Q_{\alpha\cup\beta}&\mathrel{:=}&Q_{\alpha}\cup Q_{\beta}\\[3.00003pt]
Q_{\alpha;\beta}&\mathrel{:=}&Q_{\alpha}\mathop{;}Q_{\beta}\\[3.00003pt]
Q_{\alpha^{\ast}}&\mathrel{:=}&Q_{\alpha}^{*}\\[3.00003pt]
\end{array}

{defi}

[
ModelGraph
]
A
model graph
is a Kripke model
\mathcal{M}=(W,\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\},V)
such that:

(1)

Each element of
W
is a locally consistent saturated set of formulas;

(2)

If
X\in W
, then
p\in X
iff
X\in V(p)
;

(3)

If
X\xrightarrow{a}Y
and
[a]\varphi\in X
for some atomic program
a
, then
\varphi\in Y
;

(4)

If
X\in W
and
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in X
for any program
\alpha
, then there exists a
Y\in W
such that
(X,Y)\in Q_{\alpha}
and
\lnot\varphi\in Y
.

Lemma 38

(
truthLemma
✓)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}
is a model graph.
Then for all states
X
and all formulas
\varphi\in X
it holds that
