\mathcal{M},X\Vdash\varphi
.

Proof 6.1
.

The proof of this Lemma is more or less standard.
By a simultaneous induction on formulas and programs we prove that the following
statements hold, for all formulas
\varphi
and all programs
\alpha
:

(C1) If
\varphi\in X
, then
\mathcal{M},X\Vdash\varphi
;

(C2) If
\lnot\varphi\in X
, then
\mathcal{M},X\not\Vdash\varphi
;

(C3)
Q_{\alpha}\subseteq R_{\alpha}
;

(C4) If
[\alpha]\varphi\in X
and
X\xrightarrow{\alpha}Y
, then
\varphi\in Y
.

Note that we work with a fixed model graph
\mathcal{M}
in this proof, so for the sake of brevity we will only write
X\Vdash\varphi
for
\mathcal{M},X\Vdash\varphi
.

Base cases for (C1) and (C2):

If
\varphi=\bot
or
\varphi
is a proposition letter, then statements (C1) and (C2) hold by conditions (a) and (b) in
Subsection 6.1
.

Base case for (C3):

For atomic programs
\alpha=a
, we have
Q_{a}=R_{a}
by
Subsection 6.1
.

Base case for (C4):

For atomic programs, statement (C4) holds by condition (c) in
Subsection 6.1
.

Induction steps for (C1) and (C2):

Let
\varphi
be a formula. By induction hypothesis, statements (C1) and (C2) hold for all subformulas of
\varphi
, and statements (C3) and (C4) hold for all programs in
\varphi
.

Case
\varphi=\lnot\psi
:

(C1)
If
\neg\psi\in X
,
then by the induction hypothesis (C2) for the subformula
\psi
, we have
X\not\Vdash\psi
, hence
X\Vdash\lnot\psi
.
(C2)
If
\lnot\varphi=\lnot\lnot\psi\in X
, then because
X
is saturated, we have

\psi\in X
and by induction hypothesis (C1) for
\psi
, we have
X\Vdash\psi
and hence
X\not\Vdash\lnot\psi
.

Case
\varphi=\psi\land\chi
:

(C1)
If
\psi\land\chi\in X
, then by saturation,
\psi\in X
and
\chi\in X
, which by induction hypothesis implies

X\Vdash\psi
and
X\Vdash\chi
, thus
X\Vdash\psi\land\chi=\varphi
.
(C2)
If
\lnot(\psi\land\chi)\in X
, then
\lnot\psi\in X
or
\lnot\chi\in X
, which
by induction hypothesis (C2) for
\psi
and
\chi
implies

X\not\Vdash\psi\land\chi
.

Case
\varphi={[\alpha]}\psi
:

(C1) If
[\alpha]\psi\in X
, then by induction hypothesis (C4) for
\alpha
we have that

\psi\in Y
for all
Y
such that
X\xrightarrow{\alpha}Y
.
By induction hypothesis (C1) for
\psi
we also have that
\psi\in Y
implies

Y\Vdash\psi
, hence we have
X\Vdash[\alpha]\psi
.

(C2)
If
\lnot[\alpha]\psi\in X
, then by condition (d) there is a
Y
such that
(X,Y)\in Q_{\alpha}
and
\lnot\psi\in Y
.
By induction hypothesis (C3) for
\alpha
, we have
Q_{\alpha}\subseteq R_{\alpha}
and by induction hypothesis (C2) for
\psi
, we have
Y\Vdash\lnot\psi
.
Combining these observations we find
X\not\Vdash[\alpha]\psi
.

Induction step for (C3):

If
\alpha
is a test, say,
\alpha={\tau?}
, then by
Subsection 6.1
we have
Q_{\tau?}=\{(X,X)\mid\tau\in X\}
.
By the induction hypothesis (C1) for subformula
\tau
we find that
Q_{\tau?}\subseteq\{(X,X)\mid\mathcal{M},X\Vdash\tau\}=R_{\tau?}
.
The other cases are straightforward by using the induction hypothesis for (C3).
For instance, we have
Q_{\alpha;\beta}=Q_{\alpha}\mathop{;}Q_{\beta}\subseteq R_{\alpha}\mathop{;}R_{\beta}=R_{\alpha;\beta}
.

Induction step for (C4):

Let
\alpha
be a non-atomic program, and assume as induction hypothesis that statement (C4) holds for all subprograms of
\alpha
, and that statements (C1) and (C2) hold for all subformulas of
\alpha
, in particular for formulas
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
.

Case
\alpha={\tau?}
,
\alpha=\beta\cup\gamma
or
\alpha=\beta;\gamma
:

Assume that
[\alpha]\varphi\in X
and
X\xrightarrow{\alpha}Y
.
Then by saturation of
X
there is some test profile
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\alpha)
such
that
B^{\ell}_{\alpha,\varphi}=F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\cup\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}\}\subseteq X
.
Recall that the formulas in
F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
are of the form
\neg\tau
where
{\tau?}
is a subprogram of
\alpha
, and hence
\tau
is a subformula of
\alpha
.
By induction hypothesis (C2) on these subformulas
\tau
, we obtain that
\mathcal{M},X\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
.
By
14
there is
(X,Y)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
for some

\vec{\delta}=\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n}\in P^{\ell}{(\alpha)}
.
Now observe that by
6
(c) each
\delta_{i}
belongs to
\mathit{Progs}(\alpha)
, but is not equal to
\alpha
.
Hence each
\delta_{i}
is a proper subprogram of
\alpha
, so that we may apply the inductive hypothesis to it.
Now
n
successive applications of the inductive hypothesis (C4) on the programs
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n}
imply that
\varphi\in Y
, as required.

Case
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
:

Assume that
[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi\in X
and
X\xrightarrow{\beta^{\ast}}Y
.
Then there is a minimal
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(X,Y)\in{(R_{\beta})}^{n}
.
We now prove (C4) by an inner induction over
n
.

Base case
n=0
: In this case
X=Y
, so we must show that
\varphi\in X
. By saturation of
X
,
there is some
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta^{\ast})
such that
B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\varphi}\subseteq X
.
Since
\varepsilon\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}
, we obtain that

\varphi=\Box(\varepsilon,\varphi)\in B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\varphi}
, hence
\varphi\in X
.

Induction step:
Let
n>0
and assume the claim holds for
m\mathrel{:=}n-1
.
It follows from
(X,Y)\in R_{\beta}^{n}
and minimality of
n
that there is a
Z\neq X
such that
X\xrightarrow{\beta}Z
and
Z\xrightarrow{\beta^{m}}Y
.

By saturation of
X
there is some
\ell\in\mathit{TP}(\beta^{\ast})
such that

B^{\ell}_{\beta^{\ast},\varphi}=F^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}\cup\{\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\mid\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}\}\subseteq X
.
It follows by the induction hypothesis (C2) on the subformulas
\tau
such that
\neg\tau\in F^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}=F^{\ell}{(\beta)}
that
\mathcal{M},X\Vdash\bigwedge F^{\ell}{(\beta)}
.
By
14
we may conclude that
(X,Z)\in R_{\vec{\delta}}
, for some
\vec{\delta}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta)}
.
Note that
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
since
n
was minimal and thus
X\neq Z
.
It follows that
\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}\in P^{\ell}{(\beta^{\ast})}
, and because of this we have
\Box(\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast},\varphi)=\Box(\vec{\delta},[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi)\in X
.
Now, as in the previous case, through successive applications of the inductive hypothesis for (C4) on the programs in
\vec{\delta}
we find
[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi\in Z
.
But now we may apply the inner induction hypothesis on
Z
and
Y
, for the
formula
[\beta^{\ast}]\varphi
.
This gives
\varphi\in Y
as required.

Note that condition (d) in the definition of a model graph requires that certain pairs of saturated sets belong to the relation
Q_{\alpha}
, for some program
\alpha
.
In the next subsection, where we will define a concrete model graph, we will prove that it satisfies this condition (d) by induction on the complexity of programs.
For this proof it will be convenient to extend the definition of the
Q
-relations as follows.

{defi}

[
Qcombo
]
Let
X
be some set of formulas, and let
\vec{\delta}
be a list of programs.
We define the relations
Q_{X},Q_{\vec{\delta}}
and
Q_{X,\vec{\delta}}
on
W
as
follows:

Q_{X}\mathrel{:=}\mathsf{Id}_{W}\cap\bigcap_{\tau\in X}Q_{\tau?},

while
Q_{\vec{\delta}}
is defined by induction on the length of the list

\vec{\delta}
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}Q_{\varepsilon}&\mathrel{:=}&\mathsf{Id}_{W}\\[3.00003pt]
Q_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}&\mathrel{:=}&Q_{\alpha}\mathop{;}Q_{\vec{\gamma}}\end{array}

Finally we set

Q_{X,\vec{\delta}}\mathrel{:=}Q_{X}\mathop{;}Q_{\vec{\delta}}.

Lemma 39

(
cpHelpA
✓)
.

Let
\alpha
be a PDL-program.
Then we have
Q_{X,\vec{\delta}}\subseteq Q_{\alpha}
for every pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
.

Proof 6.2
.

By induction on the shallow program structure of
\alpha
.
The claim for the base cases with an atomic program
\alpha=a
or with a test
\alpha={\tau?}
follows directly from
Subsection 6.1
.
For the inductive step we have three cases.
If
\alpha=\beta\cup\gamma
then the claim follows from applying the induction hypothesis to
\beta
and
\gamma
.
For the cases
\alpha=\beta;\gamma
and
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
before applying the induction hypothesis to
\beta
(and
\gamma
) we make an additional case distinction on whether the unfolding of
\beta
yields only tests or (also) a non-empty list of programs.
Notably, in the case
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
there is
no
need for an additional inner induction.
The proof overall is very similar to that of
14
, so we omit further details here (note that this lemma has been verified in Lean).

6.2.
Tableau game

The search space of tableaux for a sequent
\Gamma
can be described as a game between two players.
Given a sequent
\Gamma
, the
tableau game

\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
is a two-player graph game with players Builder and Prover.
Intuitively, Builder aims to show that the sequent
\Gamma
is satisfiable, whereas it is Prover’s goal to construct a closed tableau for
\Gamma
.

The game proceeds by the two players moving a token from one position to another.
A
position
of
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
is either:

•

a (possibly loaded) sequent
\Delta
, or

•

a triple of the form
(\Delta,\varphi,R)
such that
\Delta\cup\{\varphi\}
is a (possibly loaded) sequent such that
\varphi\not\in\Delta
, and
R
is a PDL-rule applicable to
\Delta,\varphi
where
\varphi
is the principal formula, or

•

v_{\bot}
, a sink node that has no outgoing edges.

Positions of the first type belong to Prover; at a position
\Delta
he picks
a formula
\varphi\in\Delta
and a rule
R
to be applied to
\varphi
, thus moving
the token to the position
(\Delta\setminus\{\varphi\},\varphi,R)
.
Positions of the second type belong to Builder; at a position
(\Delta,\varphi,R)

she picks one of the rule children of
R
, and this rule child is then the next position.
(In many cases, she has no real choice, since the rule has only one rule child.)
The sink node also belongs to Builder. It is needed for technical reasons to define the winning condition (see below).
A
match
of
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
consists of a sequence of positions, starting at
\Gamma
, induced by the players’ moves.
We also call it a
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
-match.
Such a match is
won by Prover
if it reaches a closed sequent or a loaded-path repeat.
On the other hand, a match is
won by Builder
if it reaches a sequent to which no rule is applicable, or a free repeat.
Here, we define loaded-path repeats and free repeats essentially in the same way as in
Subsection 4.1
, i.e.,
a repeat in the game
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
is a position of the form
\Delta
which has occurred previously in the match.
Such a repeat is free, if
\Delta
is not loaded, and it is a loaded-path repeat if
\Delta
is loaded, and all sequent positions (i.e., positions of the first kind) in the match between the two occurrences of
\Delta
are also loaded.
More precisely, in terms of the game graph, positions labelled with a free repeat have no outgoing edges and belong to Prover. Positions that are labelled by a loaded-path repeat or a closed sequent have one outgoing edge to the sink node
v_{\bot}
which belongs to Builder. Hence the winning condition reduces to the usual graph game condition that a player who is stuck (i.e., it is their turn but they cannot make a move) loses the match.

A
strategy
for a player
P
prescribes a move at every position in the game where it is
P
’s turn.
Such a strategy is
winning
if it leads to a win for
P
, no matter which moves the other player makes.
A strategy for a player
P
can be represented as a subgraph of the game tree
obtained by deleting all except one outgoing edge (corresponding to the prescribed move) from the positions where it is
P
’s turn. If a position for
P
has no outgoing edges, it stays this way. At positions for the other player, all outgoing edges remain. This results in a tree structure in which the branches are all the possible matches that can arise when
P
plays according to the strategy.
We simplify this structure further by identifying the positions for
P
that have out-degree 1 with their unique successor, resulting in a tree in which each vertex corresponds to a partial match ending in a position of the first kind (labelled with a sequent) and the local branching corresponds to choices of the other player.

Formally, we define a strategy tree for Prover as follows.

{defi}

A
strategy tree for Prover in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
is a structure
\mathbb{T}=(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r,\Lambda,f)
, where
(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r)
is a tree with root
r
,
\Lambda
is a labelling which assigns to each node
s\in T
a (possibly loaded) sequent
\Lambda(s)
, and
f
assigns to each node
s\in T
a pair
(R,\varphi)
where
R
is a PDL-rule and
\varphi
is a formula
such that

•

\Lambda(r)=\Gamma
;

•

For every
s\in T
with children
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
and such that
f(s)=(R,\varphi)

we have that

\Lambda(s)~/~\Lambda(t_{1})|\dots|\Lambda(t_{n})
is an instance of
R
with principal formula
\varphi
.

We say that
\mathbb{T}
is
winning
for Prover if
\mathbb{T}
is finite and each of its branches represents a
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
-match that is won by Prover.

It follows immediately from the definition of the game and
Subsection 6.2
that a strategy for Prover corresponds to a tableau over the PDL rules, and the strategy is winning if and only if the corresponding tableau is a closed PDL-tableau.
Hence, a winning strategy tree for Prover yields a closed PDL-tableau.

Lemma 40

(
gameP
✓)
.

A strategy tree
\mathbb{T}
for Prover corresponds to a tableau over the PDL rules, and
if
\mathbb{T}
is winning for Prover in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
then
\Gamma
has a closed PDL-tableau.

Proof 6.3
.

Let
\mathbb{T}=(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r,\Lambda,f)
be a strategy tree for Prover in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
. Define a labelling function
L
by taking
L(s)\coloneqq R
if
f(s)=(R,\varphi)
. By the rules of the game,
\mathcal{T}=(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r,\Lambda,L)
is a tableau over the PDL rules (cf.
Subsection 2.3
). Furthermore, by the winning condition of the game,
\mathbb{T}
is winning for Prover if and only if
\mathcal{T}
is a closed PDL-tableau.

In a strategy tree for Builder, at a node
s
, for every possible rule application that can be chosen by Prover there is a child
t
labelled with the rule child prescribed by the strategy in response to Prover’s move, and all children of
s
arise in this way.
More precisely, a strategy tree for Builder is defined as follows.

{defi}

[
BuildTree
]
A
strategy tree for Builder in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
is a structure
\mathbb{T}=(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r,\Lambda,g)
,
where
(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r)
is a tree with root
r
,
\Lambda
is a labelling which assigns to each node
s
in
T
a (possibly loaded) sequent
\Lambda(s)
,
and
g
assigns to each node different from
r
a pair
(R,\varphi)

such that

•

\Lambda(r)=\Gamma
;

•

For all
s\in T
: if rule
R
can be applied to
\Lambda(s)
with principal formula
\varphi
then there is a
t\in T
such that
s\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}t
and
g(t)=(R,\varphi)
;

•

For all
s,t\in T
: if
s\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}t
and
g(t)=(R,\varphi)
then rule
R
can be applied to
\Lambda(s)
with principal formula
\varphi
and
\Lambda(t)
is one of the rule children of the resulting instance of
R
.

We say that
\mathbb{T}
is
winning
for Builder if it is finite and each of its branches represents a
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
-match that is won by Builder.

In
Subsection 6.2
,
g(t)
is a possible move for Prover at
\Lambda(s)

which would lead to position
(\Lambda(s)\setminus\{\varphi\},\varphi,R)
in the game,
and
\Lambda(t)
is the rule child chosen by Builder at this position
according to the strategy represented by
\mathbb{T}
.
Winning strategy trees for Builder give rise to satisfying models, as we will explain in more detail in the next subsection.

Lemma 41

(
matchesFinite
✓)
.

All matches of
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
are finite.

Proof 6.4
.

Recall from
27
and
29
that all (local) PDL-tableaux are finite.
The proofs there used that all rule applications in a tableau stay inside the Fischer-Ladner closure of the sequent at the root.
Similarly, here in the game
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
all Prover positions are sequents that are subsets of the (loading-extended) Fischer-Ladner closure of
\Gamma
.
We use a similar argument to
29
, proceeding as follows.
Suppose towards a contradiction that there is an infinite match.
Consider the infinite list of sequents given by all Builder positions along this match.
All sequents consist of (possibly loaded) formulas from the Fischer-Ladner closure of
\Gamma
.
Because the Fischer-Ladner closure is finite there can only be finitely many different sequents occurring in the match.
There cannot be a free repeat, because it would end the match with a win for Builder.
This means that from some position onwards the sequents at all later positions are loaded.
As the match from this point is infinite by assumption, some sequent must be repeated within this loaded part.
This gives us a loaded-path repeat that ends the match (with a win for Prover), contradicting the assumption that the match is infinite.

As a corollary of
41
, we obtain
determinacy
of the tableau game.

Corollary 42

(
gamedet
✓)
.

\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
is determined, that is, either Builder or Prover has a winning strategy in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
.

As a consequence of
40
and
42
, in order to prove the completeness of the PDL-tableau system, it suffices to show that, for any sequent
\Gamma
, if Builder has a winning strategy in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
then
\Gamma
is satisfiable.

6.3.
From winning strategies to model graphs

Throughout this section, we assume a winning strategy for Builder in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)

which is represented by the strategy tree
\mathbb{T}=(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r,\Lambda,g)
.

We first gather some basic observations and definitions concerning
\mathbb{T}
— some of which are analogous to the ones we used for tableaux.
We will write
<_{\mathbb{T}}
for the transitive closure of
\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}
and
\leq_{\mathbb{T}}

for the reflexive transitive closure of
\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}
.
A
path
in
\mathbb{T}
is a sequence
\pi={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
such
that
s_{i}\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}s_{i+1}
, for all
i
; a
branch
of
\mathbb{T}

is a maximal path.
Observe that branches of
\mathbb{T}
correspond to matches of
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
in which
Builder plays her winning strategy; in particular (cf.
Subsection 6.2
), if
s\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}t
and
g(t)=(R,\varphi)
then

\Lambda(t)
is a rule child of
\Lambda(s)
of the application of
R
with principal formula
\varphi
.
It follows that a leaf of
\mathbb{T}
is either labeled with a sequent to which no
rule is applicable, or it is a
free repeat leaf
; that is, it has a
unique ancestor in
\mathbb{T}
with the same, free, label.
Given such a free repeat leaf
u
, we will denote this ancestor as
c(u)
and refer to it
as the companion of
u
.
By the same argumentation that we used to establish the finiteness of local PDL-tableaux, one can use
28
to show that
(M)
must be used on the path from
c(u)
to
u
.

{defi}

[
PreState
]
A node
t
in
\mathbb{T}
is called
initial
if either
t=r
or else
t

is the child of a modal rule instance (i.e.,
g(t)=((M),\varphi)
for some
\varphi
).
A
pre-state
is a maximal sequence
\pi={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
of nodes in
\mathbb{T}
such that
s_{1}
is initial, there is no application on
\pi
of the rules
(M)
,
(L+)
or
(L-)
, i.e., for all
s_{i}
on
\pi
,
g(s_{i})=(R,\varphi)
implies that
R\notin\{(M),(L+),(L-)\}
,
and

•

either
\pi
is a path on
\mathbb{T}
,

•

or
\pi
is a special concatenation
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}
of two paths
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
.
In this case we require that the last node
s
of
\pi^{\prime}
is a free repeat leaf, and that
\pi^{\prime\prime}
starts from its companion node
c(s)
, which means that the first node of
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is an ancestor of the last node of
\pi^{\prime}
.
The final requirement is then that the entire path
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is an initial segment of the (unique) path from
c(s)
to
s
.

Given any path or pre-state
\pi={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
on
\mathbb{T}
, we
define
\Lambda(\pi)\mathrel{:=}\bigcup_{i}\Lambda(s_{i})
.

Recall that, given a (possibly) loaded formula
\varphi
, we let
\varphi^{-}
denote its
unloaded version
; we will let
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
denote the set of formulas we obtain by unloading every formula in
\Lambda(\pi)
.

45
below states that every set of the form
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
where
\pi
is a pre-state is a locally consistent and saturated set.
In its proof, the following auxiliary result will be necessary, which provides information on how we can collect the formulas in
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
from the label sets on its path.

We say that
\varphi
is the principal formula at
s_{i}
if
s_{i}\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}s_{i+1}
and
g(s_{i+1})=(R,\varphi)
, cf.
Subsection 6.2
, i.e.,

\varphi\in\Lambda(s_{i})
, and
\Lambda(s_{i+1})
is one of the rule children resulting from applying
R
to the sequent
\Lambda(s_{i})
with
\varphi
as its principal formula.

Lemma 43

(
PreState.formsCases
✓)
.

Let
s_{j}
be some node on a pre-state
\pi={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
and let
\varphi\in\Lambda(s_{j})
be some formula. Then, either

(1)

\varphi\in\Lambda(s_{k})
, and
\varphi
has one of the following forms:
p
,
\lnot p
,
[a]\psi
,
\lnot[a]\psi
or

\lnot\underline{[a]}\psi
(in the case
\varphi=\lnot\underline{[a]}\psi
, we also have
s_{j}\leq_{\mathbb{T}}s_{k}
); or

(2)

there is some
i
with
j\leq i<k
such that
\varphi
is the principal formula
at
s_{i}
and, in case
\varphi
is loaded,
s_{j}\leq_{\mathbb{T}}s_{i}
.

Proof 6.5
.

Suppose that no rule other than possibly
(M)
,
(L+)
or
(L-)
can make
\varphi
principal.
In that case, it must be that
\varphi
has one of the forms specified in case (a).
Since no rule makes
\varphi
principal, and since every non-principal formula passes unchanged to each child (i.e., the parent and every child have the same context), it follows that
\varphi\in\Lambda(s_{k})
.
Now suppose that
\varphi=\lnot\underline{[a]}\psi
. This means that every
s_{j^{\prime}}
with
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq k
is loaded (since
(L-)
is not applied). This means that we cannot encounter a free repeat leaf along this subsequence, hence this subsequence corresponds to a path in
\mathbb{T}
, i.e.,
s_{j}\leq_{\mathbb{T}}s_{k}
.

Now suppose that some rule other than
(M)
,
(L+)
or
(L-)
can make
\varphi
principal.
By the maximality condition in the definition of pre-state, and the ‘iff’ in
Subsection 6.2
that ensures that every applicable rule is applied, it follows that
\varphi
is principal
by some rule other than
(M)
,
(L+)
or
(L-)
at
s_{i}
such that
j\leq i
; then one of the children of this rule appears in the pre-state (by maximality) as the label at
s_{i+1}
. Thus
i<k
.
Finally, if
\varphi
is loaded, then the path from
s_{j}
to
s_{i}
is loaded. As in the argument in the preceding paragraph, this means that we cannot encounter a free repeat leaf along this subsequence. Hence, this subsequence corresponds to a path in
\mathbb{T}
, i.e.,
s_{j}\leq_{\mathbb{T}}s_{i}
.

Note that statement (b) cannot be strengthened to
s_{j}\leq_{\mathbb{T}}s_{k}
since it is possible for loading to be removed even without
(L-)
being applied, due to the test operator.

Lemma 44
.

Let
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi
be a PDL formula where
\alpha
is not atomic and let
\pi
be a pre-state such that
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in\Lambda(\pi)
.
Then there is a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
X\cup\{\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\}\subseteq\Lambda(\pi)
.
Similarly, if
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\varphi\in\Lambda(\pi)
, then there is a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
X\cup\{\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\}\subseteq\Lambda(\pi)
.

Proof 6.6
.

This follows from the proof rules (
Subsection 3.4
), the definition of the
\mathsf{unfold}
functions, and the previous lemma which guarantees that the formula will be made principal at some node in the pre-state (by a
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
or
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
rule).

Lemma 45

(
PreState.locConsSatBas
✓)
.

Let
\pi
be a pre-state.
Then
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
is saturated and locally consistent, and the sequent
labelling the last node of
\pi
is basic.

Proof 6.7
.

Saturation of
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
follows from
43
and
44
.
Local consistency follows from the soundness of the local PDL rules (
24
)
and the fact that
\mathbb{T}
is winning for Builder, hence, in particular, no sequent label occurring in
\mathbb{T}
is closed.
By maximality of
\pi
, the sequent
\Gamma
labelling the last node
s
on
\pi
is either a sequent to which no rule can be applied, which implies that
\Gamma
is basic, or it is a sequent such that all children of
s
are obtained via an application of the modal rule (
Subsection 4.1
). In this case, the side condition of
(M)
entails that
\Gamma
is basic.

In the following key definition of the completeness proof, we see how to define a model graph from the winning strategy tree for Builder.

{defi}
[
BuildTree.toModel
]
The structure
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}\mathrel{:=}(W,\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\},V)
is defined as follows.
Its domain
W
consists of all sets of the form
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
, where
\pi
is a pre-state of
\mathbb{T}
.
For an atomic program
a
we define the relation
R_{a}\subseteq W\times W
by putting

(X,Y)\in R_{a}\ \text{if}\ X_{a}\cup\{\lnot\varphi\}\subseteq Y,\text{ for some }\lnot[a]\varphi\in X.

Finally, the valuation
V
is given by

V(p)\mathrel{:=}\{X\in W\mid p\in X\}.

Our goal will be to prove
49
below, which states that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
is indeed a model graph.
That is, we need to show that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
satisfies the conditions (a), (b), (c) and (d) from
Subsection 6.1
.
Condition (a) will follow from the already stated
45
.
Conditions (b, c) will follow immediately from the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
.
The main challenge is to show that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
satisfies condition (d).
To show this, it suffices to prove that for every pre-state
\pi
, whenever
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
, there exists a pre-state
\pi^{\prime}
such that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{\alpha}
and
\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Observe that if
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
, then there are two possible cases:
either
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in\Lambda(\pi)
, or

\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\psi\in\Lambda(\pi)
for some loaded formula
\psi
such that
\varphi=\psi^{-}
.
We will prove these two cases separately, starting with the loaded case; the
unloaded case will be shown in
48
.

Lemma 46

(
PreState.loadedExists
✓)
.

Let
t
be a node of
\mathbb{T}
on the pre-state
\pi
, and let

\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\varphi
be a loaded formula in
\Lambda(t)
.
Then there is a node
u
on
\mathbb{T}
such that
t<_{\mathbb{T}}u
,
\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda(u)
and
u
lies on some pre-state
\rho
such that

(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho))\in Q_{\alpha}

where
Q_{\alpha}
is obtained by applying
Subsection 6.1
to the family
\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
from
Subsection 6.3
.

Proof 6.8
.

The intuition underlying the proof is to look at matches that continue the
partial match represented by the node
t
along
\pi
, and where Builder plays
her winning strategy.
If Prover keeps the same formula loaded, he can ensure that the
resulting path from
t
stays loaded, until a node
u
is reached such that

\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda(u)
.
In particular, no back edge of
\mathbb{T}
is taken.

Formally, we prove this lemma by an outer induction on
\alpha
, and an inner
induction on the converse of the tree order
<_{\mathbb{T}}
.

In the base case of the outer induction we have
\alpha=a
for some atomic program
a
.
Then by
43
we find
\lnot[\underline{a}]\varphi\in\Lambda(s_{k})

and
t\leq_{\mathbb{T}}s_{k}
.
Now suppose that at this position
s_{k}
Prover applies the modal rule with
principal formula
\lnot[\underline{a}]\varphi
.
Let
u
be the induced
\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}}
-successor of
s_{k}
, then we find
t<_{\mathbb{T}}u

and
\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda(u)
.
Furthermore, let
\rho
be any pre-state starting at
u
, then we have

\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda(\rho)
and
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho))\in Q_{a}
.
That is, all conditions of the Lemma are satisfied.

In the inductive step of the outer induction proof
\alpha
is a non-atomic
program.
By
43
there is a node
t_{1}
on
\pi
such that
t<_{\mathbb{T}}t_{1}

and
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\varphi
is the principal formula at
t_{1}
.
It then follows by the loaded diamond rule (
\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}
) that for some pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
we have
X\cup\{\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\}\subseteq\Lambda(t_{2})
, where
t_{2}
is the immediate successor of
t_{1}

on
\pi
.
It follows that
X\subseteq\Lambda(\pi)
, so that we find
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi))\in Q_{X}
.

Now make a case distinction.

Case
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
:

In this case it is clear that
u\mathrel{:=}t_{2}
and
\rho\mathrel{:=}\pi
satisfy the
conditions of the lemma.
For instance,
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi))\in Q_{\alpha}
is immediate
from
39
.

Case
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
,
\alpha
is not of the form
\beta^{\ast}
:

In this case by
15

\vec{\delta}
is of the form
\vec{\delta}=a\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n}
, where
a
is atomic and every
\delta_{i}
is a

proper
subprogram of
\alpha
.
Hence we may apply the outer inductive hypothesis to
a,\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n}
, successively.
It follows that there are nodes
u_{0},\ldots,u_{n}
in
\mathbb{T}
such that

t<_{\mathbb{T}}u_{0}<_{\mathbb{T}}\cdots<_{\mathbb{T}}u_{n}
,
\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda(u_{n})
,
as well
as pre-states
\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{n}
such that
u_{i}
lies on
\rho_{i}

(for each
i
),
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho_{0}))\in Q_{a}
and

(\Lambda^{-}(\rho_{i}),\Lambda^{-}(\rho_{i+1}))\in Q_{\delta_{i}}

(for each
i<n
).
We then have
t<_{\mathbb{T}}u_{n}
and from
39
it easily follows that

(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho_{n}))\in Q_{X}\mathop{;}Q_{\vec{\delta}}\subseteq Q_{\alpha}
as required.

Case
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
,
\alpha
is of the form
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
:

In this case
\vec{\delta}
is of the form
\vec{\delta}=a\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n}\beta^{*}
, where
a
is atomic and

(X,a\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n})\in D_{\beta}
.
In particular, every
\delta_{i}
is a subprogram of
\beta
, and hence a

proper
subprogram of
\alpha
.

Reasoning as in the previous case (but with
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{*}]}\varphi
taking
the role of
\lnot\varphi
) we find a node
u^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{T}
and a pre-state
\rho^{\prime}

such that
u^{\prime}
lies on
\rho^{\prime}
,
t<_{\mathbb{T}}u^{\prime}
,
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{*}]}\varphi\in\Lambda(u^{\prime})
, and
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho^{\prime}))\in Q_{\beta}
.
We now use the
inner
induction hypothesis on
u^{\prime}
,
\rho^{\prime}
and
the loaded formula
\lnot\underline{[\beta^{*}]}\varphi=\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\varphi
.
This gives a node
u
and a pre-state
\rho
such that
u
lies on
\rho
,

\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda(u)
,
u^{\prime}<_{\mathbb{T}}u
and

(\Lambda^{-}(\rho^{\prime}),\Lambda^{-}(\rho))\in Q_{\beta^{*}}
.
From this we immediately obtain
t<_{\mathbb{T}}u
and

(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho))\in Q_{\beta}\mathop{;}Q_{\beta^{*}}\subseteq Q_{\beta^{*}}=Q_{\alpha}
, which means that we are done.

For unloaded diamond formulas we have to work a bit harder.
For this reason we introduce the following auxiliary lemma that will be used to show
48
, the analogue of
46
for unloaded diamonds.

Lemma 47

(
PreState.freeAtomicStep
✓)
.

Let
\pi
be a pre-state with
\lnot[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\varphi\in\Lambda(\pi)
, where
n
is maximal, i.e.,
\varphi
itself is not a box formula.
Then there is a pre-state
\pi^{\prime}={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
such that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{a}
and
\lnot\underline{[\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]}\varphi\in\Lambda(s_{1})
.

Proof 6.9
.

Let
\pi={(t_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq m}
, then we find

\lnot[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\varphi\in\Lambda(t_{m})
by

43
.
We make a case distinction.

Case
\Lambda(t_{m})
is free:

Prover may now simply apply the rule
(L+)
, loading the formula
\lnot[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\varphi
to

\lnot\underline{[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]}\varphi

(recall that
(L+)
loads maximally by definition),
and subsequently apply the modal rule.
The next position in the game will contain the formula

\lnot\underline{[\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]}\varphi
, and any pre-state
\pi^{\prime}
starting at
s_{1}
will meet the criteria of the Lemma.

Case
\Lambda(t_{m})
is loaded:

Prover can now only load the formula
\lnot[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\varphi

if he first
liberates
the loaded formula of
\Lambda(t_{m})
,
by means of an application of the rule
(L-)
.
Recall that we can only apply the rule
(L-)
if
\Lambda(t_{m})
is basic.
This is established by applying
43
to each formula in
\Lambda(t_{m})
.
Let
s
be the child of
t_{m}
in the strategy tree
\mathbb{T}
such that

\Lambda(s)
is the unloaded version of
\Lambda(t_{m})
.
We make a further case distinction.

Case
s
is not a free repeat:

We may now simply continue similarly to the previous case:
Prover may apply the rule
(L+)
at position
s
, etc.

Case
s
is a free repeat:

By definition of a repeat this means that
s
has a companion, that is, an
ancestor

s^{-}
in
\mathbb{T}
such that
\Lambda(s^{-})=\Lambda(s)
.
Let
\rho
be the pre-state such that
s^{-}
is either the final position of
\rho
or obtained from this final position by the unloading rule.
We may now continue as before, finding the required pre-state
\pi^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}
by applying first the loading rule and then the modal rule at the node
s^{-}
.
(Note that by definition of the relation
R_{a}
,
\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime})
is not only an
a
-successor of
\Lambda^{-}(\rho)
, but also of
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
.)

We can now prove the analogue of
46
for non-loaded diamonds.

Lemma 48

(
freeDiamondExistence
✓)
.

Let
\pi
be a pre-state, and let
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi
be a formula in
\Lambda(\pi)
.
Then there is a pre-state
\pi^{\prime}
such that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{\alpha}
and
\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Proof 6.10
.

We will prove the lemma by induction on
\alpha
, but for the induction to work smoothly we need a slightly stronger version of the statement.

Claim
:
Let
\pi
be a pre-state, and let
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi
be a formula in
\Lambda(\pi)
.
Write
\varphi=[\eta_{1}]\cdots[\eta_{k}]\psi
, where
\psi
itself is not a box formula, and define
\underline{\varphi}\mathrel{:=}\underline{[\eta_{1}]\cdots[\eta_{k}]}\psi
.
Then there is a pre-state
\pi^{\prime}
such that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{\alpha}
and
\lnot\varphi
or
\lnot\underline{\varphi}
belongs to
\Lambda(\pi^{\prime})
.

Case
\alpha=a
:

Immediate by
47
.

Case
\alpha={\tau?}
:

It is easy to see that if
\lnot[\tau?]\varphi\in\Lambda(\pi)
, then we have
\{\tau,\lnot\varphi\}\subseteq\Lambda(\pi)
.
From
\tau\in\Lambda(\pi)
it follows that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi))\in Q_{\tau?}
so that we can take
\pi
itself as our
\pi^{\prime}
.

Case
\alpha
is not atomic:

By
44
and
39
there is a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that

X\subseteq\Lambda(\pi)
,
\lnot\Box(\vec{\delta},\varphi)\in\Lambda(\pi)
, and
Q_{X,\vec{\delta}}\subseteq Q_{\alpha}
.

First consider the case where
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
.
This implies that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi))\in Q_{X,\vec{\delta}}\subseteq Q_{\alpha}
and
\lnot\Box(\varepsilon,\underline{\varphi})=\lnot\underline{\varphi}\in\Lambda(\pi)
, so that we are done.

Now consider the case where
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
.
In this case
\vec{\delta}
must be of the form
\vec{\delta}=a\vec{\gamma}
, for some atomic program
a
and some program list
\vec{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}
.
It follows by
47
that there is some pre-state
\rho
such that

(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho))\in Q_{a}

and
\lnot\underline{[\gamma_{1}]\cdots[\gamma_{n}]}\underline{\varphi}\in\Lambda(\rho)
.
But then by
n
iterative applications of
46
we find a pre-state

\pi^{\prime}
such that
\lnot\underline{\varphi}\in\Lambda(\pi^{\prime})
and

(\Lambda^{-}(\rho),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{\gamma_{1}}\mathop{;}\cdots\mathop{;}Q_{\gamma_{n}}.

We note that here we do
not
need the induction hypothesis since we refer to the earlier lemma on loaded formulas.
But from
X\subseteq\Lambda(\pi)
it follows that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi))\in Q_{X}
, so that with
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho))\in Q_{a}
we find that

(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{X}\mathop{;}Q_{a}\mathop{;}Q_{\gamma_{1}}\mathop{;}\cdots\mathop{;}Q_{\gamma_{n}}=Q_{X}\mathop{;}Q_{\vec{\delta}}\subseteq Q_{\alpha}

as required.

Finally, it is easy to see how the Lemma follows from the Claim.

Theorem 49

(
strmg
✓)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be the strategy tree representing a winning strategy for Builder in
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
.
Then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
is a model graph with some state
X\in W
such that
\Gamma\subseteq X
.

Proof 6.11
.

By
Subsection 6.3
,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
includes a state for every pre-state of
\mathbb{T}
.
The root of
\mathbb{T}
is labelled with
\Gamma
, hence there exists a pre-state
\pi
of
\mathbb{T}
such that
\Gamma\subseteq\Lambda^{-}(\pi)\in W
.
It remains to check the four conditions of
Subsection 6.1
to confirm that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
is indeed a model graph.

(a)

For this condition we need to check that each element of
W
is a locally consistent saturated set of formulas.
Let
X
be an element of
W
.
From the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
, it follows that there exists a pre-state
\pi
on
\mathbb{T}
such that
X=\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
.
Thus, we can apply
45
to obtain that
X
is locally consistent and saturated.

(b, c)

Immediate from the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
in
Subsection 6.3
.

(d)

Assume that
X\in W
and
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in X
.
Let
\pi
be any pre-state of
\mathbb{T}
such that
X=\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
.
Then for some node
t
on the pre-state
\pi
we either have
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\in\Lambda(t)
, or else
\lnot[\underline{\alpha}]\psi\in\Lambda(t)
for some (possibly loaded) formula
\psi
such that
\varphi=\psi^{-}
.
To show that there exists a
Y\in W
such that
(X,Y)\in Q_{\alpha}
and

\lnot\varphi\in Y
, we use
48
in the first case and

46
in the second.

6.4.
Completeness proof

We now have all the material that is needed for the completeness proof.

Proof 6.12

(Proof of
37
)
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
is a consistent sequent, i.e., there is no closed tableau for
\Gamma
.
Then by
40
and
42
Builder has a winning strategy
\mathbb{T}
in the tableau game
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
.
By
49
we obtain the model graph
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
with a state
X
such that
\Gamma\subseteq X
.
Using the Truth
38
we find that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}},X\models\Gamma
, hence
\Gamma
is satisfiable.

7.
Interpolation: Preliminaries and Overview

In this section, we provide an overview of the interpolation argument.
In the next section, we define the tableau system that is used; it is an extension of the PDL-tableau system we encountered in the previous sections such that each sequent is formally split into a left and right component. The interpolating formulas are defined in
Section 9
; in the case of proper clusters, this introduces the crucial quasi-tableau construction. Finally, their correctness is established in
Section 10
.

7.1.
Preliminaries

{defi}

[
Formula.voc
]
Let
\xi
be a formula or a program.
The vocabulary
\mathsf{voc}(\xi)
of
\xi
is defined as the set of atomic formulas and atomic programs occurring in
\xi
.
Moreover, for a sequent
\Gamma
, we define
\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma)\mathrel{:=}\bigcup\{\mathsf{voc}(\varphi)\mid\varphi\in\Gamma\}
.

{defi}

[
Interpolant
]
Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be PDL formulas such that
\models\varphi\to\psi
.
An
interpolant
for
\varphi\to\psi
is a formula
\theta
such that

\mathsf{voc}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\varphi)\cap\mathsf{voc}(\psi)
,

\models\varphi\to\theta
, and

\models\theta\to\psi
hold.

We generally use the Greek letter
\theta
to denote interpolants.

With these definitions, we state our main theorem.

Theorem 50

(
interpolation
)
.

PDL has the Craig Interpolation Property.
That is, for all formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
such that
\models\varphi\to\psi
, there exists an interpolant for
\varphi\to\psi
.

A typical and important consequence of the Craig Interpolation Property is the Beth definability property which states that implicit definability implies explicit definability.

{defi}
[
Formula.impDef
,
Formula.expDef
]
A formula
\varphi(p)
is said to
implicitly define

p
in terms of
\mathsf{voc}(\varphi)\setminus\{p\}
, if

\varphi(p_{0}),\varphi(p_{1})\models p_{0}\leftrightarrow p_{1}

where
p_{0},p_{1}\notin\mathsf{voc}(\varphi)
.

A formula
\psi
is an
explicit definition
of
p
relative to
\varphi
, if
\mathsf{voc}(\psi)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\varphi)\setminus\{p\}
and

\varphi(p)\models p\leftrightarrow\psi.

The argument that the Craig Interpolation Property entails the Beth definability property for PDL is similar to that for many other propositional logics.
We refer to the work of Hoogland
[
27
]
for further details and examples of other logics.

Corollary 51

(
beth
)
.

PDL has the Beth definability property.
That is, for any formula
\varphi(p)
that implicitly defines
p
in terms of
\mathsf{voc}(\varphi)\setminus\{p\}
,
there exists an explicit definition of
p
relative to
\varphi
.

Proof 7.1
.

Fix a formula
\varphi(p)
.
Suppose that
\varphi(p_{0}),\varphi(p_{1})\models p_{0}\leftrightarrow p_{1}
.
By
1

\models\varphi(p_{0})\to(\varphi(p_{1})\to(p_{0}\leftrightarrow p_{1}))
.
By propositional reasoning we have:

\models(\varphi(p_{0})\land p_{0})\to(\varphi(p_{1})\to p_{1}).

By
50
, there exists an interpolant, i.e., a formula
\theta
such that:

(0)

\mathsf{voc}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\varphi(p_{0})\land p_{0})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\varphi(p_{1})\to p_{1})

(1)

\models(\varphi(p_{0})\land p_{0})\to\theta

(2)

\models\theta\to(\varphi(p_{1})\to p_{1})

By Property (0), we have that
\{p,p_{0},p_{1}\}\cap\mathsf{voc}(\theta)=\emptyset
.
Hence, by uniformly substituting
p_{0}
with
p
in Property (1), we obtain

\models(\varphi(p)\land p)\to\theta

which by
1
implies

\varphi(p)\models p\to\theta
.
Similarly, from Property (2) we obtain

\models\theta\to(\varphi(p)\to p)

which by
1
implies

\varphi(p)\models\theta\to p
.
Combining these, we obtain

\varphi(p)\models p\leftrightarrow\theta

which means that
\theta
is an explicit definition of
p
relative to
\varphi
.

The proof-theoretic tool that we use in this paper is a tableau calculus. Definitions about the tableau calculus are typically stated in terms of (un)satisfiability rather than validity/provability, so let us restate interpolation in those terms.

{defi}
[
PartInterpolant
]
Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two sequents such that
\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
is unsatisfiable.
An
interpolant
for
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
is a formula
\theta
such that:

•

\mathsf{voc}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
,

•

\Gamma_{1}\models\theta
, and

•

\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\theta
.

The following fact establishes the connection between
Subsection 7.1
and the above:

Fact 52
.

Let
\varphi,\psi
be formulas.
Then
\models\varphi\to\psi
iff
\{\varphi\}\cup\{\lnot\psi\}
is unsatisfiable.
Furthermore, if
\models\varphi\to\psi
, then a formula

\theta
is an interpolant for
\varphi\to\psi

if and only if

\theta
is an interpolant for
\{\varphi\}
and
\{\lnot\psi\}
.

7.2.
An overview of the construction

Before going into the technical details of our construction of the interpolant, we provide a motivating overview.
The reader may want to skip parts of this sketch for now, and come back to it when engaged in the technical details that are to follow.

Let
\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
be an unsatisfiable set of PDL formulas.
The task is to compute an interpolant (
Subsection 7.1
) for
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.
By completeness (
37
) and
Subsection 4.1
, there is a closed PDL-tableau rooted at
\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
.
2
2

2

Readers more familiar with proof systems may interpret this as a proof of
\lnot((\bigwedge\Sigma_{1})\land(\bigwedge\Sigma_{2}))
.

Recognizing the distinct roles of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
in the interpolation statement, the starting point is an extended ‘split PDL-tableau’ system (defined formally in the next section) such that the closed PDL-tableau is rooted at the split sequent
\Sigma_{1};\Sigma_{2}
.
Thus, an interpolant for
\Sigma_{1};\Sigma_{2}
is an interpolant for
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
as per
Subsection 7.1
.

We observe that the semicolon notation is overloaded: previously we saw that it denotes sequential composition of programs — here it is used to demarcate the formulas with ancestry to the first/left component
\Sigma_{1}
and second/right component
\Sigma_{2}
.
Each use of the semicolon is still unambiguous because sequential composition is only applied to programs and never to sequents or formulas.

The tableau rules have the property that at most one component contains a loaded formula; such a component is called the loaded component.
The split sequents
\Sigma_{1};\Sigma_{2}
and
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime};\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}
are considered equal if
\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}
and
\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}
.

The standard proof-theoretic route to interpolation is Maehara’s method (see
[
36
]
and
[
48
, p. 33]
).
The idea is to define interpolants for the leaves of the closed tableau and combine the interpolants that have been obtained at the children of a rule to define an interpolant for its parent.
For example, we add a conjunction or disjunction, or prefix the interpolant obtained in the previous step with a modality. Defining an interpolant for a closed leaf is straightforward (
Subsection 9.1
) because its unsatisfiability is directly evident. E.g., from a leaf labeled
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime},p;\Sigma_{2}^{\prime},\lnot p

we can read off the interpolant
p
, and from
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime},p,\lnot p;\Sigma_{2}
we read off the interpolant
\bot
.
However, a closed PDL-tableau may also contain leaves that are loaded-path repeats. Recall that a loaded-path repeat has the same label as a sequent closer to the root (its companion), and every sequent on the path from companion to loaded-path repeat is loaded. Maehara’s method cannot be applied here because it is not possible to read off an interpolant from the loaded-path repeat: the unsatisfiability of its label is not evident.

Adapting Maehara’s method to cyclic tableaux.

By the above discussion, the real challenge is to construct an interpolant (
Subsection 7.1
) at the companion of a loaded-path repeat in a closed PDL-tableau. It should be noted that a general analysis of such leaves requires a richer graph structure than a branch, to account for the nesting of companion nodes from multiple loaded-path repeats.
For this purpose, we consider a
cluster

C
(
Subsection 4.2
) rooted at
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
; this is a maximal subgraph of the closed tableau that is strongly connected if for each loaded-path repeat we were to add an edge to its companion.
In the following,
we assume that
\Gamma_{1}
is unloaded and
\Gamma_{2}
is loaded
, and construct a formula
\theta
such that
\Gamma_{1}\models\theta
,
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\theta
, and

\mathsf{voc}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.
In the case that it is
\Gamma_{1}
that is loaded and
\Gamma_{2}
is unloaded, we first swap the sides of the cluster so that it is rooted at
\Gamma_{2};\Gamma_{1}
and then run the argument that follows to compute an interpolant. Finally, observe that the negation of an interpolant for
\Gamma_{2};\Gamma_{1}
is an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.
3
3

3

An alternative to swapping the sides of the cluster is observing that the argument constructs a formula
\theta
that is relativized to the loading: the unloaded component entails
\theta
and the loaded component entails
\lnot\theta
.

Let us first consider a
specific situation
where, on the loaded path from
c(t)
to
t
, rules are only applied to the loaded component.
With the aim of reaching a fixed-point equation, we start by assigning
the proposition letter
q_{c(t)}
as the candidate (‘pre-interpolant’) for the interpolant
at
t
.
Note that an interpolant at
t
is also an interpolant at
c(t)
since the two
nodes have the same label.
The idea is to proceed from
t
towards
c(t)
, extending the pre-interpolant as
described below.
We ultimately compute a pre-interpolant
\iota_{c(t)}
at
c(t)
.
We summarize the key ideas for this extension of Maehara’s method in the items

(
E1
)
and
(
E2
)
below, and
(
E3
)
which is introduced after
Subsection 7.2
.

E1

At a local rule in the loaded component, the pre-interpolant for the parent is the
conjunction
4
4

4

A disjunction formula in the loaded component contributes a conjunction to the pre-interpolant because we ultimately want the loaded component
\Gamma_{2}
to entail the
negation
of the interpolant. Meanwhile, a conjunction formula in the loaded component does not change the pre-interpolant since the set of formulas in the sequent is already read conjunctively.

of the pre-interpolants that were computed for the children of that rule.

E2

For a modal rule on atomic program
a
— the rule is applied to the unique loaded formula
\lnot\underline{[a]}\varphi
in the loaded component — the pre-interpolant for the parent is
[a]\iota_{y}
where
\iota_{y}
is the pre-interpolant already computed for the child.

By repeatedly applying the above rules, and using the logical equivalence

[a](\xi\land\chi)\equiv[a]\xi\land[a]\chi
, we have that the pre-interpolant
\iota_{c(t)}
at
c(t)
has
the form
[\alpha]q_{c(t)}\land\psi
, where
q_{c(t)}
does not occur in
\alpha
nor in
\psi
.
Here
\alpha
is obtained as the composition of atomic programs that result from multiple applications of
(
E2
)
.
Since
t
and
c(t)
are identically labeled, they have the same interpolant
so we reach the equation
q_{c(t)}\equiv[\alpha]q_{c(t)}\land\psi
.
Crucially, an equivalence of this form has a solution
q_{c(t)}=[\alpha^{*}]\psi

in PDL, so this is how the pre-interpolant will be defined at companion nodes

(
E3
)
.

So we have resolved the above specific situation where rules on the loaded path were only applied in the loaded component. Of course, in general, rules may also have been applied in the unloaded component, and branching rules in the unloaded component require particular attention. If we were to follow Maehara’s method for such a branching rule, we would define the pre-interpolant for the
parent node as the
disjunction
5
5

5

Observe that a disjunction formula in the unloaded component contributes a disjunction to the pre-interpolant because we ultimately want the unloaded component
\Gamma_{1}
to entail the interpolant.
of the pre-interpolants of its children.
However, when arriving at a companion node, the presence of disjunctions means that the fixed-point equation would not have the desired shape

q_{c(t)}\equiv[\alpha]q_{c(t)}\land\psi
, and in such a case, it is unclear whether the equation even has a solution (in the language of PDL).

To arrive at a solution to this new issue, consider the
special case
where
a branching rule is applied to the unloaded component of a node
t
that has a child
s
that belongs to the cluster
C
, and a child
v
that lies
outside of
C
.
In this case, we may inductively assume that we have already found a proper
interpolant
\theta_{v}
for the node
v
and a pre-interpolant
\iota_{s}
for
s
, and
we define
\iota_{t}\mathrel{:=}[\lnot\theta_{v}?]\iota_{s}
.
This formula is logically equivalent to the disjunction
\theta_{v}\lor\iota_{s}
, but the difference is that we
have now ‘packaged’ the interpolant
\theta_{v}
which contains no occurrence of any

q
-variable inside the boxed test program
{\lnot\theta_{v}?}
.
Consequently, taking advantage of the test operator, the equation that we obtain at the companion node will have the desirable shape we mentioned above. Hence, a solution inside PDL is evident.

{exa}

Consider the closed PDL-tableau in
Figure 10

which consists of a cluster
C
and nodes that are adjacent to the cluster.
Let
C^{+}
be the set of all nodes.
We use the term
exits
to refer to the elements of
C^{+}\setminus C
.

p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
[a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
(M)

p\lor[a^{\ast}]p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
(\Box)

(\lnot\land)
\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p

\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
\lnot\lnot p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p

(\lnot)

(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
p

p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot p
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p

\heartsuit

Figure 10.
A closed tableau for
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
with the highlighted proper cluster
C
.
The interpolants at
C^{+}\setminus C
(already computed) have been indicated within a solid box and placed to the left of the node.

-

Let
t
be the bottom rightmost node in
Figure 10
. It is a leaf that is a loaded-path repeat in the cluster, so assign its pre-interpolant as the variable
q_{c(t)}
.
Observe that
t
is the right child of a
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
rule; by inspection, the left child of the
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
rule has interpolant
p
.

-

The parent of the
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
rule is assigned the pre-interpolant
p\land q_{c(t)}
by
(
E1
)
.

-

The parent of the
(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})
rule belongs to a
\lnot[a^{\ast}]p
-region, i.e., the four nodes whose second component is
\lnot[a^{\ast}]p
.
The significance of regions in the general construction will be explained later on.
There is a single node adjacent to this region that is outside the cluster: the left child of the rule
(\lnot\land)
applied to the unloaded component which, by inspection, has interpolant
\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p
.
The right child of the rule
(\lnot\land)
has pre-interpolant
p\land q_{c(t)}
(this is because the parent of the
(\lnot)
rule is given the same pre-interpolant as its child).
Hence the parent of
(\lnot\land)
has pre-interpolant
[(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?](p\land q_{c(t)})
.
The
(\Box)
rule in the unloaded component is non-branching so it does not change the pre-interpolant either, hence the pre-interpolant at the root of the
\lnot[a^{\ast}]p
-region is
[(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?](p\land q_{c(t)})
.

-

Moving from this
\lnot[a^{\ast}]p
-region to the premise of the
(M)
rule on atomic program
a
, by
E2
we get
[a][(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?](p\land q_{c(t)})
as the pre-interpolant
\iota_{c(t)}
for the root.

Now solve
q_{c(t)}\equiv\iota_{c(t)}
to obtain
q_{c(t)}=[{(a;(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?)}^{\ast}]p
as discussed in the paragraph following
(
E2
)
where a solution is identified for equations of this form.
Call this formula
\theta
.
By inspection:
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)\models\theta
, and
\lnot[a][a^{\ast}]p\models\lnot\theta
.

The trick of replacing a disjunction with a test-boxed formula works if the pre-interpolant of only one of the disjuncts contains an auxiliary
q
-variable. However, we cannot rule out the possibility that both pre-interpolants contain auxiliary variables.
The general solution requires a further abstraction, where the pre-interpolants are computed not for individual nodes, but instead for a region, i.e., the set of nodes in the cluster with the same loaded component.

E3

Let
C_{\Delta}
be the set of nodes in the cluster
C
of which the loaded
component is the set
\Delta
.
By enforcing a
uniformity condition
on the tableau calculus which ensures that the same non-modal rule was applied with the same principal formula to the loaded component of every node
t\in C_{\Delta}
, we may presume that a pre-interpolant
\iota_{y}
corresponding to the region(s) that follow from the application of
the
rule to (all nodes with) the loaded component
\Delta
has been inductively found.
Define
\theta_{\Delta}
as the disjunction of the interpolants from those nodes outside of the cluster but directly adjacent to
C_{\Delta}
— these interpolants exist by the inductive hypothesis, and do not contain any
q
-variables.
Then we define the interpolant for
C_{\Delta}
as
[\lnot\theta_{\Delta}?]\iota_{y}
.

The key observation
in the interpolation proof for PDL is that, in order to
obtain an interpolant for the root of a cluster
C
of a closed split tableau,
we do not need to base the definition of the pre-interpolants on the tree
structure of
C
itself.
Rather we define for each cluster
C
an auxiliary structure
\mathcal{Q}
which we call a
quasi-tableau

since it resembles a tableau.
The idea is then to define pre-interpolants for the nodes of
\mathcal{Q}
, inductively
from leaves to root.
To make this work, we need to synchronize the order in which rules are applied
in
C
: our uniformity condition states that when a non-modal rule is applied
to the loaded component
\Delta
, the same rule must be applied
uniformly throughout
C_{\Delta}
.
Crucially, following the above rules
(
E1
)
to
(
E3
)
,
we make sure that pre-interpolants have a shape that ensures
that we can solve, inside PDL, the equations that appear at the
companion nodes.
Finally, having defined pre-interpolants for every node of the quasi-tableau,
we define the interpolant for the root of the cluster
C
to be the
pre-interpolant that we associate with the root of its quasi-tableau

\mathcal{Q}
.

Why the construction yields an interpolant.

Leaving aside the variable condition for this overview, consider a cluster
C
whose root
r_{C}
is labelled
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, and suppose that
\Gamma_{1}
is unloaded and
\Gamma_{2}
contains a loaded formula
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\varphi
.
Let
\iota_{r}
be the pre-interpolant computed for the root
r
of
\mathcal{Q}
. Let us motivate
\Gamma_{1}\models\iota_{r}
and
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\iota_{r}
.

To establish that
\Gamma_{1}\models\iota_{r}
we prove, by a leaf-to-root
induction on the quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
, that every node
x
in
\mathcal{Q}
satisfies the
following statement, where
\Delta_{x}
is the label of
x
,
t
is any node
in the region
C_{\Delta_{x}}
, and
\sigma
is a substitution that
replaces every internal variable
q_{c(z)}
with a formula representing the
region
C_{\Delta_{c(z)}}
: the unloaded component of
t
entails
\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle
.
Since the pre-interpolant
\iota_{r}
contains no internal variables
and
\Gamma_{i}
is the unloaded component at the root of the cluster, the result follows immediately.

To establish that
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\iota_{r}
we reason towards a
contradiction by assuming that
\Gamma_{2}\land\iota_{r}
is satisfiable, i.e.,
there is a Kripke model
\mathcal{M}
such that

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Gamma_{2},\iota_{r}
for some state
v
in
\mathcal{M}
. Since
\neg[\alpha]\varphi\in\Gamma_{2}
,
it follows that there is a finite path of minimal length (‘witness distance’)
in
\mathcal{M}
to some state
w
such that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\neg\varphi
.
This finite path in
\mathcal{M}
determines a walk through the cluster
C
— it is a walk in the
sense that some nodes in the cluster may be visited multiple times
due to loaded-path repeats — such that at each state
v^{\prime}
on the path in
\mathcal{M}
,
there is a node in
C^{+}
which corresponds to
a
z\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
.
Crucially, the last state
w
on this path in
\mathcal{M}
must correspond to a node
s
in the tableau just outside
C
, i.e.,
s\in C^{+}\setminus C
,
since this is only way to witness
\neg\varphi
i.e.,
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\neg\varphi
.
That implies
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
for
z\in\mathcal{Q}
corresponding to
s
.
While
\iota_{z}
is generally just a pre-interpolant, it is an interpolant in this special case of quasi-tableau nodes corresponding to nodes in the tableaux that are just outside the cluster (as we will show in
68
(a)); intuitively, Maehara’s method is applicable to nodes outside the cluster, so we already have interpolants for nodes just outside
C
.
Given that
\iota_{z}
is an interpolant we thus have
\Delta_{z}\models\lnot\iota_{z}
.
Hence
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\iota_{z},\iota_{z}
. The latter is impossible so we have reached a contradiction.

8.
Split Tableaux

We now define the split PDL-tableau system. The development is analogous to standard PDL-tableau, but we include the formal details for preciseness.

{defi}

A
split sequent
is a pair of sequents (i.e., a pair of finite sets of formulas from
\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}
).
A split sequent
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})
will usually be denoted as
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, and we refer to
\Gamma_{1}

and
\Gamma_{2}
as, respectively, its first or left component, and its second or right component.
If
\Gamma_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) is loaded (i.e., contains a loaded formula), then we call
\Gamma_{i}
loaded, otherwise we call it unloaded.

We now define the notion of a split PDL-tableau; nodes of the tableau are now labeled with split sequents instead of sequents.

{defi}

Given a set
\mathsf{L}
of inference rules, a
split tableau
\mathcal{T}
for a split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
is a tuple
(V,\lessdot,r,\Lambda,L)
where

(V,\lessdot,r)
is a rooted tree,

L
is a function that assigns to every interior node
v\in V
a rule
L(v)\in\mathsf{L}
,

\Lambda
is a function that assigns to every node
v\in V
a split sequent
\Lambda(v)
,
and the split sequent
\Lambda(r)
of the root is equal to
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.
Furthermore, if
u\in V
is an interior node with children
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
then
\Lambda(u)~/~\Lambda(v_{1})|\dots|\Lambda(v_{n})
is a rule instance of the rule
L(u)
(respecting any side conditions).

For a node
v\in V
with
\Lambda(v)=\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, sometimes we will write
\Lambda_{1}(v)
instead of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}(v)
instead of
\Gamma_{2}
.
We will call
\Lambda_{1}(v)
and
\Lambda_{2}(v)
, respectively,
the first or left component of
v
, and the second or right component of
v
.

{defi}

A
local split PDL-tableau
for
\Delta;\Gamma
is a tableau over the following set of rules, called the local split rules:

\Delta,\lnot\lnot\varphi;\Gamma

{(\lnot)}_{1}

\Delta,\varphi;\Gamma

\Delta,\varphi\land\psi;\Gamma

{(\land)}_{1}

\Delta,\varphi,\psi;\Gamma

\Delta,\lnot(\varphi\land\psi);\Gamma

{(\lnot\land)}_{1}

\Delta,\lnot\varphi;\Gamma\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Delta,\lnot\psi;\Gamma

\Delta,[\alpha]\varphi;\Gamma

{(\Box)}_{1}

\alpha
non-atomic

\big\{\Delta,\Sigma\mid\Sigma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)\big\};\Gamma

\Delta,\lnot[\alpha]\varphi;\Gamma

{(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})}_{1}

\alpha
non-atomic

\{\Delta,\Sigma\mid\Sigma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)\};\Gamma

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi;\Gamma

{(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})}_{1}

\alpha
non-atomic

\big\{\Delta,\Sigma\mid\Sigma\in\underline{\mathsf{unfold}}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\xi)\big\};\Gamma

\Delta,\lnot[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi;\Gamma

{(L+)}_{1}

\Delta,\Gamma
free, basic and
n\geq 0
maximal

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi;\Gamma

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi;\Gamma

{(L-)}_{1}

\Delta;\Gamma
basic

\Delta,\lnot[\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi;\Gamma

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot\lnot\varphi

{(\lnot)}_{2}

\Gamma;\Delta,\varphi

\Gamma;\Delta,\varphi\land\psi

{(\land)}_{2}

\Gamma;\Delta,\varphi,\psi

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot(\varphi\land\psi)

{(\lnot\land)}_{2}

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot\varphi\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot\psi

\Gamma;\Delta,[\alpha]\varphi

{(\Box)}_{2}

\alpha
non-atomic

\Gamma;\big\{\Delta,\Sigma\mid\Sigma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)\big\}

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot[\alpha]\varphi

{(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})}_{2}

\alpha
non-atomic

\Gamma;\{\Delta,\Sigma\mid\Sigma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)\}

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi

{(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})}_{2}

\alpha
non-atomic

\Gamma;\big\{\Delta,\Sigma\mid\Sigma\in\underline{\mathsf{unfold}}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\xi)\big\}

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi

{(L+)}_{2}

\Delta,\Gamma
free, basic and
n\geq 0
maximal

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi

{(L-)}_{2}

\Gamma;\Delta
basic

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot[\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi

In the above,
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{F}
,
\alpha
is a non-atomic program,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathcal{P}
,
a
is an atomic program, and
\xi\in\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e.,
\xi
is a possibly loaded formula.

Furthermore, if
i\in\{1,2\}
and
t
is a child of node
s
such that
L(s)={(L+)}_{i}
then
L(t)\neq{(L-)}_{i}
.
That is, if a node was obtained using
{(L+)}_{i}
then it is not allowed to apply
{(L-)}_{i}
immediately to it. The rule
{(L+)}_{i}
is called a
loading rule
, and
{(L-)}_{i}
is called an
unloading
rule.

By some minor modifications of the reasoning in Subsection
3.4
we obtain the following split version of Lemma
24
.

Lemma 53
.

All split local rules are locally sound and locally invertible.

{defi}

The split modal rules are:

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a]}\xi;\Gamma

{(M)}_{1}

\Delta_{a},\lnot\xi;\Gamma_{a}

\Gamma;\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a]}\xi

{(M)}_{2}

\Gamma_{a};\Delta_{a},\lnot\xi

where
\Delta,\Gamma
are basic and
\xi\in\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e.,
\xi
is a possibly loaded formula.

The set of split PDL-rules is then defined as the set of split local rules together with the set of split modal rules.

The rules of the form
{(R)}_{1}
will be called left rules and the rules of the form
{(R)}_{2}
right rules.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a split tableau over the split PDL-rules.
A node
t
is
closed
if
\Lambda_{1}(t)\cup\Lambda_{2}(t)
is closed (see
Subsection 4.1
).
A
repeat
is a node
t
such that
\Lambda_{1}(t)=\Lambda_{1}(s)
and
\Lambda_{2}(t)=\Lambda_{2}(s)
, for some ancestor
s
of
t
;
in this case, the nearest such ancestor to
t
is called its
companion
and denoted
c(t)
.
A node
s
is
free
if
\Lambda_{1}(s)\cup\Lambda_{2}(s)
is a free sequent, and
loaded
otherwise.
A repeat
t
is a
loaded-path repeat
if the path from
c(t)
to
t
consists only of loaded nodes.

A
cluster
is defined analogously to
Subsection 4.2
, as a maximal strongly connected subset of nodes in
\mathcal{T}
after edges are added from each leaf that is a loaded-path repeat to its companion.

{defi}

A
split
PDL-tableau is a tableau over the split PDL-rules, which adheres to the following conditions:

(T1S)

every loaded-path repeat is a leaf;

(T2S)

every free repeat is a leaf.

{defi}

A split PDL-tableau is
closed
if each of its leaves is either closed or a loaded-path repeat.
A split PDL-tableau is
open
if it is not closed.

The notions from
Subsection 4.2
of loaded path, the companion relation

\heartsuit

, the edge relation
\lessdot
, its transitive closure
<
, its reflexive transitive closure
\leq
, the relations
\equiv_{c}
and
<_{c}
, the notions of clusters, proper clusters and singleton clusters can be defined completely analogously for split PDL-tableaux.

Lemma 54
.

Every split PDL-tableau is finite.

Proof 8.1
.

Analogous to
29
, because any split PDL-tableau will only contain finitely many different split sequents, determined by the Fischer-Ladner closure.

The following lemma will be useful to show that the vocabulary condition holds.

Lemma 55
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(V,\lessdot,r,\Lambda,L)
be a tableau over the split PDL-rules and let
s,t\in V
be such that
s\leq t
.
Then,
\mathsf{voc}(\Lambda_{i}(s))\supseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Lambda_{i}(t))
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof 8.2
.

By inspection of the rules.

8.1.
Uniformity

Next, we introduce two
uniformity conditions
for split tableaux, which will be needed to define the pivotal construction in the interpolation proof, viz., that of a
quasi-tableau
(in
Subsection 9.2
).
Informally, the uniformity conditions together say that for loaded nodes, the unloaded component must first be reduced to a basic sequent, and after that, nodes with the same loaded component must synchronize on rules for propositional connectives and the unfold rules
{(\Box)}_{i}
and
{(\Diamond)}_{i}
that are applied in the loaded component. Note that the synchronization does not apply to the loading rules
{(L+)}_{i}
due to the side condition that the
i
-th component is basic. Hence, different diamond formulas can be loaded, so that the modal rule can be applied to different diamond formulas.

{defi}

A split PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
is
uniform
if it satisfies the following conditions:

U1.

If
s\in\mathcal{T}
is such that
\Lambda_{i}(s)
is loaded then a local rule is applied to a formula in the unloaded component of
s
, unless this component is basic;

U2.

If
s,t\in\mathcal{T}
are such that
\Lambda_{i}(s)=\Lambda_{i}(t)
is loaded and not basic, and the unloaded components of both
s
and
t
are basic, then at
s
and at
t
the same rule is applied, to the same formula in the loaded component of the node.

Remark 56
.

Although condition U1 is not explicitly used in the proofs, it simplifies the formulation of condition U2.
In particular, U1 ensures that all rules are applied fully to the unloaded component before synchronizing the application of a rule to the loaded components.
While an alternative notion of uniformity that omits U1 and adjusts U2 accordingly is likely possible, we find using U1 and U2 more intuitive.

50
, the main theorem of the document, follows directly from
59
and the following theorem.

Theorem 57

(
tabToInt
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a uniform, closed split PDL-tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.
Then
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
has an interpolant.

We will prove the theorem by induction on the size (number of nodes) of
\mathcal{T}
, making a case distinction as to whether the root of
\mathcal{T}
belongs to a singleton or a proper cluster.
In the case for proper clusters, we will need the following definition and observation.

{defi}

Let
C
be some cluster of a split PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
.
Then we define

C^{+}\mathrel{:=}C\cup\{s\in T\mid t\lessdot s,\text{ for some }t\in C\}

as the set of nodes that either belong to
C
or are the child of a node in
C
.
We refer to nodes in
C^{+}\setminus C
as
exit nodes
, or simply
exits
.

The following lemma collects some properties of clusters in split tableaux that we will use implicitly from now on.

Lemma 58
.

Let
C
be a proper cluster of a closed split tableau
\mathcal{T}
.

(1)

C
is a subtree
6
6

6

The pair
(T^{\prime},\lessdot^{\prime})
is called a
subtree
of a tree
(T,\lessdot)
if it is a tree such that
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
and
\lessdot^{\prime}\subseteq\lessdot
.

of
(T,\lessdot)
; in particular,
C
has a root.

(2)

Either
\Lambda_{1}(t)
is loaded for every
t\in C
, or

\Lambda_{2}(t)
is loaded for every
t\in C
.

(3)

If
\Lambda_{i}(t)=\emptyset
for some
t\in C
, then

\Lambda_{i}(t)=\emptyset
for all
t\in C
.

Proof 8.3
.

We have (a) since
C
is a (maximal) strongly connected set of nodes (once the edges have been added from a leaf to its companion), and since
T
is a tree.
Part (c) is immediate from an inspection of the rules.
To show (b), we first establish the following claim:

Claim.

If
s\equiv_{c}t
,
s\lhd t
, and
\Lambda_{i}(s)
is loaded, then
\Lambda_{i}(t)
is loaded.

By definition,
s\lhd t
iff
s\lessdot t
or
s\hskip 1.99997pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\heartsuit$}\hskip 1.00006ptt
.
If
s\hskip 1.99997pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\heartsuit$}\hskip 1.00006ptt
, then the sequents of
s
and
t
must be equal (recall this means that the split sequent tuples are equal, so in particular they have the same loaded component).
If
s\lessdot t
, it follows by inspection of the rules that the loading must either remain in the same component, or it must be removed.
If it is removed, then we have that
t
is free, and thus, by
33
(d), that
s\not\equiv_{c}t
.
This establishes the claim.

Since
C
is a proper cluster, there exists an
s\in C
that is loaded.
For all nodes
t
in
C
, there is a
\lhd
-path from
s
to
t
, and hence by induction on path length
using the above Claim, it follows that all nodes in
C
are loaded in the same component as
s
.

8.2.
Soundness and completeness for uniform split tableaux

The following theorem states a soundness and completeness result for (uniform) split PDL-tableaux.
It is on the basis of this result that we can indeed use split tableaux to establish interpolation for PDL.

Theorem 59

(Soundness and completeness for split tableaux.)
.

For all split sequents
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
is not satisfiable;

(2)

there is a closed split tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
;

(3)

there is a uniform, closed split tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

Proof 8.4
.

We prove the implications (1)
\Rightarrow
(2),
(1)
\Rightarrow
(3), (3)
\Rightarrow
(2) and (2)
\Rightarrow
(1).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
Due to the
similarities to the completeness proof in
Section 6
we
confine ourselves to a sketch.

The crucial idea is to modify the tableau game of
Subsection 6.2
.
The
split tableau game

\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
is a two-player graph
game with players Builder and Prover.
A position of
\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
is either:

•

a (possibly loaded) split sequent
\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2}
; or

•

a pair of the form
\big((\Gamma,\varphi,R_{1}),\Delta_{2}\big)
, such that
R_{1}
is a left rule applicable to the split sequent
\Gamma,\varphi;\Delta_{2}

with
\varphi
as the principal formula; or

•

a pair of the form
\big(\Delta_{1},(\Gamma,\varphi,R_{2})\big)
, such that

R_{2}
is a right rule applicable to the split sequent
\Delta_{1};\Gamma,\varphi

with
\varphi
as the principal formula.

Positions of the first type belong to Prover; at a position
\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2}
he picks either a formula
\varphi\in\Delta_{1}
and a left rule

R_{1}
to be applied to
\varphi
, or a formula
\varphi\in\Delta_{2}
and a right
rule
R_{2}
to be applied to
\varphi
.
He thus moves the token to a position of the second or the third type.
Positions of the second type belong to Builder; at a position
\big((\Gamma,\varphi,R_{1}),\Delta_{2}\big)
she picks one of the rule children of
R_{1}
, and
this rule child is then the next position, of the first type.
Positions of the third type also belong to Builder, and her admissible moves are
defined similarly.

A
match
of
\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
consists of a sequence of positions, starting at
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, induced by the players’ moves.
Such a match is
winning for Prover
if it reaches a closed split sequent
or a
loaded-path repeat
.
On the other hand, a match is
winning for Builder
if it reaches a sequent
to which no rule is applicable, or a free repeat.
Recall (
Section 8
) that the notion of a repeat is now defined in terms of split sequents.

Similarly to the simpler game
\mathcal{G}
it is easy to show that full matches of this
game must be finite, so that
\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
is determined.
It is also straightforward to prove that a winning strategy for Prover in

\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
induces a closed split tableau.
Thus the crucial result to prove is the following:

\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}\text{ is satisfiable if Builder has a
winning strategy in }\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}).

(16)

To prove Claim (
16
) we assume that Builder has a winning strategy
in
\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
, and as in
Subsection 6.2
we
represent this winning strategy by its strategy tree
\mathbb{T}=(T,\lessdot_{\mathbb{T}},r,\Lambda)
.
Now,
\Lambda
is a labelling that assigns to each node
s
in
T
a
(possibly loaded)
split
sequent
\Lambda(s)=\Lambda_{1}(s);\Lambda_{2}(s)
.
As before we will construct a
model graph

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
out of
\mathbb{T}
.

For this purpose, we define
pre-states
completely analogously to
Subsection 6.3
.
That is, a pre-state is a maximal sequence
\pi={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
on
\mathbb{T}
such that
s_{1}
is initial; there is no application on
\pi
of a modal rule, a loading rule or an unloading rule; and
\pi
is either a path or the composition
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}
of two paths such that the last node
s
of
\pi^{\prime}
is a repeat leaf, the first node of
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is its companion, and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is an initial segment of the path from
c(s)
to
s
.
Given any path
\pi={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
on
\mathbb{T}
and
h\in\{1,2\}
, we define
\Lambda_{h}(\pi)\mathrel{:=}\bigcup_{i}\Lambda_{h}(s_{i})
and we let
\Lambda^{-}(\pi)
denote the set of formulas we obtain from unloading the set
\Lambda_{1}(\pi)\cup\Lambda_{2}(\pi)
.

The goal is now to prove that this structure
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
is a model graph
indeed, and for this purpose we prove the following
split
versions of

46
,
47
, and
48
.

46
{\,}{}^{s}

Let
t
be a node of
\mathbb{T}
on the pre-state
\pi
, let
h\in\{1,2\}
, and
let
\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\varphi
be a loaded formula in
\Lambda_{h}(t)
.
Then there is a node
u
on
\mathbb{T}
such that
t<_{\mathbb{T}}u
,
\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda_{h}(u)
and
u
lies on some pre-state
\rho
such that

(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\rho))\in Q_{\alpha}
.

47
{\,}{}^{s}

Let
\pi
be a pre-state and let
h\in\{1,2\}
be such that
\lnot[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]\varphi\in\Lambda_{h}(\pi)
, where
n
is maximal, i.e.,
\varphi
itself is not a box formula.
Then there is a pre-state
\pi^{\prime}={(s_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq k}
such that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{a}
and
\lnot\underline{[\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]}\varphi\in\Lambda_{h}(s_{1})
.

48
{\,}{}^{s}

Let
\pi
be a pre-state, let
h\in\{1,2\}
and let
\lnot[\alpha]\varphi
be a
formula in
\Lambda_{h}(\pi)
.
Then there is a pre-state
\pi^{\prime}
such that
(\Lambda^{-}(\pi),\Lambda^{-}(\pi^{\prime}))\in Q_{\alpha}
and
\lnot\varphi\in\Lambda_{h}^{-}(\pi^{\prime})
.

In all three cases, the proof of the split lemma is
almost verbatim
the same as the proof of the original statement: the only modification is to replace at some spots a set
\Lambda(t)
or
\Lambda(\pi)
with its left- or right-hand side version
\Lambda_{h}(t)
or
\Lambda_{h}(\pi)
.

From the ‘split lemmas’,
46
s
,

47
s
and
48
s
, it easily follows (as in the proof of

49
) that the structure
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{T}}
is a model graph,
and from this the statement Claim (
16
) follows in the same manner as
in
Subsection 6.2
.
This means that we are done, since, as we saw, Claim (
16
) is the crucial
statement needed to prove the implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(2).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3)
This is a minor variation of
the proof of the implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(2).
The idea is simply to tweak the rules of the split tableau game
\mathcal{G}_{s}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
in such a way that a winning strategy for Prover in the resulting
game
\mathcal{G}_{t}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
induces a closed split tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
that in addition is
uniform
.
This is quite simple: we restrict Prover’s admissible rules at a position

\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2}
in the following way:

•

if one of the components is loaded and the unloaded component is not basic, then Prover must pick a formula from the unloaded component;

•

if one of the components is loaded but not basic, and the unloaded component is basic, then we require Prover to pick the smallest admissible and unloaded formula in the loaded component with respect to some arbitrary but fixed order on the formulas in
\mathit{FL}(\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2})
.
