Clearly, the absence of a uniform, closed split tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
implies the non-existence of a winning strategy for Prover in the tweaked game
\mathcal{G}_{t}(\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2})
.
Analogous to
41
and
42
for
\mathcal{G}
we can also show that
\mathcal{G}_{t}
is determined, hence there must exist a winning strategy for Builder in the tweaked game. The construction of a model graph from this winning strategy proceeds from the split versions of

46
,
47
, and
48
as in (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) above. Indeed, observe that the saturation in the definition of model graph (
Subsection 6.1
) is a closure condition so the order in which formulas are picked does not matter as long as they are eventually picked (
43
remains true so the latter holds).

(3)
\Rightarrow
(2)

This follows trivially by ignoring the uniformity property.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)

This statement comprises the soundness of the split tableau system, and, similarly to the completeness case, its proof is a routine variation of the soundness proof of the unsplit system.
We only provide a brief sketch.

Our argumentation for soundness roughly consists of two parts: a check that the split versions of the rules remain locally sound, and a “global” proof that any sequent in a closed split tableau is unsatisfiable.

The first task is a minor variation of the corresponding analysis in the unsplit case. Note that all the intricacies there revolve around the
(\Box)
and
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
rules, and the key argument concerns the properties of the
\mathsf{unfold}
operations.
Whether the context of an application of
(\Box)
and
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
consists of a split or an unsplit sequent is not relevant for the argument.

Similarly, the second task can be executed by almost verbatim following the argumentation in Section
5
. To provide a bit more detail, in the soundness proof we formulate and prove split versions of Lemmas
35
and
36
, and then word by word follow the reasoning in the “Proof of Theorem
34
” part.
For the proofs of said split versions of Lemmas
35
and
36
, the core argument concerns an analysis of the development of loaded formulas in a closed tableau. Again, whether this development takes place in the context of split or unsplit sequents is of no consequence.

9.
Defining Interpolants

9.1.
Interpolants for singleton clusters

In this subsection, we consider the relatively easy case where the tableau root forms a singleton cluster.
That is, we consider a cluster with respect to
\equiv_{c}
as defined in
Subsection 4.2
that consists of a single node.

Lemma 60
.

Let the tableau
\mathcal{T}
for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
consist of a single, closed node.
Then
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
has an interpolant.

Proof 9.1
.

If the split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
is closed, we must have at least one of
the following cases:

•

\bot\in\Gamma_{1}
or
\{\varphi,\lnot\varphi\}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}
for some formula
\varphi
:

then we have
\Gamma_{1}\models\bot
and
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\bot
, hence let
\theta\mathrel{:=}\bot
.

•

\bot\in\Gamma_{2}
or
\{\varphi,\lnot\varphi\}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}
for some formula
\varphi
:

then we have
\Gamma_{1}\models\lnot\bot
and
\Gamma_{2}\models\bot
, hence let
\theta\mathrel{:=}\lnot\bot
.

•

\varphi\in\Gamma_{1}
and
\lnot\varphi\in\Gamma_{2}
for some formula
\varphi
:

then we have
\Gamma_{1}\models\varphi
and
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\varphi
, hence let
\theta\mathrel{:=}\varphi
.

•

\lnot\varphi\in\Gamma_{1}
and
\varphi\in\Gamma_{2}
for some formula
\varphi
:

then we have
\Gamma_{1}\models\lnot\varphi
and
\Gamma_{2}\models\varphi
, hence let
\theta\mathrel{:=}\lnot\varphi
.

Moreover, in all cases we obviously have
\mathsf{voc}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.
Consequently, in all cases we have defined an interpolant
\theta
for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

Lemma 61

(
localInterpolantStep
✓)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a uniform closed tableau for the split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
,
and assume that the root
r
of
\mathcal{T}
forms a singleton cluster.
Furthermore, assume that for every child
t
of
r
we already have obtained an
interpolant
\theta_{t}
for the split sequent
\Lambda_{1}(t);\Lambda_{2}(t)
.
Then
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
has an interpolant.

Proof 9.2
.

We make a case distinction as to the rule that is applied at the root of
\mathcal{T}
.

If the
n
children of
r
are obtained using a rule different from
(M)
, then we have
one of the following cases:

•

A rule was applied in the second component.
The children of
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
are then of the form
\Gamma_{1};\Pi_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{1};\Pi_{n}
,
and we have
\Gamma_{1}\models\theta_{1}\land\ldots\land\theta_{n}
.
Moreover
\Gamma_{2}
only holds in a state in which also one of the
\Pi_{i}
holds due to the fact that all local rules are locally sound by
24
.
But then from
\Pi_{i}\models\lnot\theta_{i}
for each
i\leq n
, we get that
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\theta_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\lnot\theta_{n}
, and thus
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot(\theta_{1}\land\ldots\land\theta_{n})
.
Let
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta_{1}\land\ldots\land\theta_{n}
.
Because we have
\mathsf{voc}(\Pi_{i})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
for each
i
, it follows that
\mathsf{voc}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.
Hence
\theta
is an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

•

A rule was applied in the first component.
The children of
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
are then of the form
\Pi_{1};\Gamma_{2},\ldots,\Pi_{n};\Gamma_{2}
,
and we have
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\theta_{1}\land\ldots\land\lnot\theta_{n}
,
and thus
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot(\theta_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\theta_{n})
.
Moreover,
\Gamma_{1}
only holds in a state in which also one of the
\Pi_{i}
holds.
From
\Pi_{i}\models\theta_{i}
for each
i\leq n
we get that
\Gamma_{1}\models\theta_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\theta_{n}
.
Hence let
\theta\mathrel{:=}\theta_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\theta_{n}
.
Because we have
\mathsf{voc}(\Pi_{i})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})
for each
i
, it follows that
\mathsf{voc}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.
Hence
\theta
is an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

Before we consider the case for the
(M)
rule, we note that
\Gamma_{a}\models\varphi
implies
\Gamma\models[a]\varphi
, for any atomic program
a
.
To see this, suppose
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Gamma
for some pointed model
(\mathcal{M},v)
.
Then we have
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Gamma_{a}
and thus
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\varphi
for all
w
from
\mathcal{M}
with
v\xrightarrow{a}w
.
But this means we have
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash[a]\varphi
.

Moreover
\Gamma_{a},\lnot\psi\models\varphi
implies
\Gamma,\lnot[a]\psi\models\lnot[a]\lnot\varphi
, because if we have
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Gamma,\lnot[a]\psi
for some pointed model
(\mathcal{M},v)
, then there exists a
w\in\mathcal{M}
with
v\xrightarrow{a}w
and
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\Gamma_{a},\lnot\psi
.
By the assumption, it follows that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\varphi
, and hence
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\lnot[a]\lnot\varphi
.

If now
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
has a child
\Pi_{1};\Pi_{2}
with an interpolant
\theta
which is obtained using the modal rule
(M)
, then we have one of the following cases:

•

\Gamma_{1}
contains a loaded formula of the form
\lnot\underline{[a]}\chi
.
Then we have
\Pi_{1};\Pi_{2}=\lnot\chi,{(\Gamma_{1})}_{a};{(\Gamma_{2})}_{a}
, and a
\theta
such that

\lnot\chi,{(\Gamma_{1})}_{a}\models\theta
and
{(\Gamma_{2})}_{a}\models\lnot\theta
.

If
\Pi_{2}
is non-empty, then
a\in\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.
As shown above,
\lnot\chi,{(\Gamma_{1})}_{a}\models\theta
implies
\Gamma_{1}\models\lnot[a]\lnot\theta
(since
\lnot[a]\chi\in\Gamma_{1}
),
and
{(\Gamma_{2})}_{a}\models\lnot\theta
implies
\Gamma_{2}\models[a]\lnot\theta
.
Hence,
\lnot[a]\lnot\theta
is an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

If
\Pi_{2}
is empty, then
\Gamma_{1}
is not satisfiable.
To see this, simply notice that the split tableau obtained from
\mathcal{T}
by only changing the label of the root to
\Gamma_{1};\emptyset
is also a closed tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\emptyset
,
and thus by
59
, we get that
\Gamma_{1}
is inconsistent.
Then we have
\Gamma_{1}\models\bot
and
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\bot
, and
\bot
is an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

•

\Gamma_{2}
contains a loaded formula of the form
\lnot\underline{[a]}\chi
.
Then we have
\Pi_{1};\Pi_{2}={(\Gamma_{1})}_{a};\lnot\chi,{(\Gamma_{2})}_{a}
, and a
\theta
such that

{(\Gamma_{1})}_{a}\models\theta
and
\lnot\chi,{(\Gamma_{2})}_{a}\models\lnot\theta
.

If
\Pi_{1}
is non-empty, then
a\in\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.
As shown above,
{(\Gamma_{1})}_{a}\models\theta
implies
\Gamma_{1}\models[a]\theta
,
and
\lnot\chi,{(\Gamma_{2})}_{a}\models\lnot\theta
implies
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot[a]\theta
(since
\lnot[a]\chi\in\Gamma_{2}
).
Hence,
[a]\theta
is an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

Analogous to the previous case, if
\Pi_{1}
is empty, then
\Gamma_{2}
is not satisfiable.
Then we have
\Gamma_{1}\models\lnot\bot
and
\Gamma_{2}\models\bot
, and
\lnot\bot
is an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

This shows the lemma.

9.2.
Defining interpolants for proper clusters

Next, we take care of the more difficult case of defining interpolants for roots of proper clusters,
assuming that interpolants have been found for all exits of the cluster.
The formal result is stated in
62
, which provides the inductive step for proper clusters in the proof of

57
.

Lemma 62

(
clusterInterpolation
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a uniform closed split PDL-tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, and assume that the root
r
of
\mathcal{T}
belongs to a proper
cluster
C
.
Furthermore, assume that for all exit nodes
t\in C^{+}\setminus C
,
we have an interpolant
\theta_{t}
for the split sequent

\Lambda_{1}(t);\Lambda_{2}(t)
.
Then
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
has an interpolant.

In the remainder of this section and in
Section 10
, let
\mathcal{T}
be a uniform closed split PDL-tableau for

\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
and let
C
be a proper cluster of
\mathcal{T}
such that the root
r
of
\mathcal{T}
is the root of
C
.
We first consider the case where
\Gamma_{2}
is loaded, and then in the proof of
62
we will show how to define the interpolant for the case where
\Gamma_{1}
is loaded.

Fix
\mathcal{T},C,r,\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
for the rest of
Section 9
and for
Subsection 10.1
.

where
\Gamma_{2}
is assumed to be loaded.
Here are some first observations on
C
.

Lemma 63
.

For all
t\in C
, the following hold:

(1)

\Lambda_{2}(t)
is loaded (and thus, nonempty).

(2)

\Lambda_{1}(t)
is empty iff
\Gamma_{1}
is empty.

(3)

All children of
t
are in
C^{+}
. If
t
is not a loaded-path repeat then at least one of them belongs to
C
,
and if
t
is a loaded-path repeat then its companion
c(t)
belongs to
C
.

Proof 9.3
.

We fix any
t\in C
and show the three claims.

(1)

By the assumptions, the root of cluster
C
is labelled
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{2}
is loaded.
Since every node in
C
lies on a path from some companion to its repeat, it follows that every node in
C
is loaded.
Since loading can only change components by first unloading, the loading cannot change sides inside
C
.

(2)

If
\Gamma_{1}
is empty then the label
\Lambda_{1}(r)
of the first component at the root is empty.
No rule introduces formulas into an empty component, so also
\Lambda_{1}(t)
must be empty.

(3)

The first part of the claim is immediate from the definition of
C^{+}
.
The second part holds since otherwise
t
would not belong to
C
.

Remark 64
.

In case
\Gamma_{1}=\emptyset
, we can simply define the interpolant for
r
as
\theta_{r}\mathrel{:=}\top
.
In the remainder of this section we will therefore mainly consider the case where
\Gamma_{1}\neq\emptyset
(but in the key definitions and lemmas we will briefly cover the case where
\Gamma_{1}=\emptyset
as well).
Note that
\Gamma_{1}\neq\emptyset
implies that
\Lambda_{1}(t)
is nonempty for all
t\in C
.

It will be convenient to introduce some further notation.
Note that given any map we use square brackets for its direct image map, so that
\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\mathrel{:=}\{\Lambda_{2}(t)\mid t\in C^{+}\}
is the set of (loaded) sequents that occur as second component in
C^{+}
, and similarly for
\Lambda_{2}[C]
.

{defi}

Given a sequent
\Delta\in\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]
, we define

\begin{array}[]{lll}C_{\Delta}&\mathrel{:=}&\{t\in C\mid\Lambda_{2}(t)=\Delta\},\\
C_{\Delta}^{+}&\mathrel{:=}&\{t\in C^{+}\mid\Lambda_{2}(t)=\Delta\},\end{array}

and we let
C_{\Delta}^{L}
(
C_{\Delta}^{R}
) denote the set of nodes in
C_{\Delta}
where a left rule (a right rule) is applied.
For example,
t\in C_{\Delta}^{L}
means that
t
is a node in
C
whose second component is
\Delta
— here
\Delta
is a sequent that occurs as the second component of some node in
C^{+}
— with the property that a left rule was applied at
t
.

Below we gather some further observations.

Lemma 65
.

For all sequents
\Delta\in\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]
, the following hold:

(1)

C_{\Delta}=C_{\Delta}^{L}\cup C_{\Delta}^{R}
and
C_{\Delta}^{L}\cap C_{\Delta}^{R}=\emptyset
.

(2)

If
t\in C_{\Delta}^{L}
then
\Lambda_{1}(t)
is not basic.

(3)

If
t\in C_{\Delta}^{L}
then all of its children belong to
C_{\Delta}^{+}
, and at least one to
C_{\Delta}
.

(4)

If
C_{\Delta}
is not empty then
C^{R}_{\Delta}
is not empty.

(5)

If
\Delta
is basic and
t\in C_{\Delta}^{R}
then the modal rule is applied at
t
to the loaded formula in
\Lambda_{2}(t)=\Delta
, and the unique child of
t
also belongs to
C
.

(6)

If
\Delta
is not basic and
C^{R}_{\Delta}\neq\emptyset
then there is a unique local rule
R_{2}
and a possibly loaded formula
\xi\in\Delta
such that for all
t\in C_{\Delta}^{R}
, the rule
R_{2}
with principal formula
\xi
is applied at
t
.
Consequently, there are sequents
\Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{n}
such that

\bigwedge\Delta\equiv\bigvee_{i}\bigwedge\Pi_{i}
, and, for all
t\in C_{\Delta}^{R}
, the children of
t
can be listed as
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
such that
\Lambda(s_{i})=\Lambda_{1}(t);\Pi_{i}
, for all
i
.

Proof 9.4
.

Since every rule is either a left or a right rule but not both, we have (a).
Item (b) follows from the earlier observation that
\Lambda_{2}(t)
is loaded, and thus,
\Lambda_{1}(t)
is free; if a rule on the first component was applied at
t
, then it cannot be basic.
Item (c) is an instantiation of
63
(c), together with the observation that, obviously, the rule applied at any
t\in C_{\Delta}^{L}
is a left rule, distinct from
(M)
, and hence does not touch
\Delta
.

We prove item (d):
Assume that
C_{\Delta}\neq\emptyset
.
Then there exists
t\in C_{\Delta}
such that
t
is minimal in the tree relation, that is, there is no
t^{\prime}\in C_{\Delta}
such that
t^{\prime}<t
.
Moreover, since all nodes in
C
are loaded in the second component by
63
(a), it follows that
\Delta
is loaded.
If
t\in C^{R}_{\Delta}
then we are done. So suppose
t\notin C^{R}_{\Delta}
.
We will show that there exists
u\in C_{\Delta}
such that
t<u
,
\Lambda_{1}(u)
is basic and
u
is not a loaded-path repeat.
That implies that a rule was applied at
u
and that it was not a rule on the first (i.e., left) component. Since every rule is a left or right rule (Item (a)), it follows that
u\in C^{R}_{\Delta}
.
This
u
is obtained as follows.
Since
t\notin C^{R}_{\Delta}
, Item (a) implies that
t\in C^{L}_{\Delta}
, i.e., some left rule is applied at
t
. By Item (c), there is a child
v
of
t
that belongs to
C_{\Delta}
. By repeating this argument until no more left rules can be applied, we obtain a path
v_{0}v_{1}\cdots v_{n}
in
C_{\Delta}
such that
t=v_{0}
and
\Lambda_{1}(v_{n})
is basic, and for all
i<n
we have
v_{i}\in C^{L}_{\Delta}
.
We now prove that for all
i\leq n
, the node
v_{i}
cannot be a loaded-path repeat. Suppose towards a contradiction that
v_{i}
is a loaded-path repeat. Then by minimality of
t
we must have
t\leq c(v_{i})<v_{i}
.
Hence the unique path from
c(v_{i})
to
v_{i}
in
\mathcal{T}
must be a segment of the path
v_{0}v_{1}\cdots v_{n}
.
By
28
, the path from
c(v_{i})
to
v_{i}
must include an application of the modal rule,
but since only left rules are applied along this path, and the loaded formula is in the right component, this is impossible.
Hence we have a contradiction, and conclude that no
v_{i}
is a loaded-path repeat, so we can take
u=v_{n}
.

The first observation of Item (e) follows from the fact that the right component of
t
is loaded; the second observation is another instance of
63
(c).

Finally, for Item (f), suppose that
\Delta
is not basic and
C^{R}_{\Delta}\neq\emptyset
.
By uniformity, the same (right) rule is applied at every
t\in C^{R}_{\Delta}
, and since
\Delta=\Lambda_{2}(t)
is not basic, this rule must be local, and therefore only affects the right component
\Lambda_{2}(t)
.
The rest of the statement in Item (f) then follows from the local soundness and invertibility of the rule applied at
t
(
53
).

The next definition introduces the pivotal structure of the interpolation proof.
The basic idea is to represent the cluster
C
by a finite labeled tree
\mathcal{Q}
of which the nodes are labeled with
right-side sequents only
.
Every node
x
in
\mathcal{Q}
will represent a certain subset
R_{x}\subseteq C^{+}
(to be defined in
Subsection 9.2
).

{defi}

Step by step we will construct a structure
\mathcal{Q}=(Q,\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}},k,\Delta)
that we will refer to as the
quasi-tableau
associated with the cluster
C
.
Here,
(Q,\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}})
is a finite tree,
k\colon Q\to\{1,2,3\}
assigns a

type

k(x)
to every node
x\in Q
, and
\Delta
is a map that labels every node

x\in Q
with a finite sequent
\Delta_{x}\in\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]
.

We construct
\mathcal{Q}
starting from the root, and maintain the following invariant for all nodes
x\in Q
:
If
x
is not a leaf then
C_{\Delta_{x}}\neq\emptyset
. (Note that
C_{\Delta_{x}}\neq\emptyset
iff
\Delta_{x}\in\Lambda_{2}[C]
.)

To start the construction, we add the root node
r_{\mathcal{Q}}
to
\mathcal{Q}
and let
r_{\mathcal{Q}}
have type 1 and
label
\Gamma_{2}
, i.e.,
\Delta_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}\mathrel{:=}\Gamma_{2}=\Lambda_{2}(r)
is the loaded right sequent of the
root
r
of
C
. Since
r\in C_{\Gamma_{2}}
, the invariant holds at the root of
\mathcal{Q}
.

Inductively, given a node
x\in Q
, the set of children of
x
is defined
by means of the following case distinction.
In each case, we show that if
x
satisfies the invariant, then so do all its children.

Case
k(x)=1
:

If
\Delta_{x}\in\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\setminus\Lambda_{2}[C]
, or if
there exists
z\in Q
such that
z
is an ancestor of
x
and
\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{z}
,
then
x
is a leaf.
In the latter case, we call
x
a
repeat
.
In this case, the invariant holds trivially.
Otherwise, that is, if
x
is not a repeat and
\Delta_{x}\in\Lambda_{2}[C]
,
then
x
has a unique child
y
with type 2 and label
\Delta_{x}
.
In this case the invariant holds at
y
since
\Delta_{y}=\Delta_{x}\in\Lambda_{2}[C]
.

Case
k(x)=2
:

x
has a unique child
y
with type 3 and label
\Delta_{x}
.
The invariant holds at
y
since
\Delta_{y}=\Delta_{x}
and the invariant holds at
x
.

Case
k(x)=3
:

If
\Delta_{x}
is not basic, then by the invariant for
x
, we have
C_{\Delta_{x}}\neq\emptyset
.
Hence by
65
(d),
C^{R}_{\Delta_{x}}\neq\emptyset
, and
by
65
(f),
there is a unique local rule
R_{2}
and a unique, possibly loaded formula
\xi\in\Delta_{x}
such that
R_{2}
with principal formula
\xi
is applied uniformly at all
t\in C^{R}_{\Delta_{x}}
.
Let
\Delta_{x}\,/\,\Pi_{1}|\ldots|\Pi_{n}
be the rule instance of the non-split version of
R_{2}
with principal formula
\xi
.
We define the set of children of
x
in
\mathcal{Q}
as the set
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}
, where each
y_{i}
has type 1 and label
\Pi_{i}
.
We show that the invariant holds for all
y_{i}
:
If
y_{i}
is not a leaf in
\mathcal{Q}
, then since
k(y_{i})=1
it follows from the above item for
k(x)=1
that
\Delta_{y_{i}}\in\Lambda_{2}[C]
.

If
\Delta_{x}
is basic and the unique loaded formula in

\Delta_{x}
is of the form
\lnot\underline{[a][\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]}\varphi
,
then
x
has a unique child
y
with type 1 and label

{(\Delta_{x})}_{a}\cup\{\underline{\neg[\delta_{1}]\cdots[\delta_{n}]}\varphi\}
.
We show that the invariant holds at
y
: Since it holds at
x
, i.e.,

C_{\Delta_{x}}\neq\emptyset
,
by
65
(d), there exists
t\in C^{R}_{\Delta_{x}}
,
and by
65
(e), the unique child
v
of
t
belongs to
C
.
Since
v
is obtained by applying the modal rule
{(M)}_{2}
at
t
,
it follows that
\Delta_{y}=\Lambda_{2}(v)
.
Hence
\Delta_{y}\in\Lambda_{2}[C]
.

Remark 66
.

Note that termination of the construction at a node
z\in Q
takes place in one of two ways: either
z
is a repeat in
\mathcal{Q}
or
\Delta_{z}\in\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\setminus\Lambda_{2}[C]
(observe that
\Delta_{z}
must be unloaded in the latter case).
In both cases,
k(z)=1
.

Before discussing the role of the quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
in the interpolation
proof, we first gather some basic definitions and notations related to it.

As mentioned, each
x\in Q
represents a subset
R_{x}
of
C^{+}
with fixed loaded component.
If
x
has type 1 or 2 then
R_{x}
consists of all nodes in
C^{+}
that have the same loaded (right) component as
x
.
If
x
has type 3 then
R_{x}
consists of all nodes in
C
that have the same loaded (right) component as
x
, and moreover a rule is applied in the loaded (right) component.

{defi}

For
x\in Q
, we define the set (sometimes called a
region
)
R_{x}\subseteq C^{+}
as follows:

R_{x}\mathrel{:=}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{\Delta_{x}}^{+}&\text{if }k(x)\in\{1,2\}\\[2.84526pt]
C_{\Delta_{x}}^{R}&\text{if }k(x)=3.\end{array}\right.

Recall that a repeat in
\mathcal{Q}
is defined in
Subsection 9.2
.

{defi}

We let
<_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\leq_{\mathcal{Q}}
denote, respectively, the
transitive and the reflexive-transitive closure of the relation
\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Along any branch of
\mathcal{Q}
, by construction, the sequent label changes only when transitioning from a node of type 3 to a node of type 1. Furthermore, repeats are identified at the first opportunity, since they are defined in case
k(x)=1
of
Subsection 9.2
.
Consequently, for every repeat leaf
x\in Q
, there is a unique node
z\in Q
such that
z<_{\mathcal{Q}}x
,
\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{z}
and
k(x)=k(z)=1
.
Such a node
z
is called the
companion of
x
, and it is denoted
c(x)
.

We let
K_{\mathcal{Q}}=\{c(x)\mid x\in Q\text{ is a repeat leaf}\}
denote the
set of companions
.
Finally,
L_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
r_{\mathcal{Q}}
denote, respectively, the set of leaves and the root of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Given a node
x\in Q
, we set

L_{<x}\mathrel{:=}\{z\in L_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid z\text{ is a repeat leaf and }c(z)<_{\mathcal{Q}}x\leq_{\mathcal{Q}}z\}.

In words,
L_{<x}
denotes the set of repeat leaves that are descendants of
x
and whose companion node is a proper ancestor of
x
.

The following lemma follows immediately from the previous definitions.

Lemma 67
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a quasi-tableau and let
x,y\in Q
. Then:

(1)

If
x
is a repeat leaf or a companion node in
\mathcal{Q}
, then
k(x)=1
.

(2)

L_{<r_{\mathcal{Q}}}=\emptyset
.

(3)

If
x<_{\mathcal{Q}}y
, then
L_{<x}\subseteq L_{<y}
.

(4)

If
x
is not a companion and
x\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}y
, then
L_{<x}=L_{<y}
.

Proof 9.5
.

Items (a), (b) and (c) are immediate from the definitions.
We show Item (d).

Let
z\in L_{\mathcal{Q}}
be a repeat leaf.
We have that
c(z)<_{\mathcal{Q}}y
iff
c(z)<_{\mathcal{Q}}x
, since
x
is not a companion.
From
x\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}y
, it follows that
x\notin L_{\mathcal{Q}}
, and from this that
y\leq_{\mathcal{Q}}z
iff
x\leq_{\mathcal{Q}}z
.
Hence we can conclude that
L_{<x}=L_{<y}
.

We are now ready to define interpolants.
The key idea is to define for each node
x
in the quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
a so-called
pre-interpolant

\iota_{x}
.
The definition of pre-interpolants proceeds by a leaf-to-root
induction on the tree
(Q,\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}})
, and ensures that
the pre-interpolant
\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
of the root
r_{\mathcal{Q}}
of
\mathcal{Q}
is
an interpolant for the root
r
of
C
. Hence, to obtain the main result, we can take
\theta_{r}=\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
.
(In the case where
\Gamma_{1}\neq\emptyset
, that is; for the case
where
\Gamma_{1}
is empty we refer to
64
.)

To start the definition of the pre-interpolants, in the base case we need to
consider the
leaves
of
\mathcal{Q}
.
As noted in
66
, a leaf
x
has type 1, and there are two possibilities:

x
is a repeat or
\Delta_{x}
belongs to
\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\setminus\Lambda_{2}[C]
.
In the second case, it follows that
R_{x}\subseteq C_{\Delta_{x}}^{+}\setminus C
, i.e.,

x
represents a region of exit nodes.
Recall that by the assumption in
62
we already have an
interpolant
\theta_{t}
for every node
t\in C^{+}\setminus C
.
This guarantees the correctness of the following definition, and the subsequent observation.

{defi}

Given a sequent
\Delta\in\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]
, we define

\theta_{\Delta}\mathrel{:=}\bigvee\{\theta_{t}\mid t\in C^{+}_{\Delta}\setminus C_{\Delta}\}.

The following fact states that the formula
\theta_{\Delta}
behaves as a “region” interpolant for the set
C^{+}_{\Delta}\setminus C_{\Delta}
of exit nodes that have
\Delta
as the second component.
(Note that if
C^{+}_{\Delta}\setminus C_{\Delta}=\emptyset
then
\theta_{\Delta}\equiv\bot
.)

Lemma 68
.

Let
\Delta
be a sequent in
\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]
.
Then we have:

(1)

\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\theta_{\Delta}
, for all
t\in C^{+}_{\Delta}\setminus C_{\Delta}
.

(2)

\Delta\models\lnot\theta_{\Delta}
.

(3)

\mathsf{voc}(\theta_{\Delta})\subseteq\left(\bigcup_{t\in C^{+}_{\Delta}\setminus C_{\Delta}}\mathsf{voc}(\Lambda_{1}(t))\right)\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Delta)
.

Proof 9.6
.

All three statements in the lemma are straightforward consequences of the assumption that each formula
\theta_{t}
is an interpolant.
For instance, this assumption means that
\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\theta_{t}
, for all
t\in C^{+}_{\Delta}\setminus C_{\Delta}
, and so
\theta_{\Delta}
, the disjunction of these
\theta_{t}
, is also a consequence of each
\Lambda_{1}(t)
.

Continuing our discussion of the base case of the inductive definition of the pre-interpolants, we now consider the leaves that are repeats.
For these we introduce some new propositional variables that we shall call
internal
, and that will also feature in the pre-interpolants of the internal nodes of
\mathcal{Q}
(but not in the one of the root).
More precisely, we will need a propositional variable
q_{x}
for every companion node
x
in
Q
.
For the specific syntactic shape of the pre-interpolants, we need to introduce some terminology.

{defi}

The set
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
of formulas (from which we will take the pre-interpolants)
is given by the following grammar:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}\ni\iota\,\,\Coloneqq\,\,\psi\mid q\mid\iota\land\iota\mid\Box(\vec{\alpha},\iota)

where
q\in\{q_{x}\mid x\in K_{\mathcal{Q}}\}
(called the set of internal variables), and
\psi
and
\vec{\alpha}
are such that their vocabulary contains no internal variables.
Formulas in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
will be referred to as
\mathcal{Q}
-formulas
.

A formula in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is
simple
if it is of the form
\psi
or
\Box(\vec{\alpha},q_{x})
, and
in normal form
if it is a conjunction of such formulas.

We now define a normal form for every formula
\iota\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

{defi}

By structural induction on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas, we define a function

\mathsf{Spl}
mapping every
\iota\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
to a finite set of simple formulas:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{Spl}(\psi)&\mathrel{:=}&\{\psi\}\\
\mathsf{Spl}(q_{x})&\mathrel{:=}&\{[\top?]q_{x}\}\\
\mathsf{Spl}(\iota_{1}\land\iota_{2})&\mathrel{:=}&\mathsf{Spl}(\iota_{1})\cup\mathsf{Spl}(\iota_{2})\\
\mathsf{Spl}([\alpha]\iota)&\mathrel{:=}&\{[\alpha]\theta\mid\theta\in\mathsf{Spl}(\iota)\}.\end{array}

Define the
normal form
of
\iota\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
as the formula

\iota^{\textit{nf}}\mathrel{:=}\bigwedge\mathsf{Spl}(\iota).

The following fact can be proved by a straightforward induction on the complexity of
\mathcal{Q}
-formulas.

Fact 69
.

Every formula
\iota\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is equivalent to its normal form.
Moreover,
\mathsf{voc}(\iota)=\mathsf{voc}(\iota^{\textit{nf}})
.

With all these preparations out of the way, we can now define pre-interpolants, starting at the leaves of the quasi-tableau and enriching the formula as we proceed towards the root: Informally, at a repeat leaf
x
, the pre-interpolant is set as an internal variable
q_{c(x)}
, and at a leaf
x
such that
\Delta_{x}\in{\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\setminus\Lambda_{2}[C]}
, the pre-interpolant is set as
\theta_{\Delta_{x}}
; informally, this is the interpolant for the exit node.
These are the only two base cases, see
66
.
The pre-interpolant is successively enriched as we proceed towards the root: the test operator is used where disjunction would usually be used, and at a companion
c(x)
, a solution to the fixpoint equation — existence guaranteed by equivalence to the normal form — is employed to eliminate the internal variable
q_{c(x)}
.

Later in
Subsection 10.2
we will also provide a concrete example to compute an interpolant using
Subsection 9.2
.
The reader might already want to have a look at
Figure 12
to get a better understanding of how pre-interpolants are defined.

{defi}

[
iitp
]
By a leaf-to-root induction on the relation
\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}
we define, for each node

x\in Q
, a formula
\iota_{x}
(called a
pre-interpolant
) as follows:

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a repeat:

\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}q_{c(x)}
.

Case
k(x)=1
,

\Delta_{x}\in{\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\setminus\Lambda_{2}[C]}
:

\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}\theta_{\Delta_{x}}
(see
Subsection 9.2
).

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a companion:

In this case
x
has a unique child
y
, and inductively we may assume that the formula
\iota_{y}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
has already been defined.
By
69
,
\iota_{y}
is equivalent to its normal form

\iota_{y}\equiv{\bigwedge_{i}}[\alpha_{i}]q_{x}\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi,

where
q_{x}
does not occur in any of the
\alpha_{i}
or in the formula
\bigwedge_{j}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi
.
Now define

\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}[{({\bigcup_{i}}\alpha_{i})}^{*}]\big({\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi\big).

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
neither a leaf nor a companion:

In this case,
x
also has a unique child
y
, for which the formula
\iota_{y}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
has already been defined.
We now set
\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}\iota_{y}
.

Case
k(x)=2
:

In this case,
x
has a unique child
y
, and inductively we may assume that the formula
\iota_{y}
has already been defined.

\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}[\lnot\theta_{\Delta_{x}}?]\iota_{y}.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
is not basic:

Here we define

\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}\bigwedge\{\iota_{y}\mid x\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}y\}.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
basic:

In this case
x
has a unique child
y
, and we define

\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}[a]\iota_{y},

where
a
is the leading atomic program of the loaded formula in
\Delta_{x}
.

Remark 70
.

We briefly discuss the two most interesting cases of
Subsection 9.2
, which may also shed some light on the reasons why we need to differentiate the nodes of different types.

First, in the case where
k(x)=1
and
x
is a companion node, one may think of
\iota_{x}
as the greatest fixpoint of the formula
\iota_{y}
with respect to the proposition letter
q_{x}
.
The fixpoint part of this is the statement that

\iota_{x}\equiv\iota_{y}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle.

To see this, notice that if
\iota_{y}\equiv{\bigwedge_{i}}[\alpha_{i}]q_{x}\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi
then we have

\displaystyle\iota_{x}

\displaystyle=[{({\bigcup_{i}}\alpha_{i})}^{*}]\big({\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi\big)

\displaystyle\equiv[({\bigcup_{i}}\alpha_{i})][{({\bigcup_{i}}\alpha_{i})}^{*}]\big({\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi\big)\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi

\displaystyle=[({\bigcup_{i}}\alpha_{i})]\iota_{x}\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi

\displaystyle\equiv{\bigwedge_{i}}[\alpha_{i}]\iota_{x}\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi

Second, in the case
k(x)=2
, for the interpolant to be correct we need to combine the formula
\theta_{\Delta_{x}}
, which is an interpolant of the nodes in
C_{\Delta_{x}}
that belong to
C^{+}\setminus C
, with
\iota_{y}
.
For this reason, we assign the pre-interpolant
[\lnot\theta_{\Delta_{x}}?]\iota_{y}\equiv\theta_{\Delta_{x}}\lor\iota_{y}
to nodes of type 2.
Using a test here, instead of a disjunction, guarantees that the normalisation succeeds when later on we arrive at a type-1 node in
\mathcal{Q}
.

{defi}

[
itp
]
The interpolant
\theta_{r}
of the root
r
of the cluster
C
is given as

\theta_{r}\mathrel{:=}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\top&\text{if }\Lambda_{1}(t)=\emptyset,\\
\iota_{c_{\mathcal{Q}}}&\text{if }\Lambda_{1}(t)\neq\emptyset.\end{array}\right.

10.
Interpolation: Main Theorem

10.1.
Correctness of the interpolants

We will now show that the formulas that we defined in the previous section are indeed interpolants.
First, we consider the vocabulary condition.

Note that we fixed a uniform closed split PDL-tableau
\mathcal{T}
for a sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
and a proper cluster
C
of
\mathcal{T}
including the root
r
on page
9.2
above.
In the current section we continue to work with this specific cluster
C
and its quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
from
Subsection 9.2
.

Recall that
\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma)
is the set of atomic programs and proposition letters occurring in formulas in the sequent
\Gamma
,
and for
x\in Q
, we defined

L_{<x}=\{z\in L_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid z\text{ is a repeat and }c(z)<_{\mathcal{Q}}x\leq_{\mathcal{Q}}z\}
(see
Subsection 9.2
).

Lemma 71
.

For all nodes
x\in Q
, we have

\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{x})\subseteq\big(\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})\big)\cup\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<x}\}.

(17)

As an immediate corollary, we have:

\mathsf{voc}(\theta_{r})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.

Proof 10.1
.

First, observe that by
55
, it follows that
\mathsf{voc}(\Lambda_{1}(t))\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})
for all
t\in C^{+}_{\Delta_{x}}\setminus C_{\Delta_{x}}
and that
\mathsf{voc}(\Delta_{x})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
.

The main claim Claim (
17
) is proved by root-to-leaf induction on the relation
\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}
.
The base cases are covered by the first two cases below, and the induction step is covered by the remaining cases.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a repeat:

We have
\iota_{x}=q_{c(x)}
so the claim is immediate by the observation that
z\in L_{<z}
for every repeat
z
.

Case
k(x)=1
,

\Delta_{x}\in{\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\setminus\Lambda_{2}[C]}
:

Note
\iota_{x}=\theta_{\Delta_{x}}
. The claim holds by
68
(c).

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a companion:

In this case,
x
has a unique child
y
, and
\iota_{y}\equiv{\bigwedge_{i}}[\alpha_{i}]q_{x}\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi
.
By the induction hypothesis and
69
, the vocabulary
\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{y})
is a subset of

\big(\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})\big)\cup\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<y}\}
.
Hence by the definition of
\iota_{x}
, it follows that

\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{x})\subseteq\big(\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})\big)\cup\big(\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<y}\}\setminus\{q_{x}\}\big).

From the definitions of
L_{<y}
and
L_{<x}
, and since
y
is the child of
x
, we have that
\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<y}\}\setminus\{q_{x}\}\subseteq\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<x}\}
. The claim follows.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is neither a leaf nor a companion:

Then
x
has a unique child
y
, and we have
\iota_{x}=\iota_{y}
.
By
67
(d), we have
L_{<x}=L_{<y}
.
By the induction hypothesis for
y
, we obtain that:

\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{y})\subseteq\big(\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})\big)\cup\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<y}\}.

Since
\iota_{x}=\iota_{y}
and
L_{<x}=L_{<y}
, the claim follows.

Case
k(x)=2
:

In this case
x
has a unique child
y
, and
\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}[\lnot\theta_{\Delta_{x}}?]\iota_{y}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{x})
is a subset of
\mathsf{voc}(\theta_{\Delta_{x}})\cup\big(\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})\big)\cup\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<y}\}
.
Notice that
x
is not a companion by
67
(a), and thus by
67
(d) we get that

L_{<y}=L_{<x}
.
Also, by
68
(c) we find
\mathsf{voc}(\theta_{\Delta_{x}})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
. Hence the claim is established.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
is not basic:

Here
\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}\bigwedge\{\iota_{y}\mid x\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}y\}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{x})
is a subset of
\big(\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})\big)\cup\big(\bigcup_{\{y\mid x\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}y\}}\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<y}\}\big)
.
Since
x
is not a companion by
67
(a),
we have that by
67
(d)
L_{<y}=L_{<x}
for all
y
such that
x\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}y
. Hence the claim is established.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
is basic:

In this case,
x
has a unique child
y
, and
\iota_{x}\mathrel{:=}[a]\iota_{y}
where
a
is the leading atomic program of the loaded formula in
\Delta_{x}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{x})
is a subset of
\{a\}\cup\big(\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})\big)\cup\{q_{c(z)}\mid z\in L_{<y}\}
.
Since
x
is not a companion by
67
(a),
we have that by
67
(d),
L_{<y}=L_{<x}
.
From this and the fact that
a\in\mathsf{voc}(\Delta_{x})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
, the claim follows.

Finally, the corollary that
\mathsf{voc}(\theta_{r})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
is immediate since
\theta_{r}=\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
and
L_{<r_{\mathcal{Q}}}=\emptyset
.

Now that we have shown that the formula
\theta_{r}
only uses proposition letters and atomic programs that occur in both
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, we will show that it also has the desired semantic properties.

The following formula
\rho_{x}
will be useful in the proof of the semantic properties of
\theta_{r}
.

{defi}

Given a quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
, for each node
x\in Q
, let

\rho_{x}\mathrel{:=}\bigvee\{\textstyle{\bigwedge\Lambda_{1}(t)}\mid t\in R_{x}\}.

Lemma 72
.

\Gamma_{1}\models\theta_{r}
.

Proof 10.2
.

In case
\Gamma_{1}=\emptyset
, we have
\theta_{r}=\top
, and so the Lemma holds trivially.
In the remainder of the proof we therefore assume that
\Gamma_{1}\neq\emptyset
, so that
\theta_{r}=\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
.

Consider the substitution
\sigma
given by setting
\sigma\colon q_{x}\mapsto\rho_{x}
where
x\in Q
is a companion node and
\rho_{x}
is its region formula (
Subsection 10.1
).
We will show that

\text{for all $x\in Q$: }\rho_{x}\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle.

(18)

We will prove Claim (
18
) by leaf-to-root induction on the relation
\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}
, and doing so we will frequently make implicit use of the fact that

\rho_{x}\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle\quad\text{ iff }\quad\text{ for all }t\in R_{x}:\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle.

The base cases are covered by the first two cases below, and the induction step is covered by the remaining cases.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a repeat:

In this case, we have
\iota_{x}=q_{c(x)}
.
Hence
\rho_{x}\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle
reduces to
\rho_{x}\models\rho_{c(x)}
. Since
\rho_{x}=\rho_{c(x)}
(see
Subsection 9.2
), this is trivially true.

Case
k(x)=1
,

\Delta_{x}\in{\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]\setminus\Lambda_{2}[C]}
:

In this case, we have that
\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle=\theta_{\Delta_{x}}\langle\sigma\rangle=\theta_{\Delta_{x}}
.
Now take an arbitrary node
t\in R_{x}
.
By the case assumption we have
t\in C^{+}\setminus C
, so that
68
(a) yields
\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\theta_{\Delta_{x}}
.
This suffices to show that
\rho_{x}\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle
, since
t
was chosen arbitrarily.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a companion:

In this case,
x
has a unique child
y
, and we have
\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{y}
.
This implies that
R_{x}=R_{y}
so that also
\rho_{x}=\rho_{y}
; we simply write
\rho
for this formula.

Recall from
69
that
\iota_{y}
is equivalent to its normal form.
Hence for the interpolants we find, writing
\alpha\mathrel{:=}\bigcup_{i}\alpha_{i}
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\iota_{y}&\equiv&[\alpha]q_{x}\land\bigwedge_{j}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi,\\
\iota_{x}&=&[\alpha^{*}]\big({\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi\big).\end{array}

It then follows by the induction hypothesis on
y
that

\rho\models[\alpha]\rho\land\bigwedge_{j}[\beta_{j}]\sigma(q_{z_{j}})\land\psi,

and from this we get by
3

that

\rho\models[\alpha^{*}]\big({\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]\sigma(q_{z_{j}})\land\psi\big),

so that we are done.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is neither a leaf nor a companion:

In this case,
x
has a unique child
y
, and by definition
\iota_{x}=\iota_{y}
.
Furthermore, we again have
\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{y}
, implying that also
\rho_{x}=\rho_{y}
.
We now immediately obtain
\rho_{x}\models\iota_{x}
from the induction hypothesis, which states
\rho_{y}\models\iota_{y}
.

Case
k(x)=2
:

In this case,
x
has a unique child
y
, and by definition we have
k(y)=3

and
\Delta_{y}=\Delta_{x}
; we will simply write
\Delta
for this sequent.
Furthermore recall that
R_{x}=C_{\Delta}^{+}
, but
R_{y}=C_{\Delta}^{R}
,
and that
\iota_{x}=[\lnot\theta_{\Delta}?]\iota_{y}
, so that

\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle\equiv\theta_{\Delta}\lor\iota_{y}\langle\sigma\rangle
.

By an inner induction on the relation
\lessdot
of
\mathcal{T}
we will show
that all nodes
t
in
\mathcal{T}
satisfy the following:

\text{if }t\in R_{x}\text{ then }\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle.

(19)

To prove this we make a case distinction.
If
t\in R_{x}\setminus C
then by
68
(a), we have

\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\theta_{\Delta}
which suffices since

\theta_{\Delta}\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle
.
If
t\in R_{x}\cap C
then we make a further distinction.
In case
t\in C_{\Delta}^{R}
then by definition of
R_{y}
we have
t\in R_{y}
, and hence by the induction hypothesis on
y
,

\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\iota_{y}\langle\sigma\rangle
; again this suffices since

\iota_{y}\models\iota_{x}
.
Finally, if
t\in C_{\Delta}^{L}
then let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}
be the children of
t
in
\mathcal{T}
; note that since we applied a left rule to
t
, we have
\Lambda_{2}(u_{i})=\Lambda_{2}(t)
, which means that all
u_{i}
belong to
C_{\Delta}^{+}\subseteq R_{x}
.
Hence by the inner induction hypothesis, we find for all
i
that

\Lambda_{1}(u_{i})\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle
; from this it
is immediate that
\bigvee_{i}\bigwedge\Lambda_{1}(u_{i})\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle
.
This means that we are done, since we have
\bigwedge\Lambda_{1}(t)\equiv\bigvee_{i}\bigwedge\Lambda_{1}(u_{i})
by the local invertibility of the rule applied
at
t
.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
is not basic:

First of all observe that in this case we have
\Delta\mathrel{:=}\Delta_{x}\in\Lambda_{2}[C]
.
Let
\Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{n}
be sequents as in
65
and in
Subsection 9.2
.
Take an arbitrary
t\in C_{\Delta}^{R}=R_{x}
, then we may present the children of
t
in
\mathcal{T}
as
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, with
\Lambda(t_{i})=\Lambda_{1}(t);\Pi_{i}
.
Furthermore, let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
be the corresponding enumeration of the children of
x
in
\mathcal{Q}
, that is, we have
\Delta_{y_{i}}=\Pi_{i}
.
We now have
t_{i}\in R_{y_{i}}
, for all
i
, so by the induction hypothesis we find that
\Lambda_{1}(t_{i})\models\iota_{y_{i}}\langle\sigma\rangle
.
Since for all
i
,
\Lambda_{1}(t)=\Lambda_{1}(t_{i})
, it follows that
\Lambda_{1}(t)\models\bigwedge_{i}\iota_{y_{i}}\langle\sigma\rangle
, and hence by definition of
\iota_{x}=\bigwedge_{i}\iota_{y_{i}}
,
we find that
\rho_{x}\models\iota_{x}\langle\sigma\rangle
indeed holds.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
is basic:

In this case,
x
has a unique child
y
, which has type 1
and label
\Delta_{y}={(\Delta_{x})}_{a}\cup\{\lnot\xi\}
, where

\lnot\underline{[a]}\xi
is the loaded formula in
\Delta_{x}
.
Take an arbitrary
t\in C_{\Delta_{x}}^{R}=R_{x}
.
Since
\Delta_{x}
is basic, by
65
(e),
the modal rule is applied at
t
.
Let
u
be the unique child of
t
in
\mathcal{T}
, then

\Lambda_{1}(u)={(\Lambda_{1}(t))}_{a}
and

\Lambda_{2}(u)={(\Lambda_{2}(t))}_{a}\cup\{\neg\xi\}=\Delta_{y}
,
and by
65
(e), we have
u\in C
.
This means that
u\in R_{y}
, so that we obtain
\Lambda_{1}(u)\models\iota_{y}

by the induction hypothesis.
Since
\Lambda_{1}(u)={(\Lambda_{1}(t))}_{a}
, we have
{(\Lambda_{1}(t))}_{a}\models\iota_{y}

from which we easily derive that
\Lambda_{1}(t)\models[a]\iota_{y}
.
So we are done since
\iota_{x}=[a]\iota_{y}
.

Finally, to see why the lemma follows from Claim (
18
), take
x
to be the root
r_{\mathcal{Q}}
of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Then Claim (
18
) implies that
\rho_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}\models\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}\langle\sigma\rangle
.
Now since
\mathsf{voc}(\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{1})\cap\mathsf{voc}(\Gamma_{2})
by
71
, we have
\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}\langle\sigma\rangle=\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
; and because of our assumption,
\Gamma_{1}\neq\emptyset
, by definition of
\theta_{r}
we have
\theta_{r}=\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
.
Thus we obtain
\rho_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}\models\theta_{r}
.
Furthermore, since
\Lambda_{2}(r)=\Gamma_{2}=\Delta_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
we find

r\in R_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
, so that
\bigwedge\Gamma_{1}=\bigwedge\Lambda_{1}(r)
is
one of the disjuncts of
\rho_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
.
But then
\rho_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}\models\theta_{r}
implies
\Gamma_{1}\models\theta_{r}
, as required.

To show that
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\theta_{r}
, we will need the following notion of distance, relative to a given program.
Intuitively, the idea is that if we have
(v,w)\in R_{\alpha}
in some Kripke model
\mathcal{M}
, the
\alpha
-distance from
v
to
w
is the minimal number of atomic steps it takes to move from
v
to
w
along an
\alpha
-path.
Furthermore, the
\alpha
-distance from
v
to
w
is set to infinity if
(v,w)\not\in R_{\alpha}
.

{defi}

[
distance
]
Let
v
and
w
be states in some model
\mathcal{M}=(W,\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\},V)
, and let
\alpha
be some program.
By induction on the shallow structure of
\alpha
, we define the
\alpha
-distance

d_{\alpha}(v,w)
from
v
to
w
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}d_{a}(v,w)&\mathrel{:=}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1&\text{if }(v,w)\in R_{a}\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
d_{\tau?}(v,w)&\mathrel{:=}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0&\text{if }\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\tau\text{ and }v=w\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
d_{\alpha\cup\beta}(v,w)&\mathrel{:=}&\min\big\{d_{\alpha}(v,w),d_{\beta}(v,w)\big\}\\
d_{\alpha;\beta}(v,w)&\mathrel{:=}&\min\big\{d_{\alpha}(v,u)+d_{\beta}(u,w)\mid u\in W\big\}\\
d_{\alpha^{\ast}}(v,w)&\mathrel{:=}&\min\big\{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d_{\alpha}(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\in W,v_{0}=v,v_{n}=w\big\}.\end{array}

We extend this definition to lists of programs in the obvious way:

\begin{array}[]{lll}d_{\varepsilon}(v,w)&\mathrel{:=}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0&\text{if }v=w\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)&\mathrel{:=}&\min\big\{d_{\alpha}(v,u)+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u,w)\mid u\in W\big\}.\end{array}

Note that if
d_{\alpha^{\ast}}(v,w)<\infty
and
v\neq w
then
there is a sequence
v=v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}=w
such that
d_{\alpha^{\ast}}(v,w)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d_{\alpha}(v_{i},v_{i+1})
.
Moreover, we can assume
v\neq v_{1}
because if
v=v_{1}
then also
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
would already witness
d_{\alpha^{\ast}}(v,w)
. More generally, we can remove those initial elements of the sequence that are equal to
v
until we obtain a non-empty sequence whose second element is distinct from
v
(the assumption
v\neq w
implies that the sequence cannot be empty).

Lemma 73

(
distanceProps
✓)
.

Let
v
and
w
be states in some model
\mathcal{M}=(W,\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\},V)
.
Then the following hold, for any program
\alpha
and lists of programs
\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}
.

(1)

d_{\alpha}(v,w)<\infty
iff
(v,w)\in R_{\alpha}
;

(2)

d_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n}}(v,w)=d_{\alpha_{1};\dots;\alpha_{n}}(v,w)
;

(3)

if, for all
u
,
d_{\alpha}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)
then, for all
u
,

d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)
;

(4)

if
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
then, for all
u
,
d_{\alpha}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)
;

(5)

if
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
then, for all
u
,
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)
;

(6)

if
(v,w)\in R_{\alpha}
then there is a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
and
d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,w)=d_{\alpha}(v,w)
;

(7)

if
(v,w)\in R_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}
then there is a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
and
d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)=d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)
;

(8)

if
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\lnot\underline{\Box}(\alpha\vec{\gamma},\psi)
where
\psi
is an unloaded PDL formula, then there exists a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X\land\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma},\psi)
and

\min\big\{d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}=\min\big\{d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}.

Proof 10.3
.

Parts (a) and (b) are straightforward.

(c)

Let
\beta=\delta_{0};\dots;\delta_{n}
where
\vec{\delta}=\delta_{0}\dots\delta_{n}
.
Making use of part (b), it suffices to show for any
u
that

d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)\leq d_{\beta\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)
.

Let
u^{\prime}
witness minimality in
d_{\beta\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)=\min\big\{d_{\beta}(v,w)+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(w,u)\mid w\in W\big\}
in the sense that
d_{\beta\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)=d_{\beta}(v,u^{\prime})+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u^{\prime},u)
.
By definition of
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)
:

d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)\leq d_{\alpha}(v,u^{\prime})+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u^{\prime},u)
. Furthermore, from the hypothesis and part (b), we have

d_{\alpha}(v,u^{\prime})\leq d_{\beta}(v,u^{\prime})
. So:

\displaystyle d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,u)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\alpha}(v,u^{\prime})+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u^{\prime},u)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\beta}(v,u^{\prime})+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u^{\prime},u)

\displaystyle=d_{\beta\vec{\gamma}}(v,u).

(d)

We proceed by induction on the shallow program structure of
\alpha
.
Suppose

(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
, and let
u
be an arbitrary state.

Case
\alpha=a
:

Then
D_{a}=\{(\emptyset,a)\}
, and thus
\vec{\delta}=a
, making
d_{\alpha}(v,u)=d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)
hold trivially.

Case
\alpha={\tau?}
:

Then we have that
D_{\tau?}=\{(\{\tau\},\varepsilon)\}
.
If
v=u
, then since
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\tau
we have
d_{\tau?}(v,u)=0=d_{\varepsilon}(v,u)
.
If
v\neq u
, then we get that

d_{\tau?}(v,u)=\infty=d_{\varepsilon}(v,u)
.

Case
\alpha=\beta\cup\beta^{\prime}
:

Then
D_{\beta\cup\beta^{\prime}}=D_{\beta}\cup D_{\beta^{\prime}}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}
.
Then, by the inductive hypothesis, we have that
d_{\beta}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)
.
It then follows that
d_{\beta\cup\beta^{\prime}}(v,u)=\min(d_{\beta}(v,u),d_{\beta^{\prime}}(v,u))\leq d_{\beta}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)
.

Case
\alpha=\beta;\beta^{\prime}
:

From the definition we have

\displaystyle D_{\beta;\beta^{\prime}}=~

\displaystyle\{(X,\vec{\delta}\beta^{\prime})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}~\cup

\displaystyle\{(X\cup Y,\vec{\delta})\mid(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta},(Y,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta^{\prime}}\}.

Consider first a pair
(X,\vec{\delta}\beta^{\prime})\in D_{\beta;\beta^{\prime}}
with
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}
and
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
.
By inductive hypothesis, we have that
d_{\beta}(v,u^{\prime})\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u^{\prime})
for all
u^{\prime}
.
By part (c) and instantiating
u^{\prime}=u
, we obtain that
d_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\prime}}(v,u)
.

Now consider a pair
(X\cup Y,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta;\beta^{\prime}}
with
(X,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta}
and
(Y,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta^{\prime}}
.
By inductive hypothesis, for all
u^{\prime}
,
d_{\beta}(v,u^{\prime})\leq d_{\varepsilon}(v,u^{\prime})
, and then by part (c), for all
u^{\prime}
,
d_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(v,u^{\prime})\leq d_{\beta^{\prime}}(v,u^{\prime})
.
Applying the inductive hypothesis with
(Y,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta^{\prime}}
we get that
d_{\beta^{\prime}}(v,u^{\prime})\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u^{\prime})
for all
u^{\prime}
.
Combining these two observations, and instantiating
u^{\prime}=u
, we obtain
d_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)
.

Case
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
:

From the definition we have

D_{\beta^{\ast}}=\{(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\}\cup\{(X,\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast})\mid(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta},\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon\}.

Consider first the pair
(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
.
For this pair, we need to show that
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)\leq d_{\varepsilon}(v,u)
. If
v=u
, then
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)=0=d_{\varepsilon}(v,u)
. If
v\neq u
, then
d_{\varepsilon}(v,u)=\infty
, and thus
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)\leq d_{\varepsilon}(v,u)
holds trivially.

Now consider a pair
(X,\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast})\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
with
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}
and
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
. We need to show
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)
.
Let
v=v_{0},\dots,v_{n}=u
be a sequence such that

d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d_{\beta}(v_{i},v_{i+1})
,
and let
v=u_{0},\dots,u_{k}=u
be a sequence such that

d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)=d_{\vec{\delta}}(u_{0},u_{1})+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d_{\beta}(u_{j},u_{j+1})
, i.e., these sequences witness the minimality in the definitions of
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)
and
d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)
.
We now have,

\begin{array}[]{rcll}d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)&=&\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d_{\beta}(v_{i},v_{i+1})&\text{(by assumption on $v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}$)}\\[1.00006pt]
&\leq&\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}d_{\beta}(u_{j},u_{j+1})&\text{(by minimality of $v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}$)}\\[1.00006pt]
&\leq&d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u_{1})+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d_{\beta}(u_{j},u_{j+1})&\text{(by induction hypothesis)}\\[1.00006pt]
&=&d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,u)&\text{(by assumption on $u_{0},\ldots,u_{k}$).}\end{array}

(e)

This follows from part (c) and part (d).

(f)

By induction on the shallow program structure of
\alpha
.
We only cover two cases of the induction step.

Case
\alpha=\gamma;\beta
:

Since
(v,w)\in R_{\gamma;\beta}
, by part (a) it follows that

d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)<\infty
.
By definition of
d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)
,
there exists a
u
such that

d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)=d_{\gamma}(v,u)+d_{\beta}(u,w)
.

By the induction hypothesis on
\gamma
we find a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\gamma}

such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
and
d_{\gamma}(v,u)=d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)
.

Consider the definition of
D_{\gamma;\beta}
.
If
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
then

(X,\vec{\delta}\beta)\in D_{\gamma;\beta}
.
From part (e), we obtain

d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\beta}(v,w)
.
Due to the
\min
in the definition,
d_{\vec{\delta}\beta}(v,w)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)+d_{\beta}(u,w)
.
Thus

d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)=d_{\gamma}(v,u)+d_{\beta}(u,w)=d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)+d_{\beta}(u,w)\geq d_{\vec{\delta}\beta}(v,w).

We conclude that
d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)=d_{\vec{\delta}\beta}(v,w)
, as required.

On the other hand, if
\vec{\delta}=\varepsilon
, from
d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)=d_{\gamma}(v,u)\leq d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)<\infty
, and the definition of
d_{\varepsilon}(v,u)
, we have
v=u
.
Furthermore, since
d_{\beta}(u,w)\leq d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)<\infty
, by part (a) we have
(u,w)\in R_{\beta}
.
Hence
(v,w)=(u,w)\in R_{\beta}
.
By the induction hypothesis it follows that there
is a pair
(Y,\vec{\eta})\in D_{\beta}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge Y
and

d_{\vec{\eta}}(v,w)=d_{\beta}(v,w)
.
Combining all of this, and utilizing the definition of
D_{\gamma;\beta}
, we have
(X\cup Y,\vec{\eta})\in D_{\gamma;\beta}
, and

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge(X\cup Y)
, and
d_{\vec{\eta}}(v,w)=d_{\beta}(v,w)=d_{\varepsilon}(v,v)+d_{\beta}(v,w)=d_{\gamma;\beta}(v,w)
, as required.

Case
\alpha=\beta^{\ast}
:

Let
(v,w)\in R_{\beta^{\ast}}
. There are two possibilities.
If
v=w
then the claim is witnessed by
(\emptyset,\varepsilon)\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
:
it follows from definition that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge\emptyset
and

d_{\varepsilon}(v,v)=0=d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,v)
(in the definition of
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,v)
, we can take
n=0
which yields the empty sum, which equals 0).

If
v\neq w
then, since
(v,w)\in R_{\beta^{\ast}}
, by part (a) we get that
d_{{\beta^{\ast}}}(v,w)<\infty
.
Therefore, by definition of
d_{{\beta^{\ast}}}(v,w)
, there exists some
v_{1}\neq v
(see observation above this lemma)
such that

d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)=d_{\beta}(v,v_{1})+d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v_{1},w)
.
By the induction hypothesis there is a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\beta}

such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
and
d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,v_{1})=d_{\beta}(v,v_{1})
.
Note that we must have
\vec{\delta}\neq\varepsilon
since
v\neq v_{1}
.
By definition of
D_{\beta^{\ast}}
, we then have that
(X,\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast})\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
.
It remains to establish
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)=d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)
. To see this, first notice that
from
(X,\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast})\in D_{\beta^{\ast}}
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
, by part (e) we obtain

d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)
.
On the other hand,

d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)=d_{\beta}(v,v_{1})+d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v_{1},w)=d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,v_{1})+d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v_{1},w)\geq d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)
.
We conclude that
d_{\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)=d_{\vec{\delta}\beta^{\ast}}(v,w)
, so the claim is proved.

(g)

Assume
(v,w)\in R_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}
.
First note that for all lists
\vec{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{n}
we have
R_{\vec{\gamma}}=R_{\gamma_{1}\mathop{;}\ldots\mathop{;}\gamma_{n}}
and
d_{\vec{\gamma}}=d_{\gamma_{1}\mathop{;}\ldots\mathop{;}\gamma_{n}}
.
Since
\alpha\mathop{;}\gamma_{1}\mathop{;}\ldots\mathop{;}\gamma_{n}
is a single program, we can apply part (a) to obtain

d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)<\infty
.
Therefore, there exists a
u
such that
(v,u)\in R_{\alpha}
,
(u,w)\in R_{\vec{\gamma}}
,
and
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)=d_{\alpha}(v,u)+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u,w)
, i.e.,
u
witnesses the minimum in the definition of
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)
.

From
(v,u)\in R_{\alpha}
and part (f), we obtain a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
and
d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)=d_{\alpha}(v,u)
.

Note that
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)=d_{\alpha}(v,u)+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u,w)=d_{\vec{\delta}}(v,u)+d_{\vec{\gamma}}(u,w)\geq d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)
.
Since
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
, by part (e) we obtain
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)
.
Combining both inequalities, we conclude
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)=d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)
, as required.

(h)

Let
w_{0}\in W
be such that
\mathcal{M},w_{0}\Vdash\lnot\psi
and

d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w_{0})=\min\big\{d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}

Since
(v,w_{0})\in R_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}
, applying part (g), we obtain a pair
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge X
and
d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w_{0})=d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w_{0})
.
Now

\min\big\{d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w_{0})=d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w_{0})

Since
(X,\vec{\delta})\in D_{\alpha}
, by part (e) we get that
d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\leq d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)
for all
w\in W
.
From this it follows that:

\min\big\{d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}\leq\min\big\{d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}

Combining the above, we obtain the desired result, since

\displaystyle\min\big\{d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\min\big\{d_{\vec{\delta}\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}

\displaystyle\leq d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w_{0})

\displaystyle=\min\big\{d_{\alpha\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi\big\}

In order to prove that
\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\theta_{r}
, the key observation is
75
below.
In the proof we will need the following auxiliary result.

Lemma 74
.

If
x
is a repeat in
\mathcal{Q}
then the path from
c(x)
to
x
passes through a node
y
such that
\Delta_{y}
is basic.

Proof 10.4
.

Every repeat is a node of type 1, and has a (unique) predecessor.
By definition of
\mathcal{Q}
, nodes of type 1 can only succeed nodes of type 3 that are labeled with a basic sequent.

For the formulation of
75
, recall that
L_{<x}
denotes the set of repeat leaves that are descendants of
x
and whose companion node is a proper ancestor of
x
.

Lemma 75
.

Let
x
be a node in
\mathcal{Q}
.
If the sequent
\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
is satisfiable,
then so is the sequent
\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
, for some leaf
z\in L_{<x}
.

Proof 10.5
.

For the proof of this lemma we will look in more detail at
how

\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
is satisfied in some model
\mathcal{M}
by considering the “witness distance” of the loaded formula at a given node in
\mathcal{M}
that satisfies the sequent
\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
.
We need some further notation here: for
x\in Q
, let
\vec{\delta_{x}}
and
\psi_{x}
be such that
\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\delta_{x}},\psi_{x})
is the (unique) loaded formula in
\Delta_{x}
, while
\psi_{x}
itself is unloaded.
Observe that
\vec{\delta_{x}}
and
\psi_{x}
are uniquely determined by
x
.
For a state
v
in
\mathcal{M}
and node
x
in
\mathcal{Q}
, define

\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)\mathrel{:=}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\infty&\text{if }\mathcal{M},v\not\Vdash\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}\\
\min\big\{d_{\vec{\delta_{x}}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi_{x}\big\}&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

In words: if
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
then
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
is the least
\vec{\delta_{x}}
-distance from
v
to a state that witnesses
\lnot\psi_{x}
.

The main technical result is formulated in the following Claim.

Claim.

Let
x
be a node in
\mathcal{Q}
.
Then for any pointed Kripke model
(\mathcal{M},v)
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
there is a leaf
z\in L_{<x}
and a state
u
in
\mathcal{M}
, such that

(1)
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
and

(2)
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
, with

(3)
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z)<\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
if there is a basic node between
x
and
z
.

The proof of the claim is by primary induction on the relation
\lessdot_{\mathcal{Q}}
from leaf to root, and secondary induction on
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
.
Assume that
\mathcal{M}=(W,R,V)
,
v
and
x
are as in the formulation of the claim.
We make a case distinction as to the type of
x
.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a repeat:

In this case, we have
x\in L_{<x}
, and the claim holds by simply taking
z\mathrel{:=}x
and
u\mathrel{:=}v
.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a leaf because
\Delta_{x}\in{\Lambda_{2}[C^{+}]}\setminus{\Lambda_{2}[C]}
:

Since
\iota_{x}=\theta_{\Delta_{x}}
, it follows from
68
(b) that the sequent
\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
is not satisfiable, so that the claim is vacuously true.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is neither a leaf nor a companion:

By the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
,
x
has a unique child
y
, and we have
\iota_{x}=\iota_{y}
and
\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{y}
. Since
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
, it follows that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{y},\iota_{y}
. Thus, we can apply the primary inductive hypothesis at
y
, which gives the existence of a leaf
z\in L_{<y}
and a state
v\in W
such that

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}\text{ and }\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y),

where the inequality is strict if there is a basic node between
y
and
z
.

First, notice that since
\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{y}
, it follows that
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y)
, and that there is a basic node between
y
and
z
if and only if there is a basic node between
x
and
z
.
Second, since
x
is not a companion of any node by
67
(d), it follows that
L_{<x}=L_{<y}
. From these observations, we conclude that there exists a leaf
z\in L_{<x}
and a state
v\in W
such that

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
and

\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
,

where the inequality is strict if there is a basic node between
x
and
z
.
This exactly matches the claim.

Case
k(x)=1
,
x
is a companion:

Observe that by definition of
\mathcal{Q}
,
x
has a unique child
y
with
k(y)=2
and
\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{y}
(which we simply write as
\Delta
).
Furthermore, as noted in
70
,
by construction of the quasi-tableau,

\iota_{y}\equiv{\bigwedge_{i}}[\alpha_{i}]q_{x}\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi
and

\iota_{x}\equiv{\bigwedge_{i}}[\alpha_{i}]\iota_{x}\land{\bigwedge_{j}}[\beta_{j}]q_{z_{j}}\land\psi
, hence
\iota_{x}\equiv\iota_{y}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be the Kripke model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}(W,R,V^{\prime})
, where
V^{\prime}
is the valuation on
W
that modifies
V
by mapping
q_{x}
to the set

\{u\in W\mid\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\iota_{x}\}
.
The difference between
V
and
V^{\prime}
may affect the truth of a
pre-interpolant that contains
q_{x}
, but it will not affect the truth of any formula in
\Delta_{z}
for all
z\in Q
,
since such formulas are
q_{x}
-free.
Also note that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{x}
iff

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{y}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle
iff

\mathcal{M}^{\prime},v\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{y}
. The first ‘iff’ is
due to the definitions of
\iota_{x}
and
\iota_{y}
, and the second ‘iff’ is due to
2
about substitution.

Since
x
is closer to the root of
\mathcal{Q}
than
y
, from the primary induction hypothesis at
y
, we obtain a leaf
z\in L_{<y}
and a state
v^{\prime}\in W
such that

\mathcal{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}\text{ and }\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v,y),

(20)

where the inequality is strict if there is a basic node between
y
and
z
.
Applying the Substitution Lemma to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
gives us
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle
.
Now make a further case distinction.

Subcase
z\in L_{<x}
:

By this assumption,
x
is not the companion of
z
. Hence
\iota_{z}=q_{c(z)}\neq q_{x}
,
so
\iota_{z}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle=\iota_{z}
.
Then
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle
simplifies to

\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
.
From the latter and from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
obtained in Statement (
20
),
we have that
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},z)=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},z)
as

\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
have the same relational structure, and since in both cases
\mathsf{wd}
returns the witness distance of the same loaded formula.
For the same reason — and since by assumption
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{x}
and hence

\mathcal{M}^{\prime},v\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{y}
— we have
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v,y)=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
.
Chaining these observations with
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v,y)
from Statement (
20
), we obtain
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)

which constitutes part (2) of the Claim.
Part (3) then follows by the observation that there is a basic node between
x

and
z
iff there is a basic node between
y
and
z
.

Subcase
z\not\in L_{<x}
:

In this case, we have
z\in L_{<y}\setminus L_{<x}
which means that the companion of
z
must be
x
.
It follows that
\Delta_{z}=\Delta
and
\iota_{z}=q_{c(z)}=q_{x}
and thus we find
\iota_{z}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle=\iota_{x}
.
Since
\mathcal{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
from Statement (
20
), by the Substitution Lemma we also have
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}\langle q_{x}\mapsto\iota_{x}\rangle
, and the latter is simply
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{x}
.
Thus
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},x)=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},z)
, since
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
have the same relational structure, and
\mathsf{wd}
returns the witness distance of the same loaded formula.
Also, by
74
there is a basic node between
x
and
z
; this
node then also lies between
y
and
z
, so that in fact
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},z)

is strictly smaller than
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v,y)
.

Combining the above we get that
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},x)=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},z)<\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(v,y)=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
where the last equality relies on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{y}
(already shown) and the fact that
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
have the same relational structure.
(In passing we note that the strict inequality implies that the subcase
z\not\in L_{<x}
cannot occur when
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)=0
.)
Since
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},x)<\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)

and
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{x}
, we can apply the secondary induction hypothesis to obtain a node
z^{\prime}\in L_{<x}
and a state
u\in W
such that
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\Delta_{z^{\prime}},\iota_{z^{\prime}}
and

\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},x)<\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
, which means that we are done.

Case
k(x)=2
:

Let
y
be the unique child of
x
, then by definition of
\mathcal{Q}
, we have

\Delta_{x}=\Delta_{y}
(which we write simply as
\Delta
)
and
\iota_{x}=[\lnot\theta_{\Delta}?]\iota_{y}\equiv\theta_{\Delta}\lor\iota_{y}
.
By
68
the sequent
\Delta,\theta_{\Delta}
is not satisfiable, and so it must be the case that

\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta,\iota_{y}
.
We may now apply the primary induction hypothesis to
y
and obtain a leaf
z\in L_{<y}
and a state
u\in W
such that
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
and
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y)
, with the inequality being strict if there is a basic node between
y
and
z
.
Since
k(x)\neq 1
,
67
(a) implies that
x
is not the companion of any repeat, and thus
L_{<y}=L_{<x}
. Hence the claim is established.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
is basic:

In this case, the loaded formula in
\Delta_{x}
must be of the form

\lnot\underline{\Box}(a\vec{\gamma},\psi_{x})
for some atomic program
a
.
Then by definition we have
\iota_{x}=[a]\iota_{y}
,
and
\Delta_{y}={(\Delta_{x})}_{a},\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\gamma},\psi_{x})
,
where
y
is the unique child of
x
.

In addition, since
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\lnot\underline{\Box}(a\vec{\gamma},\psi_{x})
,
there must be some
w
at minimum witness distance (a finite value by
73
(a))
such that
\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi_{x}
with an
a
-successor
v^{\prime}
of
v

such that
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\gamma},\psi_{x})

and
d_{\vec{\gamma}}(v^{\prime},w)=d_{a\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)-1
.
It is then easy to see that
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\Vdash\Delta_{y},\iota_{y}
.
By the primary induction hypothesis applied to
y
, we obtain a leaf
z\in L_{<y}
and a state
u
in
\mathcal{M}
such that
(
1^{\prime}
)
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
and
(
2^{\prime}
)
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},y)
.
Since
k(x)\neq 1
,
67
(a) implies that
x
is not the companion of any repeat, and thus we find
z\in L_{<x}
and

\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v^{\prime},y)\leq d_{\vec{\gamma}}(v^{\prime},w)<d_{a\vec{\gamma}}(v,w)=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
.

Case
k(x)=3
,
\Delta_{x}
is not basic:

Let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
be the children of
x
, labeled with, respectively,
the sequents
\Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{n}
.
Notice that in this case, it follows from the definitions that
\iota_{x}=\bigwedge_{i}\iota_{y_{i}}
.

We will first show that there exists a child
y_{i}
such that
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y_{i})=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
and that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i},\iota_{y_{i}}
.
To show this, we distinguish two subcases based on whether at the elements of
R_{x}=C^{R}_{\Delta_{x}}
a rule was applied to an unloaded formula or to the loaded formula.

The rule was applied to an unloaded formula:

If at the elements of
R_{x}=C^{R}_{\Delta_{x}}
a rule was applied to an unloaded formula,
then the loaded formula remains unchanged,
and thus
\vec{\delta}_{x}=\vec{\delta}_{y_{i}}
for all
i\leq n
.
(Recall that
\vec{\delta}_{x}
is the program list in the loaded formula of
\Delta_{x}
.)
Therefore,
d_{\vec{\delta_{x}}}(v,w^{\prime})=d_{\vec{\delta_{y_{i}}}}(v,w^{\prime})
for all
w^{\prime}\in W
and for all
i\leq n
.
From this, it follows that
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y_{i})=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
for all
i\leq n
.
By the local invertibility of the local rules we have
\bigwedge\Delta_{x}\equiv\bigvee_{i}\bigwedge\Pi_{i}
.
Hence, it follows from
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i},\iota_{y_{i}}
, for some
i\leq n
.
We have thus shown that there exists a child
y_{i}
of
x
such that
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y_{i})=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i},\iota_{y_{i}}
.

The rule was applied to the loaded formula:

If at the elements of
R_{x}=C^{R}_{\Delta_{x}}
a rule
R_{2}
was applied to the loaded formula, then
R_{2}
must have been the rule
{(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})}_{2}
, since
\Delta_{x}
is not basic.
Let
\alpha_{x}
and
\vec{\gamma_{x}}
be such that
\vec{\delta_{x}}=\alpha_{x}\vec{\gamma_{x}}
, and let

D_{\alpha_{x}}=\{(F_{1},\vec{\beta_{1}}),\dots,(F_{k},\vec{\beta_{k}})\}.

Since the branching at
x
in
\mathcal{Q}
corresponds to the application of
{(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})}_{2}
, it follows that
k=n
, and
we can assume that for all
i\leq n
, the sequent
\Pi_{i}
is obtained from
\Delta_{x}
by replacing the loaded formula
\lnot\underline{\Box}(\alpha_{x}\vec{\gamma_{x}},\psi_{x})
with
F_{i}\cup\{\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\beta_{i}}\vec{\gamma_{x}},\psi_{x})\}
.
Since
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\lnot\underline{\Box}(\alpha_{x}\vec{\gamma_{x}},\psi_{x})
, we have by
73
(h) that there exists a pair
(F_{i},\vec{\beta_{i}})\in D_{\alpha_{x}}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\bigwedge F_{i}\land\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\beta_{i}}\vec{\gamma_{x}},\psi_{x})
, and that

\min\big\{d_{\alpha_{x}\vec{\gamma_{x}}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi_{x}\big\}=\min\big\{d_{\vec{\beta_{i}}\vec{\gamma_{x}}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi_{x}\big\}.

Since
\Pi_{i}=(\Delta_{x}\setminus(\lnot\underline{\Box}(\alpha_{x}\vec{\gamma_{x}},\psi_{x})))\cup F_{i}\cup\{\lnot\underline{\Box}(\vec{\beta_{i}}\vec{\gamma_{x}},\psi_{x})\}
and
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{x}
, it follows that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i}
.
Moreover, since
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\iota_{x}
and
\iota_{x}=\bigwedge\iota_{y_{i}}
, we infer that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i},\iota_{y_{i}}
.

Also notice that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)

\displaystyle=\min\big\{d_{\alpha_{x}\vec{\gamma_{x}}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi_{x}\big\}

\displaystyle=\min\big\{d_{\vec{\beta_{i}}\vec{\gamma_{x}}}(v,w)\mid\mathcal{M},w\Vdash\lnot\psi_{x}\big\}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y_{i}).

The first equality is justified by the fact that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Delta_{x},\iota_{x}
and the third by having
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i},\iota_{y_{i}}
.

This concludes the proof of this subcase.

We have thus shown that there exists a child
y_{i}
of
x
such that
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y_{i})=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
and that
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i},\iota_{y_{i}}
.
Let us fix such a child
y_{i}
.

Since
\mathcal{M},v\Vdash\Pi_{i},\iota_{y_{i}}
,
we can apply the inductive hypothesis at
y_{i}
, to obtain that
there exists a leaf
z\in L_{<y_{i}}
and a state
u
in
\mathcal{M}
, such that

\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
and

\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y_{i})
,
where the inequality is strict if there is a basic node between
y_{i}
and
z
.

Notice that since
k(x)\neq 1
,
67
(a) implies that
x
is not the companion of any repeat, and thus
L_{<y_{i}}\subseteq L_{<x}
.
From this it follows that
z\in L_{<x}
.
Since
\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,y_{i})=\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
, and since
x
is not basic, we conclude that

\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,z)\leq\mathsf{wd}_{\mathcal{M}}(v,x)
,
where the inequality is strict if there is a basic node between
x
and
z
.
These observations and the fact that
\mathcal{M},u\Vdash\Delta_{z},\iota_{z}
are exactly what we wanted to show for this case.

Lemma 76
.

\Gamma_{2}\models\lnot\theta_{r}
.

Proof 10.6
.

Note that for the root
r_{\mathcal{Q}}
of
\mathcal{Q}
the set
L_{<r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
is empty.
It then follows by
75
that the sequent
\Delta_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}},\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
cannot be satisfied.
From this we immediately get that the formula
\lnot\theta_{r}=\lnot\iota_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
is a consequence of the sequent
\Gamma_{2}=\Delta_{r_{\mathcal{Q}}}
.

We are now ready to combine various lemmas to prove the correctness of the interpolants.

Proof 10.7

(Proof of
62
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(V,\lessdot,r,\Lambda,L)
be a uniform closed tableau for the split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
,
and assume that the root
r
belongs to a proper cluster
C
.
By assumption the split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
is loaded.

Assume that
\Gamma_{2}
is loaded.
By
71
,
72
and
76
, the formula
\theta_{r}
as given in
Subsection 9.2

is an interpolant for the root
r
of
C
.

Now assume that
\Gamma_{1}
is loaded.
Intuitively, we now “flip” the tableau, exchanging the left and right parts of all split sequents.
Formally, let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=(V,\lessdot,r,\Lambda^{\prime},L^{\prime})
be given by:

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}(s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\Lambda_{2}(s)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}_{2}(s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\Lambda_{1}(s)

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}R_{1}&\text{if }L(s)=R_{2}\\
R_{2}&\text{if }L(s)=R_{1}\end{array}\right.

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a uniform closed PDL-tableau for the split sequent
\Gamma_{2};\Gamma_{1}
.

Furthermore, recall that we have assumed that for every node
t\in C^{+}\setminus C
we already have an interpolant
\theta_{t}
for the split sequent
\Lambda_{1}(t);\Lambda_{2}(t)
.
Then we also have an interpolant for every node
t\in C^{+}\setminus C
, for the split sequent
\Lambda_{2}(t);\Lambda_{1}(t)
, by simply taking
\lnot\theta_{t}
.

Now we can proceed as in the case where
\Gamma_{2}
was loaded, using
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
instead of
\mathcal{T}
.
By
71
,
72
and
76
we get that the interpolant
\theta_{r}
given by
Subsection 9.2
is a correct interpolant for the flipped split sequent
\Gamma_{2};\Gamma_{1}
.
Therefore
\lnot\theta_{r}
is a correct interpolant for our original split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, as wanted.

We can now combine the lemmas to prove that split tableaux do indeed yield interpolants.

Proof 10.8

(Proof of
57
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a uniform, closed tableau for the split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.
We want to show that
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
has an interpolant.

We proceed by induction on the size (number of nodes) of
\mathcal{T}
.

For the base case, if
\mathcal{T}
