is a single node, then its label must be a closed sequent and by
60
we are done.

As the inductive hypothesis, suppose that for any uniform, closed, split tableau
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
with fewer nodes than
\mathcal{T}
there exists an interpolant for the label of the root of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
We make a case distinction.

If the root of
\mathcal{T}
forms a singleton cluster, then we find an interpolant by
61
.

On the other hand, if the root of
\mathcal{T}
does not form part of a singleton cluster, then it is part of a proper cluster
C
, and in particular, loaded.
Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
be a sub-tableau of
\mathcal{T}
whose root is a node in
C^{+}\setminus C
.
Observe that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a tableau for the split PDL-rules, and uniform.
We will show that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a PDL-tableau and that it is closed.
Observe that if
v
is a free repeat or a loaded-path repeat in
\mathcal{T}
, then it is a leaf in
\mathcal{T}
, and thus it is also a leaf in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
As all repeats in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
are repeats in
\mathcal{T}
, it follows that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a split PDL-tableau.

We show that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is closed.
Let
s
be a leaf in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. We must show that
s
is closed or a loaded-path repeat.
So assume
s
is not closed. Then
s
must be a loaded-path repeat in
\mathcal{T}
, as otherwise
\mathcal{T}
would be open.
Therefore, there exists a companion
c(s)
in
\mathcal{T}
such that the path from
c(s)
to
s
is loaded.
It follows that
s
and
c(s)
are in the same cluster and thus, that
c(s)
is in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, which entails that
s
is a loaded-path repeat in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
From this it follows that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is closed.

By inductive hypothesis, we can obtain interpolants for all nodes
t\in C^{+}\setminus C
, and by applying
62
, we obtain the claim.

We now get our main result.

Proof 10.9

(Proof of
50
)
.

Suppose
\models\varphi\to\psi
.
Then
\varphi\land\lnot\psi
is unsatisfiable and by
59
there is a closed tableau for the split sequent
\varphi;\lnot\psi
.
By
57
there is an interpolant
\theta
for this split sequent and by
52
this
\theta
is also an interpolant for
\varphi\to\psi
.

10.2.
Constructing an interpolant for a particular example

To demonstrate how to construct an interpolant, consider the PDL validity
(p\land[a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p))\to[a][a^{\ast}]p
.
To find an interpolant (in the sense of
Subsection 7.1
) for this valid implication we now construct an interpolant (in the sense of
Subsection 7.1
)
for the split sequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}

where
\Gamma_{1}=\{p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)\}

and
\Gamma_{2}=\{\lnot[a][a^{\ast}]p\}
.
Notice that since
\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
is unsatisfiable, we can use the algorithm to find an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

The first step is to find a closed tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.
As there exists only a finite number of tableaux for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
, it is possible to algorithmically find a closed split PDL-tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.
As an example, the tableau in
Figure 11
is a closed split PDL-tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.

Notice that this tableau contains only one proper cluster, the highlighted cluster
C
.
Next we will construct the quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
for the cluster
C
.
From this construction we will find the interpolant for the root of the cluster
C
.

p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot[a][a^{\ast}]p
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
(L+)_{2}

[a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
(M)_{2}

p\lor[a^{\ast}]p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
(\Box)_{1}

(\lnot\land)_{1}
\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
\lnot\lnot p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
(\lnot)_{1}

(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})_{2}
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot p
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p

\heartsuit

Figure 11.
A closed tableau for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
with the highlighted proper cluster
C
.

The interpolants for the two nodes in
C^{+}\setminus C
can be obtained following the method described in
Subsection 9.1
as these are singleton clusters.
In particular, notice that for the leaf with split sequent

p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot p

we obtain the interpolant
p
,
and for the leaf with split sequent

\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p

we obtain the interpolant

\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p
.
From this, and the fact that there is no leaf with the sequent
\{\lnot[a][a^{\ast}]p\}
in the loaded component, it follows that:

\displaystyle\theta_{\{\lnot p\}}

\displaystyle=p,

\displaystyle\theta_{\{\lnot[a^{\ast}]p\}}

\displaystyle=\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p,

\displaystyle\theta_{\{\lnot[a][a^{\ast}]p\}}

\displaystyle=\bot.

Having made these computations, we can now construct the quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
for the cluster
C
with the corresponding pre-interpolants according to
Subsection 9.2
.
The result is shown in
Figure 12
.

x_{1}:\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
x_{2}:\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
x_{3}:\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
y_{1}:\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
y_{2}:\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
y_{3}:\lnot\underline{[a^{\ast}]}p
l_{1}:\lnot p
r_{1}:\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
(\iota=[(\lnot\bot?;a;(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?)^{\ast}][\lnot\bot?;a;(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?]p)
(\iota=[\lnot\bot?][a][(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?](p\land q_{x_{1}}))
(\iota=[a][(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?](p\land q_{x_{1}}))
(\iota=[(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?](p\land q_{x_{1}}))
(\iota=[(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?](p\land q_{x_{1}}))
(\iota=p\land q_{x_{1}})
(\iota=p)
(\iota=q_{x_{1}})

Figure 12.
The quasi-tableau
\mathcal{Q}
for the cluster
C
annotated with pre-interpolants.

Observe that the obtained pre-interpolant

\iota_{x_{1}}=[{(\lnot\bot?;a;(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?)}^{\ast}][\lnot\bot?;a;(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?]p

for the root
x_{1}
of
\mathcal{Q}
is indeed an interpolant for
p{,}\ [a][a^{\ast}](p\lor[a^{\ast}]p)~;~\lnot\underline{[a][a^{\ast}]}p
.
To make this observation a little easier, note that
\iota_{x_{1}}\equiv[{(a;\lnot[a^{\ast}]p?)}^{\ast}][a;\lnot[a^{\ast}]p?]p
.
Finally, observe that the interpolant assigned to the root of the tableau is also
\iota_{x_{1}}
, and that this is indeed an interpolant for
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}
.
This concludes the example.

11.
Conclusion

We have shown that Propositional Dynamic Logic (PDL) has Craig Interpolation.
Our proof is based on the key ideas of Borzechowski
[
8
]
: we defined a tableau system for PDL, showed its soundness and completeness and then used an adaptation of Maehara’s method to construct interpolants.
We end with some remarks about our ongoing project to implement the system and formally verify the proof, as well as remaining open questions.

Implementation.

We provide an implementation of the tableau system at

https://github.com/m4lvin/modal-tableau-interpolation
.
It is written in Haskell and comes in two variants:
one version stays close to the system developed by Borzechowski
[
8
]
with
n
-formulas,
the other version uses our
\mathsf{unfold}
functions.
We encourage the reader to try out the web interface available at
https://tools.malv.in/tapdleau
.

Verification.

We also started to formally verify the proof in the interactive theorem prover Lean
[
40
]
.
We marked all verified claims with “✓” throughout the article.
As of early August 2026 the formalization includes the Soundness
34
and the Completeness
37
with all their dependencies.
We have also verified smaller results such as
2
,
3
and
73
but not yet the whole Interpolation
50
.
Several more authors have contributed to our formalization, and parts of the proofs were written using the Aristotle AI tool
[
1
]
.
For details about this ongoing project we refer to
https://github.com/m4lvin/lean4-pdl
and the short article about a previous project
[
22
]
with a proof of interpolation for the basic modal logic
\mathsf{K}
in Lean 3.

Open questions.

The somewhat unclear situation in the literature has arguably also blocked research building on top of the result.
Now that we have Craig interpolation for PDL, there are interesting follow-up questions that can be pursued:

•

As we also discuss in
Appendix C
, it was shown by Kracht
[
32
]
that in order to show that PDL has interpolation it suffices to establish the property for its test-free fragment.
The other direction has
not
been shown, and we do not know whether test-free PDL has interpolation.
Note that full PDL is more expressive than its test-free version, as was shown by Berman and Paterson
[
4
, Main Theorem]
.

It is of course tempting, but not immediately clear how to adapt our method to test-free PDL.
Note that we use tests in the construction of interpolants for proper clusters — in the case
k(x)=2
of
Subsection 9.2
, as discussed in
70
.
These tests are often trivial, i.e., of the form
{\lnot\bot?}
, but not always.
For example, in
Subsection 10.2
we started with a test-free split sequent but obtained an interpolant containing the non-trivial test
{(\lnot\lnot\lnot[a^{\ast}]p)?}
.
That is, even when given test-free validities our method will generally yield interpolants with tests.
Of course, this does not show that test-free PDL does not have interpolation and for the concrete example in
Subsection 10.2
there exists a test-free interpolant, namely
[a][a^{\ast}]p
.

•

In a similar direction, we leave it open whether the method would work for
other fragments and variants
of PDL obtained by removing or adding additional operators such as intersection and converse.

•

There is a well-known correspondence between interpolation for logics and amalgamation of structures and algebras.
For an example, see the work of Hodges
[
26
, Section 5.5]
where interpolation for first-order logic is shown in this way.
This correspondence was also the basis for the algebraic proof attempt by Kowalski in 2002
[
29
]
.
PDL contains
Kleene Algebra with tests
(KAT) in the sense that a valid KAT-equation such as
\alpha=\beta
corresponds to the PDL-validity
[\alpha]p\leftrightarrow[\beta]q
where
p
is fresh
[
31
, Section 9]
.
We leave open the question what the implications of the Craig Interpolation Property for PDL are for Kleene algebras with tests, and (possibly) other classes of algebras.

Acknowledgements.

We thank additional former members of the PDL reading group that we started in 2022/23 in Amsterdam and Groningen: Johannes Marti, Jan Rooduijn and Guillermo Menéndez Turata.

Appendix B
Rule Overview

Below are the local rules from
Subsection 3.4
and the modal rule from
Subsection 4.1
.

\Delta,\lnot\lnot\varphi

(\lnot)

\Delta,\varphi

\Delta,\varphi\land\psi

(
\land
)

\Delta,\varphi,\psi

\Delta,\lnot(\varphi\land\psi)

(\lnot\land)

\Delta,\lnot\varphi\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Delta,\lnot\psi

\Delta,[\alpha]\varphi

(\Box)

\alpha
non-atomic

\big\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\square}(\alpha,\varphi)\big\}

\Delta,\lnot[\alpha]\varphi

(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})

\alpha
non-atomic

\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\varphi)\}

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[\alpha]}\xi

(\underline{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}})

\alpha
non-atomic

\big\{\Delta,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\in\underline{\mathsf{unfold}}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\xi)\big\}

\Delta,\lnot[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi

(L+)

\Delta
free and basic and
n\geq 0
maximal

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a][\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]}\varphi

(L-)

\Delta
basic

\Delta,\lnot[\alpha_{1}]\ldots[\alpha_{n}]\varphi

\Delta,\lnot\underline{[a]}\xi

(M)

\Delta
basic

\Delta_{a},\lnot\xi

where
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{F}
,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathcal{P}
,
a
atomic, and
\xi\in\mathcal{F}\cup\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e.,
\xi
is a possibly loaded formula.

Furthermore, if
t
is a child of a node
s
and
L(s)=(L+)
then
L(t)\neq(L-)
.
That is, we are not allowed to apply
(L-)
immediately after
(L+)
.

Appendix C
History of the Problem

We are aware of three prior attempts in the literature to show that Propositional Dynamic Logic (PDL) has the Craig Interpolation Property.
Here we summarize the history of the problem and how our proof relates to these attempts.

Two years after the introduction of PDL by Fischer and Ladner
[
19
]
, in 1981 Leivant defined a finitary sequent calculus for PDL to which he then applied Maehara’s method in order to obtain interpolation
[
35
]
.
However, that proof of interpolation is actually for CPDL, a constructive variant of PDL defined by Leivant, and not standard classical PDL.
To transfer interpolation from CPDL to PDL, Leivant claims that inserting double negations in front of every connective suffices to relate the two logics
[
35
, Proposition 3.2.2]
.
However, no proof details are given, and in particular it is not clear whether programs and test formulas also need to be modified in this translation.
It remains an open question whether and how this can actually be done.

Several years later, in 1988 Borzechowski (a co-author of the present work) wrote his Diplomarbeit (comparable to a master’s thesis) on PDL and interpolation under the supervision of Rautenberg at FU Berlin
[
8
]
.
The thesis was written in German and never published — our suspicion is that not many people in the research community actually read it.
A translation to English was made available in 2020 by Gattinger
[
7
]
, another co-author of the present work.
Like with Leivant, the main technique used by Borzechowski is Maehara’s method, this time applied to a tableau system for PDL itself instead of a sequent calculus for a constructive variant.
Borzechowski’s proof
[
8
]
provided the starting point for the present work.
We further discuss the changes and additions we made in the next subsection.

We continue the history of the problem another decade later.
In his 1999 book, Kracht also discusses “The Unanswered Question”
[
32
, Section 10.6]
.
He mentions the proof attempts by Leivant and Borzechowski, and says that “in neither case was it possible to verify the argument”
[
32
, p. 493]
.
Kracht does not further discuss the proof by Borzechowski, but says that Leivant “makes use of the fact that if
\varphi\vdash_{\textbf{PDL}}\psi
then we can bound the size of a possible countermodel so that the iterat
\alpha^{\ast}
only needs to search up to a depth
d
which depends on
\varphi
and
\psi
”
[
32
, p. 493]
.
Indeed, this is what makes the finitary
\ast
-rule used in
[
35
]
admissible.
Kracht then suggests that the argument by Leivant would require “that interpolation is preserved under intersection”
[
32
, p. 493]
, and Kracht rightly notes that this is not the case in general.
However, Leivant’s argument actually does not use an intersection of logics and does not need preservation of interpolation under intersection.
For more details, see
[
21
]
.
Overall, the criticism by Kracht does not apply to the proof by Leivant, but as mentioned above there are other gaps in it.

It is worth mentioning that Kracht shows that if test-free PDL has interpolation, then PDL has interpolation
[
32
, Theorem 10.6.2]
.
We do not use this result here but work with full PDL including tests.
To our knowledge it remains open whether test-free PDL has interpolation.

A third proof attempt was made in 2002, by Kowalski
[
29
]
.
This attempt was fundamentally different from the other two, as it used algebraic instead of proof-theoretic methods and claimed to show the superamalgamation property for free dynamic algebras.
The article was withdrawn in 2004
[
30
]
, after a mistake was found in the proof that could not be repaired.
Since then the question was considered open again, and for example highlighted by Van Benthem in
[
52
]
.

Appendix D
Differences from Borzechowski (1988)

The proof we have presented here still uses the three key ideas from Borzechowski’s
Diplomarbeit

[
8
]
, but we made several modifications to the tableau system and the ways in which we prove its properties.
We summarize the four main modifications below.

Avoid n-formulas by using
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
and
(\Box)
rules

Recall that our unfolding mechanism (see
Subsection 3.2
and
Subsection 3.1
) was specifically made to prevent
local repeats
, i.e., to never generate the same formula within a local tableau.
Local repeats arise due to the interaction of iteration
∗
and the test operator
{?}
, or due to the presence of nested iterations.
For example, formulas like
[{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
,
\lnot[{(q?)}^{\ast}]p
, and
\lnot[{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
all produce local repeats.

The mechanism that deals with local repeats in Borzechowski’s system
[
8
]
is called
n
-formulas.
Next, we present this mechanism and compare it to the rules
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
and
(\Box)
that replace it in our approach.

An
n
-formula is defined as a string which is obtained by replacing in a PDL formula one or more programs of the form
\alpha^{\ast}
by
\alpha^{(n)}
.
This replacement indicates that
\alpha^{\ast}
has been unfolded, hence if a formula
[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
is regenerated in a node label, then it is a local repeat.
Semantically, the interpretation of an
n
-formula is the same as the original PDL formula from which the
n
-formula was obtained.
Thus, a formula
[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
is semantically equivalent to the PDL formula
[\alpha^{\ast}]\varphi
.
It is worth noting that
(n)
is simply a symbol and
n
does not stand for a number.

Tableau nodes in this system are labeled with sets consisting of
n
-formulas and/or PDL formulas.
Furthermore, instead of having the rules
(\Box)
and
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
that deal with every possible program operator, Borzechowski’s system has the following rules that follow the well-known Segerberg axioms:

\Gamma,\lnot[\alpha\cup\beta]\varphi

(\lnot\cup)

\Gamma,\lnot[\alpha]\varphi\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Gamma,\lnot[\beta]\varphi

\Gamma,\lnot[\tau?]\varphi

(\lnot?)

\Gamma,\tau,\lnot\varphi

\Gamma,\lnot[\alpha;\beta]\varphi

(\lnot;)

\Gamma,\lnot[\alpha][\beta]\varphi

\Gamma,[\alpha\cup\beta]\varphi

(\cup)

\Gamma,[\alpha]\varphi,[\beta]\varphi

\Gamma,[\tau?]\varphi

(?)

\Gamma,\lnot\tau\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Gamma,\varphi

\Gamma,[\alpha;\beta]\varphi

(;)

\Gamma,[\alpha][\beta]\varphi

\Gamma,\lnot[\alpha^{\ast}]\varphi

(\lnot n)

\Gamma,\lnot\varphi\hskip 5.0pt\mid\hskip 5.0pt\Gamma,\lnot[\alpha][\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi

\Gamma,[\alpha^{\ast}]\varphi

(n)

\Gamma,\varphi,[\alpha][\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi

Figure 13.
Rules for boxes and negated boxes in Borzechowski’s system
[
8
]
.

In addition to these rules, a Local Tableau must also satisfy these conditions:

(1)

A node label
\Gamma,\lnot[a]\varphi
or
\Gamma,[a]\varphi
where
\varphi
is an
n
-formula is replaced with
\Gamma,\lnot[a]\psi
or
\Gamma,[a]\psi
, respectively, where
\psi
is obtained by replacing each
(n)
with
\ast
in
\varphi
.

(2)

A node label
\Gamma,[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
is replaced with
\Gamma
.

(3)

If it is possible, then a rule must be applied to an
n
-formula.

(4)

No rule may be applied if the node label contains a formula of the form
\lnot[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
.

Finally, it is important to mention that the definition of a closed tableau
[
8
, Definition 16]
entails that leaves that contain a formula of the form
\lnot[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
are considered closed.

The
n
-formulas, the rules in
Figure 13
, the conditions (1)–(4), and the treatment of leaves that contain a formula of the form
\lnot[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
as closed, constitute the four elements that handle the local repeats in Borzechowski’s system.

To see how this system handles particular examples of local repeats, consider the examples in
Figure 14
.
These examples show how the
n
-formulas and conditions (1)–(4) deal with local repeats by deleting the formulas of the form
[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
or stopping the system from applying any rules to nodes that contain a formula of the form
\lnot[\alpha^{(n)}]\varphi
.

In the left tableau, the crossed-out formula
\cancel{[{(a^{\ast})}^{(n)}]p}
is there to indicate that condition (2) has been applied to obtain that node, and thus the node only contains the formulas
p
and
[a^{\ast}][{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
.
Notice also that in the only leaf, condition (1) has been applied and thus the
n
-formula
[a][a^{(n)}][{(a^{\ast})}^{(n)}]p
has been replaced with its equivalent PDL formula
[a][a^{\ast}][{(a^{\ast})}^{\ast}]p
.

In the right tableau, the cross below the right leaf indicates that condition (4) has been applied, and thus that no more rules can be applied to that node, and that this node is considered closed.

p,[a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{(n)}]p
(n)

p,\cancel{[(a^{\ast})^{(n)}]p},[a][a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(n)

\lnot[(q?)^{\ast}]p
(\lnot n)
\lnot p
\lnot[q?][(q?)^{(n)}]p
q{,}\ \lnot[(q?)^{(n)}]p
(\lnot?)

Figure 14.
Local tableaux for
[{(a^{*})}^{*}]p
and
\lnot[{(q?)}^{\ast}]p
in the tableau system with n-formulas.

Although the
n
-formulas suffice to deal with local repeats, Borzechowski also had to deal with
n
-formulas as a recurring special case in many lemmas and theorems.
Furthermore, the presence of
n
-formulas also added an additional step in the construction of the interpolant.

In contrast, our tableau system does not use
n
-formulas, but instead encapsulates all the reasoning about the
n
-formulas from
[
8
]
including conditions (1)–(4) into the definition of the
\mathsf{unfold}
-functions that are used to define the rules

(\Box)
and
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
.
For example, compare the Local Tableaux of our system in
Figure 15
to those of
Figure 14
.

We explain the relationship between our
\mathsf{unfold}
-functions and the local tableaux of
[
8
]
in more detail.
For clarity, we refer to these as local MB tableaux.
For boxes, consider the maximal local MB tableau for
[\alpha]\psi
where
\psi
and all
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
are treated as atomic.
Branching in this tableau arises from applications of
(?)
.
Each
\Gamma\in\mathsf{unfold}_{\Box}(\alpha,\psi)
corresponds to some leaf of this local tableau.
Conversely, a leaf of this local tableau may be associated with multiple
\Gamma
. This can be seen by considering the maximal local MB tableau for
[p?;q?]r
. The leaf labeled with
\neg p
corresponds to both sequents

\{\neg p,\neg q\}
and
\{\neg p\}
in
\mathsf{unfold}_{\Box}(p?\mathop{;}q?,r)
. Informally, if the test
{p?}
fails then the outcome of
{q?}
does not matter.

For diamonds, consider the maximal local MB tableau for
\neg[\alpha]\psi
where
\psi
and all
\tau\in\mathit{Tests}(\alpha)
are treated as atomic.
Here, branching arises from applications of
(\neg\cup)
and
(\neg n)
.
The sequents in
\mathsf{unfold}_{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}}}(\alpha,\psi)
are the labels of the leaves in this local MB tableau that do not contain an
n
-formula
\lnot[\beta^{(n)}]\varphi
.

Through the use of the
\mathsf{unfold}
functions in rules
(\mathop{\scalebox{1}{$\Diamond$}})
and
(\Box)
, we have removed the need for
n
-formulas and the intermediate nodes that contain
n
-formulas.

[(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
p,[a][a^{\ast}][(a^{\ast})^{\ast}]p
(\Box)

\lnot[(q?)^{\ast}]p
\lnot p

[{(a^{*})}^{*}]p
and

using our system.

Completeness via games

Both the completeness proof given by Borzechowski
[
8
]
and the one presented here use the same standard approach:
given a consistent set
\Gamma
of formulas, we know that there is no closed tableau for
\Gamma
.
We can then use the existence of a maximal open tableau to build a model graph, i.e., a Kripke model where states correspond to sets of formulas that are true at them.
In the work of Borzechowski
[
8
]
this model is defined via a set
M_{0}
of maximal tableaux that contains first a maximal local tableau for
\Gamma
and then gets closed such that it “also contains every maximal saturating tableau”
[
8
, p. 36]
for any sequent occurring in a tableau already in the set.
It is not clear whether
M_{0}
is finite, nor whether it needs to be.
Then maximal local paths are used to define the states of the model graph.
The main work of the completeness proof is to show condition (d) of
Subsection 6.1
.
The argument for this is not entirely clear. In particular, the proof needs to “jump” between different tableaux in
M_{0}
but the termination of this process is not shown.

Instead of
M_{0}
, in our completeness proof here we obtain the model graph via the
tableau game
from
Subsection 6.2
.
The role of maximal local paths is played by pre-states and the main work in our completeness proof is to show that a winning strategy for the player “Builder” suffices to define a model graph.
This use of games is by now a standard tool in the theory of (fixpoint) logics (see e.g.
[
3
,
23
,
25
]
), and they provide an intuitive, yet formal, setting for reasoning about proof search.

Interpolant correctness via semantics

In Borzechowski’s Diplomarbeit
[
8
]
, the proof that the constructed interpolants are correct first extends the proof system with additional rules, argues for their admissibility and then builds tableaux in that new system to prove the correctness of the interpolants.
It should be noted this work does not include a complete proof of the admissibility of the additional rules, nor does it provide a convincing argument showing that the admissibility of these rules implies the completeness of the split system
[
8
, Lemma 13]
.
Furthermore, some definitions lack precision.
For instance, the definition of the structure obtained from a tableau by removing
n
-nodes
[
8
, Definition 26]
, and the definition of what we now call quasi-tableaux
[
8
, Definition 31]
, are both imprecise: the defined structures do not satisfy the conditions required to be considered tableaux, even though they are defined as such.
In addition, the proof of the correctness of the interpolant contains gaps.
The most significant one is in Lemma 25
[
8
, p. 40]
where some cases are handled incorrectly.

In this document, we establish the completeness of the split system directly instead of relying on the admissibility of additional rules. We also prove the correctness of interpolants directly via a semantic argument in
72
and
76
, rather than by using a proof-theoretic approach.

Simplified loading

In Borzechowski’s system
[
8
]
, the notation for loaded formulas looks like
[a][b]\varphi^{\varphi}
, but we write this as
\underline{[a][b]}\varphi
.
The purpose of this mechanism is the same and we still call it “loading”.
However, we make two changes to the loading rule
(L+)
.
First, our
(L+)
can only be applied to formulas that have an atomic program in their leading modality.
Second, our
(L+)
always loads maximally,
meaning that, e.g., the formula
[a][b^{*}][c\cup d](p\lor[a^{*}]q)
can only be loaded as

\underline{[a][b^{*}][c\cup d]}(p\lor[a^{*}]q)
.
These restrictions to
(L+)
are not necessary, but they simplify the tableau system overall.
